                  Goldfeld’s analytic density conjecture
                  and the 2-converse for elliptic curves
                                           OpenAI

                                    September 23, 2026


                                           Abstract
        We prove Goldfeld’s analytic density conjecture: for every elliptic curve E over
     Q, the quadratic twists of E with analytic rank zero and one each have density 1/2
     among signed squarefree twist parameters ordered by absolute value. We also prove
     the low-corank 2-converse: if the 2∞ -Selmer corank of E is zero or one, then it equals
     the analytic and Mordell–Weil ranks, and the Tate–Shafarevich group is finite.

                                               1
1     Introduction
Let E be an elliptic curve over Q. Write

    a(E) = ords=1 L(E, s),     r(E) = rankZ E(Q),        c2 (E) = corankZ2 Sel2∞ (E/Q).

The Selmer group uses the local Kummer conditions at every place. Its corank includes both
the Mordell–Weil rank and any divisible 2-primary contribution from the Tate–Shafarevich
group. Modularity supplies the analytic continuation and functional equation of L(E, s)
[7].
Theorem 1.1 (The 2-converse). For every elliptic curve E/Q with c2 (E) ∈ {0, 1},

                      a(E) = r(E) = c2 (E),       Sha(E/Q) is finite.

    There is no restriction on reduction type, complex multiplication, rational torsion, or
rational isogenies. The hypothesis concerns the full 2-power Selmer corank; a Mordell–Weil
rank bound alone does not give it.
    For a nonzero squarefree integer d, let E (d) denote the quadratic twist of E, and set

                       D(X) = {d ∈ Z : 0 < |d| ≤ X, d squarefree}.

Thus positive and negative parameters are counted together and ordered by absolute value.
A residue class can fix the sign of the functional equation; the half-density statement below
concerns the full set D(X).
Theorem 1.2 (Goldfeld’s analytic density conjecture). For every elliptic curve E/Q and
each j ∈ {0, 1},
                            #{d ∈ D(X) : a(E (d) ) = j}    1
                       lim                               = .
                      X→∞              #D(X)               2
Consequently the twists of analytic rank at least two have density zero. For a density-one
set of signed squarefree parameters d, the analytic and Mordell–Weil ranks of E (d) agree
and its whole Tate–Shafarevich group is finite.
    The theorem follows by combining the pointwise 2-converse with Smith’s distribution
of full 2-power Selmer coranks [69, Theorem 1.1]. Section 14 gives the deduction, including
the passage from integer to squarefree parameters. The main work of this paper is the
pointwise converse.

1.1    Consequences and companion results
The converse has a finite-descent consequence. Put

                        d2 (E) = dimF2 Sel2 (E/Q) − dimF2 E(Q)[2].

Corollary 1.3 (A finite Selmer criterion). If d2 (E) ∈ {0, 1}, then

      a(E) = r(E) = c2 (E) = d2 (E),       Sha(E/Q) is finite,     Sha(E/Q)[2∞ ] = 0.

    Thus a finite 2-descent can certify the analytic rank and the vanishing of the entire
2-primary Tate–Shafarevich group in these two cases. The proof uses the alternating Cassels–
Tate pairing to pass from d2 (E) to the full Selmer corank, and is given in Section 14.
    The density theorem resolves the frequency assertion for analytic ranks zero and one.
The mean analytic-rank assertion requires additional control: a set of density zero can still

                                              2
contribute to the rank-weighted average. The separate companion OpenAI [56, Theorems 1.1
and 1.2] proves the required analytic tail estimate and, together with Theorem 1.2, obtains
mean analytic rank 1/2 in the same signed squarefree ordering.
    A subsequent companion [55, Theorem 1.1] extends the pointwise converse to every
prime p: full p-power Selmer corank r ∈ {0, 1} forces analytic and Mordell–Weil ranks
r and finiteness of the whole Tate–Shafarevich group. Its proof uses the 2-converse and
analytic twist densities established here.
    The rank and finiteness assertions are distinct from the Birch–Swinnerton-Dyer leading-
coefficient formula. The pointwise two-primary formula of OpenAI [57, Theorem 1.1],
combined with the Selmer-corank densities in the proof of Theorem 1.2, gives the exact
two-primary formula on a density-one set of signed squarefree twists. Their common
analytic and Mordell–Weil ranks are zero and one with density 1/2 each. This consequence
is OpenAI [57, Corollary 16.1]; its leading-coefficient assertion concerns the valuation at 2.

1.2   Goldfeld’s conjecture and analytic nonvanishing
Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer’s comparisons of rational points with central L-values led to the
conjectural equality of algebraic and analytic rank [3]. The conjecture also predicts finiteness
of Sha(E/Q) and a formula for the leading coefficient [72, §1, pp. 415–421]. Goldfeld’s
original 1979 formulation asks for the mean analytic order in a family parametrized by
quadratic-field discriminants [30, p. 113, Conjecture (B)]. The density formulation predicts
that analytic ranks zero and one each occur half the time. Our counting convention
throughout is the signed squarefree set D(X).
    The analytic study of quadratic twists produced both nonzero central values and simple
central zeros. Bump–Friedberg–Hoffstein and Murty–Murty supplied auxiliary twists
with the nonvanishing needed in Heegner-point arguments; Iwaniec and Perelli–Pomykała
developed quantitative estimates for central derivatives [8, 35, 51, 59]. Ono–Skinner
obtained a lower bound of order X/ log X for the number of nonvanishing central values
among twists by fundamental discriminants D with 0 < |D| ≤ X [54, Corollary 3]. More
recently, Kumar–Mallesham–Sharma–Singh sharpened derivative nonvanishing estimates
through second moments, and Kriz–Nordentoft improved central-value lower bounds for
curves with no rational two-torsion [44, Theorem 1] [43, Theorem 1.1 and Remark 1.2].
These quantitative results yield many twists without by themselves giving a positive
proportion for every fixed curve.
    Positive proportions were obtained in special families. James proved analytic-rank-zero
proportions for particular curves, and Vatsal proved positive proportions of both analytic
ranks zero and one for X0 (19) [36, Theorem 1] [75, Theorem II]. Kriz–Li proved both
positive proportions for every rational elliptic curve admitting a rational 3-isogeny [42,
Theorem 1.5]. In the congruent-number family, Tian’s Heegner-point work gives rank one
and odd-order Tate–Shafarevich group in specified subfamilies [73]. Genus-period and
genus-point congruences, followed by Selmer statistics, give further analytic density results
[67, 74]. These arguments connect coefficient congruences and arithmetic statistics with
analytic nonvanishing, a connection also central to the present proof.
    The statistical input used here has its own progression. Smith first established higher
2-power Selmer distributions for curves with full rational two-torsion and no rational
cyclic subgroup of order four [68]. His subsequent work treated the irreducible residual
case and further rational two-isogeny cases [70, 71]. The unrestricted theorem gives full
Selmer coranks zero and one with density 1/2 each for every rational elliptic curve [69,
Theorem 1.1]. Its analytic corollary assumes Birch–Swinnerton-Dyer [69, Corollary 1.2].
We use the Selmer theorem itself and supply the pointwise analytic implication through

                                               3
Theorem 1.1.

1.3   The Selmer converse problem
Gross–Zagier and Kolyvagin established the forward implication: analytic rank at most
one gives equality with the Mordell–Weil rank and finiteness of the whole Tate–Shafarevich
group [31, 40]; see also the combined formulation in Skinner [65, §1]. The Gross–Zagier
formula relates a central derivative to the height of a Heegner point, and Kolyvagin’s
derived classes control the Selmer group. A converse must produce analytic nonvanishing
from Selmer information.
     Cyclotomic Iwasawa theory gave rank-zero converses at good ordinary odd primes
in the work of Skinner–Urban, under residual irreducibility and ramification hypotheses
[66]. Skinner and Wei Zhang proved rank-one converse criteria through Heegner points
and derived classes, under their respective reduction, residual-image and local hypotheses
[65, 82]. Burungale–Castella–Skinner subsequently removed the auxiliary ramification
conditions in the good ordinary, residually irreducible case for p > 3 [13, Theorem 1.2.2 and
the discussion following Remark 1.2.3]. At good supersingular primes p > 3, Castella–Wan
obtained the rank-one Selmer-corank implication for semistable curves [17, Theorem A].
     Reducible residual representations call for a different Iwasawa-theoretic comparison.
Castella–Grossi–Lee–Skinner proved the corank-zero and corank-one converses at good
ordinary Eisenstein primes p > 2, with local isogeny character neither trivial nor cyclotomic
[18, Theorem E]. Keller–Yin treated potentially good ordinary reduction at odd Eisenstein
primes without that character exclusion [39, Theorem B]. These results enlarge the allowed
residual representations while retaining conditions on the prime and its reduction type.
     The CM theory includes substantial results at the prime two. Burungale–Tian proved
the rank-one converse at good ordinary primes p > 3 [11]; Burungale–Castella–Skinner–
Tian included the small ordinary primes under an additional conductor hypothesis [10,
Theorem A]. Ressler and Yu obtained the small-prime good-ordinary result without that
conductor condition; see Yu’s Theorem 1 and the attribution in Burungale–Castella–
Skinner–Tian, Remark C [10, 81]. Burungale–Skinner’s combined ordinary formulation
includes p = 2, and their good supersingular CM theorem also permits p = 2 under an
additional surjectivity condition on Selmer localization at p [9, Theorems A.3 and A.1].
     In corank zero, Burungale–Tian’s theorem covers every rational CM elliptic curve at
every prime, without a reduction restriction [12, Theorem 1.1]. Combined with Smith’s
unrestricted distribution, it therefore already gives the analytic-rank-zero half for every CM
twist family. Their Theorem 1.2 gives rank zero for almost all positive squarefree n ≡ 1, 2, 3
(mod 8) in the congruent-number family ny 2 = x3 − x. Kriz’s preprint states a converse
for curves with CM by the full ring of integers at the unique ramified prime of the CM
field, and derives analytic densities for restricted CM families, including congruent-number
curves [41, Theorems√ 1.1 and 10.17]. Its dyadic pointwise statement concerns the CM
fields Q(i) and Q( −2). Together with Smith’s distribution theorem, the stated dyadic
converse would yield both half-density conclusions for quadratic twists of every curve with
CM by either of these maximal orders. Theorem 1.1 treats the full 2-power Selmer corank
for every rational elliptic curve, in both ranks zero and one.

1.4   Proof strategy
Fix the curve under consideration. We construct finite binary families of twists

                                (q ∗ )λq (x) ,   q ∗ = (−1/q)q,
                            Y
                     hx =                                         x ∈ Fb2 ,
                            q∈Q


                                                 4
where Q is a finite set of distinct good odd primes and the λq are linear forms. The
parameter at x = 0 is 1. The families have the same local twisting classes at every fixed
bad place. Their nonzero vertices have analytic order zero or one, according to the corank
of the fixed curve. A scalar determinant coordinate attached to the relevant class and
Selmer complex has a nonzero central specialization there, with uniformly bounded 2-adic
valuation. Bounded integral interpolation then recovers nonvanishing at the missing vertex
0.
    At corank zero, the interpolated class is a Beilinson–Kato class and its central spe-
cialization detects L(E,√1) [38]. At corank one, it is a Heegner class over an imaginary
quadratic field K = Q( k), whose height detects
                                   h                     i′
                                       L(E, s)L(E (k) , s)         .
                                                             s=1

An analytically nonvanishing companion E (k) makes the Selmer corank over K equal to one.
The argument retains integral Kummer conditions at 2 and every bad prime. The rational
Iwasawa input is used in its stated scope; the uniform integral estimates at 2 are proved in
Sections 13 and 12. The Heegner construction is related to Howard’s Kolyvagin-system
framework [34]; the integral bounds required here are established with the local conditions
and uniformity stated in this paper.
    The finite families are built from modular coefficients. Waldspurger’s formula supplies
the even-sign coefficients [77]. In odd sign, reductions of genus Heegner sums give weighted
ternary theta coefficients at increasing finite precisions. A uniform lower bound allows a
least normalized valuation to be selected. In the even case this is a minimum among the
coefficients of one form. In the odd case it is a minimum over bounded-weight sequences
as the precision increases; each finite collection of tests is then realized at a sufficiently
high actual precision. A test attaining the minimum detects the required analytic order
and gives a uniform upper bound. Weight-two Hecke traces turn these coefficient tests into
contraction and deletion rules for finite configurations of primes.
    There are two exhaustive residual cases. If E[2] is reducible, its invariant line is a
rational point of order two. Graphs encode prescribed quadratic residue symbols between
auxiliary primes. The resulting finite construction makes two separate coefficient tests
nonzero together at every nonzero binary vertex. The odd argument fixes one companion
field before the dimension grows. In this branch, the even construction first supplies the
lower bound needed to normalize the odd coefficient test. If E[2] is irreducible, local Selmer
equations give matrices over F2 . Subtracting a correction determined by finitely many local
types makes these matrices symmetric; their nullities still give a lower bound on the Selmer
dimension up to a fixed cost. Removing one selected prime or pair of primes, or retaining
it with either of two allowed local choices, gives three configurations. Their coefficient tests
satisfy the same sum-zero relation as the corresponding matrix determinants. This relation
does not identify the coefficient test with the determinant. We group the configurations
into blocks and arrange that the matrix entries between distinct blocks vanish. A nonzero
coefficient test bounds the matrix kernel, and hence the number of singular blocks in the
finite configuration. Fixing a configuration with a maximal number of such blocks lets us
arrange nonzero tests at every nonzero binary vertex. An additional alternating-matrix
construction supplies a companion with uniformly bounded prime count and Selmer length.
    Two parts of the argument have uses beyond this assembly. The interpolation algebra
clears denominators through a bounded complex, so the required congruence precision is
independent of the number of new primes. The graph construction treats the coefficient tests
as polynomials in prescribed residue symbols and makes separately nonzero tests nonzero


                                                  5
together. Its proof retains squared variables until after multiplying their highest-degree
parts. Neither construction presupposes the desired nonvanishing at the base.

Organization. Section 2 records the rank implications and local conventions. Section 3
states the two arithmetic transfer criteria, the lower bounds needed to normalize coefficients,
and auxiliary nonvanishing with prescribed local conditions. Sections 4 and 5 turn analytic
nonvanishing into coefficient tests and prime-substitution identities. Section 6 develops the
graph construction used in the rational-torsion converse of Section 7. Section 8 supplies the
Selmer matrices for the irreducible converse in Section 9. The remaining main-text sections
prove the arithmetic inputs: Section 10 establishes auxiliary nonvanishing, Section 11
develops the common finite-complex and binary transfer arguments, and Sections 12
and 13 construct the cyclotomic and Heegner determinant coordinates and prove their
integral bounds. Finally, Section 14 assembles the unrestricted result and its density and
finite-descent consequences.


2    Selmer ranks and local conventions
We first record the arithmetic implications used to pass between curves and to interpret
the final analytic nonvanishing. They apply to every elliptic curve over Q.
   Throughout, we use Mordell–Weil finite generation and the positive definiteness of the
canonical height modulo torsion over number fields [49, Chapter IV, finite-basis theorem,
Theorem 4.7, Remark 4.12, and Theorem 6.1].

Lemma 2.1 (Ranks and the forward implication). For an elliptic curve A/Q there is an
exact sequence

                0 −→ A(Q) ⊗ Q2 /Z2 −→ Sel2∞ (A/Q) −→ Sha(A/Q)[2∞ ] −→ 0.

In particular
                          c2 (A) = r(A) + corankZ2 Sha(A/Q)[2∞ ].
If a(A) ≤ 1, then r(A) = a(A), the whole group Sha(A/Q) is finite, and c2 (A) = a(A).

Proof. The finite Kummer exact sequences are

                0 −→ A(Q)/2n A(Q) −→ Sel2n (A/Q) −→ Sha(A/Q)[2n ] −→ 0.

Their direct limit gives the displayed sequence; taking coranks gives the identity. The last
assertion is the Gross–Zagier–Kolyvagin forward theorem [31, 40]; see also its unrestricted
formulation in Skinner [65, §1].

Lemma 2.2 (Parity and change of curve). For every elliptic curve A/Q with root number
ε(A),
                                    (−1)c2 (A) = ε(A).
                                                                                √
A rational isogeny preserves a(A), r(A) and c2 (A). For a quadratic field K = Q( k),

                         corankZ2 Sel2∞ (A/K) = c2 (A) + c2 (A(k) ).

Proof. The parity statement is the p = 2 case of Dokchitser and Dokchitser [25, Theorem 1.4,
equivalently Theorem 4.19]. At p = 2 this is Monsky’s theorem [50]. Isogenies and
their duals induce maps respecting every local Kummer condition; their composites are


                                              6
multiplication by their fixed degree. After rationalization these maps preserve ranks and
coranks. Their L-functions agree by isogeny invariance.
    Restriction to K and the two conjugation projections 1 + c and 1 − c give maps between
Sel2∞ (A/K) and the sum of the Selmer groups of A and A(k) over Q, with composites
multiplication by 2. They respect local Kummer images, so rationalization gives the corank
identity. An integral direct sum is not needed.

Lemma 2.3 (The residual alternatives). The representation E[2] is reducible over F2 if
and only if E(Q)[2] ̸= 0. In that case it is an extension of two trivial F2 -representations.
Otherwise its image is C3 or S3 in GL2 (F2 ). Quadratic twisting leaves this representation
unchanged.

Proof. An invariant line over F2 has a unique nonzero vector, which must be fixed. Con-
versely a rational point of order two spans an invariant line. Both that line and the
one-dimensional quotient are trivial, since F× 2 = {1}. The irreducible subgroups of
GL2 (F2 ) ≃ S3 are C3 and S3 . Finally a quadratic character reduces to 1 modulo 2.

    The distinction here is irreducibility over F2 , rather than absolute irreducibility: the
cyclic cubic case is included. In the rational-torsion case we may first replace the curve by
a rationally isogenous one with full rational two-torsion, if such a curve exists. Otherwise,
for any chosen rational two-isogeny E → E ′ , both Q(E[2]) and Q(E ′ [2]) are quadratic.
Lemma 2.2 justifies this choice before any constructions.

2.1   Finite lengths and twisting data
All valuations above 2 are normalized by v(2) = 1, with v(0) = +∞. For a finite Z2 -module
M , its length is log2 #M . The corank of a cofinitely generated discrete Z2 -module is the
rank of its Pontryagin dual. Its quotient by the maximal divisible subgroup is finite. This
finite quotient must be distinguished from finiteness of the original group.
     A fixed support S is a finite set of primes containing 2 and all primes of bad reduction;
it is enlarged explicitly when necessary. Write N for the conductor of E. A local filter
specifies a real sign and unit squareclasses at S for a variable odd fundamental discriminant
prime to S. The empty discriminant h = 1 denotes the untwisted curve. We also write E (h)
for twisting by a rational squareclass. For an odd prime p, the signed prime discriminant is
p∗ = (−1/p)p. Products of distinct p∗ are odd fundamental discriminants, with the empty
product interpreted as 1.
     For an odd fundamental discriminant h prime to 2N , the coprime-twist root-number
formula is
                                    ε(E (h) ) = ε(E)χh (−N )
[61, Section 1]. Thus matching unit squareclasses at the primes dividing 2N and opposite
real signs give opposite functional signs: the values of χh (N ) agree, whereas χh (−1) =
sgn(h). This verifies the sign condition when choosing the auxiliary partners below.
    Put T = T2 (E) and V = E[2]. Frobenius is arithmetic, and ap (E) = p + 1 − #E(Fp )
at a good prime. For h prime to S, set
                                                             1X
                           tp = dimF2 V Frp ,       w(h) =           tp .                (2.1)
                                                             2 p||h|

The residual identity, a transposition and a rotation of order three have tp = 2, 1, 0,
respectively. We call these split, simple and root primes; their weights are 1, 1/2, 0. Only


                                                7
types present in the residual image are used. Quadratic residue symbols written additively
take the values 0, 1 ∈ F2 , corresponding to +1, −1.
   Whenever its group is finite, write

                            s(h) = lengthZ2 Sha(E (h) /Q)[2∞ ].

This includes every twist already proved to have analytic rank zero or one. It also includes
a base of Selmer corank zero, since its full 2-primary Selmer group is then finite. We
make no whole-Sha finiteness assertion at such a base until analytic nonvanishing has been
established.

    Let Ω+E be a positive real period and let ΩE be the positive magnitude of a nonzero
anti-invariant period. Put
                                            |h| L(E (h) , 1)
                                         p
                                  L(h) =        sgn(h)
                                                             .                          (2.2)
                                              ΩE
Valuations refer to this algebraic period-normalized value at a chosen place above 2.
Changes of the fixed period, modular parametrization or isogeny affect valuations by
bounded constants. All parametrizations send the cusp ∞ to the origin. Replacing
Equation (2.2) by the central value divided by a Néron real period of E (h) changes a finite
valuation by OE (1): the differential changes by the usual twisting factor, and there are
only finitely many twisting classes at 2.
    Every uniform bound below specifies its fixed data. Constants may depend on the curve,
a chosen fixed twist and finite local preparation; they may not depend on the subsequent
cube dimension or the number of new primes. At finite precision, the sizes of primes
realizing a specified configuration may depend on that entire configuration. A limiting
construction will only be used through its finite realizations.
Lemma 2.4 (Finite descent and corank). Let F be the quotient of Sha(E/Q)[2∞ ] by its
maximal divisible subgroup. Then

                   d2 (E) = c2 (E) + dimF2 F [2],    dimF2 F [2] is even.

Thus d2 (E) ∈ {0, 1} implies c2 (E) = d2 (E) and F = 0.
Proof. The finite Kummer sequence at n = 1 gives d2 (E) = r(E) + dimF2 Sha(E/Q)[2].
The divisible subgroup is a direct sum of copies of Q2 /Z2 and splits as an abstract abelian
subgroup. Its 2-torsion dimension is its corank. Lemma 2.1 therefore gives the displayed
identity. The Cassels–Tate pairing on F is perfect. For an elliptic curve, the rational
divisor [O] defining its principal polarization makes the pairing alternating [46, Chapter I,
Remark 6.10(b) and Theorem 6.13(a)]. A finite abelian 2-group with such a pairing is an
orthogonal sum of pairs of cyclic groups of equal order: choose an element of maximal
order, pair it with an element detecting that order, split off their nondegenerate plane, and
continue. Hence its 2-rank is even. A finite 2-group with zero 2-rank is zero, proving the
last assertion.


3    Arithmetic estimates for finite twist families
The pointwise converse will follow by placing the given curve at the zero vertex of a large
finite family of twists. We arrange analytic nonvanishing at every other vertex, together
with an upper bound on a normalized valuation. The estimates in this section explain
which normalization is needed and what must be uniform as the family grows. Their proofs
occupy Sections 11, 12 and 13.

                                              8
    Fix E/Q of conductor N and retain the local weights w(h), finite Sha lengths s(h), and
period-normalized values L(h) of Section 2. In particular s(h) is defined at a corank-zero
base and at every twist already known to have analytic rank zero or one. All valuations
satisfy v(2) = 1.
    A binary family has the form

                                 (q ∗ )λq (x) ,                      q ∗ = (−1/q)q,
                             Y
                   hx = h0                           x ∈ Fb2 ,
                             q∈Q

where the new primes are distinct and prime to 2N h0 , and each λq is linear. The old
factors of h0 remain at every vertex; no new factor is present at zero. We will hold the
required local squareclasses fixed while b grows. Both the set of primes and their sizes may
depend on b.
    Here is the common transfer mechanism. For families satisfying the hypotheses of the
transfer theorems below, the arithmetic proofs attach power series

                                         ux (t) ∈ Z2 [[t]][1/2]

to the vertices, using a common family of cochain models and compatible trivializations of
the determinant lines. The differences of these coordinates therefore have a fixed meaning.
Their constant terms detect the central value in rank zero or a Heegner point in rank one.
If the analytic nonvanishing sought at the base fails, then u0 (0) = 0. The nonzero vertices
instead satisfy v(ux (0)) ≤ B1 with B1 independent of b. The denominator analysis proves
that, for every fixed integer m and sufficiently large b, some x ̸= 0 satisfies
                                                            
                                      v ux (0) − u0 (0) ≥ m.

Taking m > B1 contradicts these two assertions. The work is to obtain this congruence
with a dimension threshold independent of the number of new primes. Section 11 does so
after the new local blocks have been eliminated from the Selmer complex: a clearing factor
for the remaining bounded complex makes finitely many coefficient congruences sufficient.
Only that clearing factor needs a uniform pole bound; the numerator may involve the
whole prime set.

3.1   Transfer of a central value
For a twist with nonzero central value, put

                              D(h) = v(L(h)) − 2w(h) − s(h).                           (3.1)

The subtraction records the local contributions of the ramified primes and the finite Selmer
contribution. In the cyclotomic proof, Kato’s class and the invariant line at the real place
form the determinant coordinate ux . Its central valuation differs from D(hx ) by a fixed
bounded amount. Thus an upper bound on D is exactly the arithmetic input to the
preceding contradiction.

Theorem 3.1 (Cyclotomic missing vertex). There is a constant CE , depending only on E,
with the following property. Fix an odd fundamental discriminant h0 , prime to 2N , with
c2 (E (h0 ) ) = 0. Suppose that for arbitrarily large b there is a family

                                    (q ∗ )λq (x) ,                    λq ∈ (Fb2 )∗ ,
                              Y
                    hx = h0                              x ∈ Fb2 ,                     (3.2)
                              q∈Q



                                                     9
where Q consists of distinct primes outside 2N h0 , q ∗ = (−1/q)q, and every hx has the
same sign and the same local squareclasses at 2N . Assume that a constant B, independent
of b, satisfies
                     L(E (hx ) , 1) ̸= 0,  D(hx ) ≤ B      (x ̸= 0).                (3.3)
If E[2] is irreducible, assume also that some L, independent of b, satisfies

                                    v2 (q 2 − 1) ≤ L           (q ∈ Q).                            (3.4)

Then L(E (h0 ) , 1) ̸= 0 and
                                             D(h0 ) ≤ B + CE .                                     (3.5)
The necessary dimension may depend on h0 , its finite Selmer group, and the bound in
Equation (3.4); CE does not.

   The final constant CE bounds the specialization error. The finite Selmer group of
the base and, in the irreducible case, the bound on v2 (q 2 − 1) affect how large a cube is
needed, but not this transferred error. We shall construct the nonzero vertices so that
v(L(hx )) ≤ 2w(hx ) + C; since s(hx ) ≥ 0, this supplies the hypothesis on D(hx ).

3.2    Transfer of a Heegner point
In corank one the coordinate comes from a Heegner point. The family must also carry
a companion twist whose central value is nonzero. We first specify the two Heegner
constructions whose
                  √ indices enter the estimates.
    Let K = Q( k), where k < 0 is an odd fundamental discriminant and every prime
dividing 2N splits in K. We omit the two fields with extra units and, if E has complex
multiplication, its CM field. Fix a nonzero modular parametrization X0 (N ) → E, translated
so that the cusp ∞ maps to the origin, and compatible orientations of the Heegner points
yc ∈ E(Hc ), where Hc is the ring class field of conductor c. The Manin–Drinfeld theorem
makes the images of cusp differences torsion [26, Section 1]. Since the modular curve
and parametrization are fixed, one integer kills all these images and removes cuspidal
ambiguities.
    If χe |GK is a primitive ring class character of conductor c, write Pc (e) for the character
sum of yc . Equivalently, twist over Hc and trace to E (e) (K). If this latter group has
rank one, j(Pc (e)) is the valuation of its index on the free rank-one lattice. Passing
between this lattice and the rank-one lattice of the contributing curve among E (e) (Q) and
E (ek) (Q) changes the index by a bounded amount: restriction and addition or subtraction
of conjugates have composites multiplication by 2.
    We use two choices of data:

             genus data:       k = hh∗ ,     (h, h∗ ) = 1,     e = h∗ ,   c = 1,       h∗ fixed;
               ring data:      (k, h) = 1,     e = h,       c = |h|.

The character in genus data is unramified. In ring data, restriction at each q | h has
conductor exponent one: this follows by pulling the quadratic character back under the
local norm. Thus these are the asserted primitive ring class conductors.

Proposition 3.2 (Comparison of the two Heegner constructions). For either set of data,
                                             q     h                        i′
                       b c (e)) = CE c |k| L(E (e) , s)L(E (ek) , s)
                       h(P                                                         ,               (3.6)
                                                                             s=1




                                                       10
with a fixed nonzero constant determined by the parametrization and height conventions.
In particular, a simple zero of the product gives total Mordell–Weil rank one and finite
Shafarevich–Tate groups.
    Suppose h has analytic rank one, and use genus data with a fixed partner h∗ of analytic
rank zero and ring data with a possibly varying k, where L(E (hk) , 1) ̸= 0. Then

                       2j(P|h| (h)) − 2j(P1 (h∗ )) = v(L(hk)) + OE,h∗ (1).                     (3.7)

Here the two points are formed in their respective quadratic fields. If instead h∗ has analytic
rank one and L(E (h) , 1) ̸= 0, genus data give

                            2j(P1 (h∗ )) = v(L(h)) + OE,h∗ (1).                      (3.8)
                                                         √
    The genus construction varies the quadratic field Q( hh∗ ) while holding h∗ fixed. It
is the construction detected by the odd coefficients below. Interpolation instead uses
the ring construction: within one binary family the field K is common to every vertex.
Equation (3.7) converts a genus-index estimate and an even central-value estimate into the
ring-index estimate needed for transfer. The field K may change when a new family is
chosen.
Theorem 3.3 (Heegner interpolation). Fix E and an initial discriminant h0 . Consider
families hx , x ∈ Fb2 , obtained from h0 by adjoining new prime factors with linear activation
functions on Fb2 . No old factor is removed; the signed factor belonging to an odd prime q is
q ∗ = (−1/q)q. Suppose the local twisting classes at 2N are constant. For each family let k
define an imaginary field satisfying the standing Heegner conditions, with (k, hx ) = 1 for
every x. Each new prime must either split in K or satisfy tq = 0.
    Assume that the following bounds are fixed independently of b:
   (i) a bound for ω(k);
  (ii) a bound for the length of the finite quotient of Sel2∞ (E (h0 ) /K) by its divisible subgroup;
 (iii) a constant B.
Assume this base Selmer group has corank one. Suppose, for every x ̸= 0, the product
L(E (hx ) , s)L(E (hx k) , s) has a simple zero and

                        2j(P|hx | (hx )) − 4w(hx ) − s(hx ) − s(hx k) ≤ B.                     (3.9)

Then, for sufficiently large b, the base product has a simple zero. The dimension threshold
depends only on the fixed base and the displayed bounds, not on the number or sizes of the
new primes. The field K may vary with the family.
    In these ring data, the relevant normalized quantity is

                              2j(P|h| (h)) − 4w(h) − s(h) − s(hk).

We use the theorem to prove nonvanishing at the base. In our applications, the genus-index
and even central-value estimates will give 2j(P|hx | (hx )) ≤ 4w(hx ) + C at every nonzero
vertex. Subtracting the nonnegative Sha lengths supplies the required upper bound.
    The finite-quotient condition allows two uses of the theorem. The rational-two-torsion
argument fixes K before increasing b. The irreducible argument lets K vary and verifies
the following criterion.
Lemma 3.4. The finite-quotient bound in Theorem 3.3 holds if K belongs to a fixed finite
collection. It also holds if E (h0 ) is fixed with Selmer corank one, the curves E (h0 k) have
analytic rank zero with bounded s(h0 k), and ω(k) is bounded.

                                                 11
3.3   Lower bounds and the coefficient constructions
The transfer criteria ask for upper bounds at a well-chosen set of twists. To find those
twists, we first need lower bounds on all the normalized coefficients under consideration.
In even sign a least normalized valuation can then be selected, and a coefficient attaining
it gives both analytic nonvanishing and an upper bound with a constant independent of
the prime support. In odd sign the coefficients come at increasing finite precision: the
minimum is defined through bounded-weight sequences, and each use is realized on a finite
collection of indices.
    For the even coefficient construction, Section 4 relates twice a coefficient valuation to
v(L(h)) up to a fixed constant. For the odd construction it relates the coefficient valuation
to the genus index. The following three lower estimates supply the normalizations, with
different roles in the two residual cases.
    When E[2] is reducible, the cyclotomic argument supplies the even lower estimate
directly. By Lemma 2.3, the representation is then an extension of two trivial modules.
Theorem 3.5 (Cyclotomic lower bound). Suppose that E[2] is an extension of two trivial
F2 -modules. There is a constant CE such that every odd fundamental discriminant h prime
to 2N with L(E (h) , 1) ̸= 0 satisfies

                              v(L(h)) ≥ 2w(h) + s(h) − CE .                           (3.10)

The constant is independent of the number and sizes of the primes that divide h.
    When E[2] is irreducible, the genus construction supplies the lower estimate for either
sign. With an analytic-rank-zero fixed partner, it bounds the odd genus index. With an
analytic-rank-one fixed partner, Equation (3.8) turns it into the even central-value bound.
Theorem 3.6 (Genus bound). In genus data, suppose the product in Equation (3.6) has a
simple zero and E[2] is irreducible. Then, uniformly in h,

                          2j(P1 (h∗ )) ≥ 2w(h) + s(h) − OE,h∗ (1).                    (3.11)

In proving the uniform estimate, one may omit the finitely many h for which K meets
Q(E[2]) nontrivially.
   The remaining case is the odd coefficient construction with rational two-torsion. Here
the genus estimate just stated is unavailable. We use instead the following ring estimate,
whose residual representation is unrestricted.
Theorem 3.7 (Ring bound). In ring data, suppose the product in Equation (3.6) has a
simple zero, every q | h splits in K, and ω(k) ≤ a, where ω counts distinct prime factors.
Then
                       2j(P|h| (h)) ≥ 4w(h) + s(h) + s(hk) − OE,a (1).              (3.12)
There is no irreducibility assumption on E[2].
    The passage from this ring estimate to an odd genus estimate is part of Proposition 7.7.
The even coefficient construction first provides, for each already rank-one twist h in
the positive filter, a companion parameter k ′ satisfying the ring-bound hypotheses: it is
negative, odd fundamental, coprime to 2N h, a local square at every prime of 2N h, and
defines a nonexceptional imaginary field. Its prime count is bounded, and the companion
              ′
obeys L(E (hk ) , 1) ̸= 0 and

                            v(L(hk ′ )) ≤ 2w(hk ′ ) + s(hk ′ ) + C.

                                              12
Substitute this upper bound into Equation (3.7) and the ring lower bound. Since w(hk ′ ) =
w(h) + w(k ′ ) and w(k ′ ) is bounded, the result is

                               2j(P1 (h∗ )) ≥ 2w(h) + s(h) − C ′ .

Only then is the odd coefficient system normalized in the reducible branch. Thus the
lower-bound dependencies are

      irreducible genus bound           −→ even and odd coefficient bounds,
    reducible cyclotomic bound          −→ even coefficients, then bounded companions,
 ring bound and those companions        −→ reducible odd coefficient bound.

The reducible odd normalization is therefore available after the even companion construction.
The all-split hypothesis belongs to the ring lower bound used here. The Heegner transfer
criterion retains its stated alternative for new primes with tq = 0.

3.4   Auxiliary twists in prescribed local classes
The coefficient constructions also require an auxiliary twist with specified local classes. For
even sign the number of its prime factors must remain bounded as the local conditions
vary. The following nonvanishing result supplies both kinds of partner; its proof is given in
Section 10.

Proposition 3.8 (Auxiliary nonvanishing). Let E/Q have conductor N , and let S be a
finite set of primes containing the prime divisors of 2N . Prescribe a real sign σ ∈ {1, −1}
and a unit squareclass cp ∈ Z×    ×2
                              p /Zp for each p ∈ S. Suppose that these data are realized by
an odd fundamental discriminant, and that twists in this filter have root number ε. For
every finite set B of primes there are odd fundamental discriminants D of arbitrarily large
absolute value such that

                sign(D) = σ,        [D]p = cp    (p ∈ S),     p∤D    (p ∈ B),

Moreover:
  (i) If ε = 1, they can be chosen with L(E (D) , 1) ̸= 0 and ω(|D|) ≤ 5.
 (ii) If ε = −1, they can be chosen with L′ (E (D) , 1) ̸= 0, so the central zero is simple.
The bound five in (i) is independent of E, S, B and the prescribed classes. In either case,
every prime factor of D can be required to exceed any previously fixed bound.


4     Coefficients that detect analytic rank
The interpolation theorems require nonvanishing with a uniform valuation bound at the
nonzero vertices of a cube. We encode both requirements in one binary coefficient: it is
one when a modular coefficient has the least normalized valuation. Waldspurger’s formula
supplies the even-sign coefficients; reduction of genus Heegner sums supplies the odd-sign
coefficients at finite precision. We prove their modular and arithmetic properties separately
before choosing this normalization.

4.1   Filters and prime weights
Fix E/Q and a finite set S containing the primes of 2N and the ramification set of
F = Q(E[2]). Additional fixed primes may be included. A filter consists of a sign

                                                13
σ ∈ {1, −1} and unit squareclasses at the primes in S, imposed on positive squarefree
integers D prime to S, so that
                                       h = σD
is an odd fundamental discriminant and the functional sign of E (h) is constant. Multiplica-
tion by squares of units preserves the local conditions. We impose these conditions on all
Fourier indices, not only on squarefree ones. Projections onto the required unit square-
classes are finite combinations of quadratic coefficient twists and coprimality projections;
they enlarge the level only at S.
    For p ∈
          / S, the action on V = E[2] has the following three types:

                              type         Frp |V         weight
                              split          1              1
                             simple an element of order 2  1/2
                              root an element of order 3    0.

Thus w(D) = 12 p|D dimF2 V Frp = w(h). The fractional part of w(D) is constant on a
                 P

filter: it is determined by the quadratic discriminant character of the residual representation.
For an arbitrary squarefree set of primes we use the same additive weight. Signs are encoded
by p∗ = (−1/p)p, so that h = p|D p∗ .
                                 Q

     We use the ordinary theta multiplier cubed in weight 3/2. A finite idele unit character
and its Dirichlet character are related by the reciprocal convention: if a finite Schwartz
function obeys f (ux) = α(u)f (x), its scalar classical character on the lower-right entry is
associated to α−1 . All valuations satisfy v(2) = 1.

4.2    The even coefficient system

Proof. We first choose the packet member and its local characters. We then establish
the rationality up to phase needed for valuation comparisons, apply the coefficient-square
formula, and deduce the uniform lower bound from the interpolation estimates.
    Use the Shimura packet attached to the integral-weight newform of E (−σ) . In the
classical convention its positive Fourier index D tests the twist by χ−D , hence the curve
E (σD) . The packet and Fourier coefficient results of Waldspurger [76, 77] apply to the
cuspidal packet orthogonal to unary theta series. Their local uniqueness and global
multiplicity assertions are summarized in [78, Sections 3.1 and 5.1]. A nonzero central
value in the filter exists by Proposition 3.8. It selects a globally occurring packet member
with nonzero Fourier functional at that index. At good odd primes choose spherical vectors;
at infinity choose the holomorphic vector of weight 3/2.
    We explain the choices at a prime of S. Let λD be a nonzero local Fourier functional and
start with a vector on which it is nonzero. Average upper-unipotent translates against the
inverse additive character of frequency D. If the averaging compact subgroup is sufficiently
large, its translate by a unit scale u can contribute only when u2 is sufficiently close to 1.
The surviving scales are consequently small neighborhoods of 1 and −1. Local constancy
then permits projection onto a unit character which matches the forced action of −1,
without killing the Fourier value: the contributions of the two neighborhoods add. Such a
character may be chosen to have 2-power order. Indeed only its parity is prescribed, and
the 2-primary quotient of the unit group realizes either possible parity. Upper averaging at
integral frequency gives period one, and lower-unipotent invariance holds at sufficiently
large depth. These vectors therefore give a classical form at a level supported on S. The
parity conditions combine correctly by automorphy. The subsequent squareclass projections
preserve its character. Write that character as ψ.
    In the theta-multiplier convention the good eigenvalues of the packet are ψ(p) times
those of E (−σ) . Set η = ψχ−σ . Then ψ/η is quadratic and the weight-3/2 Hecke formula
gives Equation (4.1).
    We need more than algebraicity of the coefficients. After normalization at one squarefree
index, their nonzero squarefree valuations must be integers, so that a strict valuation
inequality gains a full factor of 2, even when the character field is ramified. We prove
this by showing that every such coefficient is rational up to a phase of 2-power order. At
the fixed level, the weight-3/2 cusp space with character ψ has a basis over the field of
character values, and coefficient conjugation changes ψ to its conjugate [64, Section 2,
Lemma 4(3)–(4), pp. 658–659]. The good Hecke equations and the vanishing of coefficients
off the filter are linear equations over Q(ψ, η). In a finite-dimensional space, finitely many
of these equations cut out their full common kernel. The constructed complex vector has
a nonzero coefficient at a squarefree index D0 of the filter, so this coefficient functional
is nonzero on some algebraic vector in that kernel. Choose such a vector. It remains
orthogonal to unary theta series: at good primes their eigenvalues have absolute value
                                                                               √
p + 1, whereas the prescribed eigenvalues have absolute value at most 2 p by Hasse’s
bound [60, Section 2, Lemma 2.1, and Corollary 5.2].
    We use Waldspurger [77, Corollary 2, pp. 483–484] to compare Fourier coefficients. Its
hypothesis (H1) holds for every nonzero classical eigenspace used here, by Proposition 2 of

                                             15
that paper. The final paragraph of the corollary’s proof removes (H2) when the indices lie
in the same local squareclass at every level prime. Our filters retain this condition at 2 as
well as at the odd primes.
    Let u be an automorphism of Q acting by an odd power e on the 2-power characters,
and put ν = η (e−1)/2 . The coefficient conjugate g u and coefficient twist g ⊗ ν have exactly
the same character and good Hecke system, since

                             ψν 2 = ψη e−1 = ψ e ,           ην 2 = η e .

They may be compared at a common level supported on S, in the same good Hecke
eigenspace W , and both have a nonzero D0 -coefficient. Let ℓn : W → C denote the nth
Fourier-coefficient functional. Waldspurger’s corollary applies to every f ∈ W and gives

                                     ℓD (f )2 = CD ℓD0 (f )2 ,

where CD is independent of f . If CD = 0, then ℓD = 0. Otherwise choose cD ∈ C with
c2D = CD and factor the identity as

                              (ℓD − cD ℓD0 )(ℓD + cD ℓD0 ) = 0.

The polynomial ring on W is an integral domain, so one factor vanishes identically. Thus
the two coefficient functionals are proportional on the whole space, including oldvectors
and linear combinations. In particular the two forms have the same ratio of their D- and
D0 -coefficients.
    It follows, for ξ = a(D)/a(D0 ), that

                                       u(ξ) = ν(D/D0 )ξ.

The local squareclass conditions imply η(D/D0 ) = z 2 for a value z in the group of values
of η. Consequently u(ξ/z) = ξ/z for every u, so ξ/z ∈ Q. This proves the phase assertion.
    With our characters and period normalization, its identity of coefficient squares is
                                           2
                                  a(D)                       L(σD)
                              
                                                = ψ(D/D0 )           .                   (4.5)
                                  a(D0 )                     L(σD0 )

Here ψ(D/D0 ) = ψ(D)ψ(D0 )−1 has valuation zero; the factor D/D0 in the classical
                                                                            p

central-value comparison is incorporated in L. The local unit squareclasses at S agree; at
a good prime the spherical squarefree factor is one at both valuations zero and one [77,
Section I.3, pp. 377–378]. There is consequently no extra product over the prime factors
of D. Taking valuations gives Equation (4.2), hence Equation (4.3). The same identity,
compared with the nonzero coefficient at D0 , gives the asserted equivalence of vanishing.
The corresponding rational-square statement for central values is Vignéras’s rationality
theorem, recalled in Waldspurger [78, Section 6.3, Theorem 6].
    If E[2] is an extension of trivial modules, combine Equation (4.3) with Theorem 3.5. If
E[2] is irreducible, choose a fixed odd fundamental discriminant h∗ of opposite infinite sign,
of analytic rank one, whose local characters agree with those of h at 2N . Proposition 3.8
supplies such a choice. Exclude its prime factors from the filter and choose its support
to avoid the finitely many exceptional genus fields. The reverse genus comparison in
Proposition 3.2 and Theorem 3.6 give the same lower bound. These are fixed exclusions
and constants, proving Equation (4.4) in both cases.



                                                   16
4.3   Odd coefficients from reduction of Heegner traces
The odd construction combines reduction of Heegner points with the ternary quadratic
forms attached to definite quaternion orders. We use Deuring’s reduction correspondence
in the oriented form of Cornut–Jetchev, together with the Eichler-order and ternary-form
description of Jetchev–Kane [21, 24, 37]. The weights below retain the genus character
and the local orientations, so that the resulting Fourier coefficients recover the reduced
genus sum at finite precision.
    Fix now a nonempty filter of odd functional sign. Choose an odd fundamental h∗ of
opposite infinite sign, with L(E (h∗ ) , 1) ̸= 0, whose local characters agree with those of h
at 2N . Exclude its primes. We may choose |h∗ | > 3 and include in its support a prime
outside the exceptional imaginary quadratic discriminants. Thus all genus fields below
have only the units {1, −1} and avoid the CM field of E. Write
                                                   √
                     k = hh∗ < 0,         KD = Q( k),        PD = P1 (h∗ ).
The point PD is the character sum of the conductor-one Heegner point for KD . We fix the
modular parametrization φ with φ(∞) = 0, so the constants in its Hecke and Atkin–Lehner
relations are torsion. At a simple zero of E (h) , its free line is that of E (h) , up to the
fixed-degree identifications in Proposition 3.2. Below, the product derivative means the
derivative at s = 1 of L(E (h) , s)L(E (h∗ ) , s).
Proposition 4.2 (Odd coefficients under a genus-index bound). Assume that a constant
C∗ satisfies
                            j(PD ) ≥ w(D) + 12 s(h) − C∗                        (4.6)
for every h in the filter with a simple zero. There are arbitrarily large precisions M , good
odd primes ℓ outside the fixed support, and holomorphic weight-3/2 forms gM = aM (n)q n ,

  (i) The additional filter at ℓ is
                   (h∗ /ℓ) = −σ,       (h/ℓ) = σ,     ρT (Frℓ ) ≡ C   (mod 2M ),         (4.7)

       quadratic ratio. Their squarefree coefficients have the form of a 2-power phase times

 (iii) If a coefficient is visible, meaning v(aM (D)) < M , then E (h) has a simple zero and

      when the product derivative is nonzero; if it is zero, the coefficient is zero modulo

For irreducible E[2], the hypothesis is Theorem 3.6. For rational two-torsion, the positive
filters used in the pointwise argument have this bound by Proposition 7.7, using only the
even source. An arbitrary twist is first absorbed into the fixed curve, so only these positive
filters are needed there.

                                              17
Proof. The proof has three stages. A reduction map detects one fixed nonzero genus point
at arbitrarily high precisions. Weighted theta series then supply holomorphic forms with
the required Hecke recurrence. Finally, an exact comparison with genus sums transfers
point divisibility and the assumed lower bound to their squarefree coefficients.

The reduction map and a detecting sequence. Choose a nonzero integral row b
with bC = σb. Under Equation (4.7), finite-field Kummer theory gives

           κM : E(Fℓ2 ) −→ H 1 (Fℓ2 , E[2M ]) = E[2M ]/(Fr2ℓ −1)E[2M ] = E[2M ].

The last identification is evaluation at arithmetic Frobenius squared. Put λM = bκM . This
map has Frobenius sign σ.
    Choose hw = σDw in the original filter with a simple zero, using Proposition 3.8. A
fixed multiple of its genus point is a nonzero point on the rational curve E (hw ) . Its division
cocycle has open translation image on the kernel of the Tate action, even after fixed
finite auxiliary fields have been adjoined. Here is the relevant argument. The translation
image is a Galois-stable Z2 -submodule of the Tate module. Its rational span is nonzero:
otherwise the point’s Kummer class would factor through the Tate image; that image has a
central nonidentity scalar, which kills its rational first cohomology, contradicting Kummer
injectivity. The rational Tate representation is irreducible by Serre [62, Chapter IV,
Section 2.1, pp. IV-9–IV-10], since EndQ (E) = Z. This includes curves with geometric
complex multiplication, whose nonreal endomorphisms are not defined over Q. The scalar
Lie-algebra assertion of Bogomolov [5, Section 2, Theorem 3, p. 62], followed by local
logarithm and exponential, supplies the required nonidentity scalar. The nonzero stable
span is therefore the whole Tate module. The translation image consequently contains a
full lattice. Passing to a finite-index subgroup preserves this fact.
    Take an element acting as C on T and as complex conjugation on the fixed quadratic
character fields, and modify it by such a translation. On the twisted module for hw its
action is σC; at its square the cocycle includes 1 + σC. Projection by b is twice the
corresponding nonzero row. We can therefore arrange nonzero detection with a fixed finite
valuation. Chebotarev, applied at successive finite precisions to this fixed compatible Galois
element and its division data, supplies arbitrarily large primes satisfying Equation (4.7),
also with h = hw , and the asserted bounded detection. A congruence on the target curve’s
Frobenius is all that is required. The supersingular objects used next are points of the
modular curve, not an additional reduction hypothesis on E.

Supersingular values and local weights. Let B be the quaternion algebra ramified
at ℓ and infinity, and let R be an Eichler order of level N . Supersingular pairs on X0 (N )
are parametrized by
                                       B × \B
                                            b × /R
                                                 b×.

They are defined over Fℓ2 . The reduction and Eichler order description apply to arbitrary N

prime to ℓ; see [37, Sections 1.1–1.2 and 2.5]. For a representative z write Rz = B ∩ z Rz
and let PM (z) be the reduced modular-parametrization value followed by λM . The cusp
differences have torsion image by Drinfeld [26, Section 1]. Multiply the parametrization once
by a fixed integer L killing the cuspidal constants in its Hecke and Atkin–Lehner relations
and the bounded-degree torsion occurring in genus sums. This choice is independent of
D, ℓ, M .

2M . Choose integer representatives of its values when making an ordinary characteristic-zero

We will identify its terms with optimal embeddings of OKD . Their local orientations give

                                                                      Q
    Define a finite compactly supported, locally constant weight f = fp as follows; a
value not satisfying a listed condition is zero.
 (a) Away from 2N h∗ ℓ, require order integrality and put fp = 1.
 (b) At 2, require (1+ x)/2 to be integral and x2 to be a split fundamental unit squareclass.
     At pe ∥N , require the split fundamental unit type and its order embedding. The cyclic
     Eichler level line selects one of the two eigenroots r of x; weight it by a 2-power unit
     character ηp (r) with
                                       ηp (−1) = wpe χh∗ (p)e .
     If p = 2, use the selected summand of (1 + x)/2, whose eigenvalues (1 + r)/2 and
     (1 − r)/2 differ by the odd unit r. Scaling by an odd u sends this operator to
     u(1 + x)/2 + (1 − u)/2 and preserves that summand; its selected root for ux is ur.
     Thus the weight changes by η2 (u), even when the two eigenvalue parities interchange.
     If 2 ∤ N , impose only the stated integrality restriction.
 (c) At p | h∗ require integral x with determinant of valuation one. Its reduction is
     nonzero nilpotent. On that orbit use the sign which transforms by (det u/p) under
     conjugation by u ∈ GL2 (Zp ). This sign exists because the stabilizer of a nonzero
     nilpotent has the form aI + bX and determinant a2 . Scaling x by a unit u changes
     this sign by (u/p).
 (d) At ℓ require integral unit norm and the unramified quadratic type. Choose a basis of
     the trace-zero line in Fℓ2 and weight the nonzero residue on that line by its Legendre
     symbol relative to the basis.
All these conditions may be imposed at finite depth. They are stable under unit scaling.
Right order-unit conjugation changes f by the inverse of χh∗ ◦ Nrd, exactly canceling the
norm character below. At ℓ, order units commute on the residue field, while a uniformizer
negates its trace-zero line. Since ℓ ≡ −1 (mod 4), the latter weight changes by −1.

    For scalar modularity, apply the Weil representation of (B 0 , Q) to f and the Gaussian

since Q is integral on its support. Choose a lattice of translation invariance for fp ; its dual

    Compare with the threefold ordinary-theta reference line, using the same additive
character and lifts. In a presentation B = (−a, −b), the quadratic form is aX 2 +bY 2 +abZ 2 .

                                (a, u)p (b, u)p (ab, u)p = ((ab)2 , u)p = 1.

This calculation holds also over Q2 . The remaining local diagonal scalar is therefore αp (u).
Upper unipotents, diagonal units, and sufficiently deep lower unipotents generate the

Choose the depths also to serve the ordinary-theta reference line and the character conduc-
tors. The central ambiguity of the metaplectic lifts cancels in the comparison, since both
representations have the same odd central action. At the chosen depth, a is congruent to
d−1 modulo the character conductor, so the local right eigenvalue is αp (d)−1 . Classical
restriction uses the inverse finite component and consequently gives the character ηB (d).
Conjugation by z preserves Q and commutes with the Weil action, so all summands have
this same level and scalar character. At good odd primes the lattice is spherical. The
Gaussian gives weight 3/2, and positive definiteness gives holomorphy at every cusp. Thus
HM is a classical form of level supported on 2N h∗ ℓ.
    At a prime p ∤ 2N h∗ ℓ, the neighbor relation has Hecke eigenvalue

                                              ηB (p)χh∗ (p)ap (E).

The factor χh∗ (p) comes from the reduced norm of a Brandt neighbor and ηB (p) from
scaling at the other primes.

                                                        20
    The multiplicities can be read directly in a matrix order. Put y = px. If y is primitive
and Q(y) is divisible by p2 , its nonzero nilpotent reduction has a unique invariant line; in
a basis adapted to that line the other off-diagonal entry is divisible by p2 , so exactly one
neighbor makes y/p integral. If y/p is integral, its reduction has 1 + (−Q(y/p)/p) invariant
lines, except that a zero reduction has a further p lines. These are precisely the middle and
last terms of the weight-3/2 Hecke recurrence. The factors |Rz× |−1 are the orbit-stabilizer
factors making the Brandt transfer valid. Since the eigenrelation for PM holds modulo 2M ,
so does this relation for HM .
    Put d = |h∗ |. The weights force Q(x) to be divisible by d. Before the final filter
projections, the series with coefficients aM (n) = HM (dn) is the same weighted theta sum
for Q/d. Its ternary determinant changes by d−3 , whose squareclass is d; hence the scalar
character acquires (d/·). Substitution of dn in the good-prime recurrence leaves ηB χh∗
unchanged. Now apply the filter projections. Extraction of these multiples changes the
character to ψM = ηB (|h∗ |/·), while ηM = ηB χh∗ is the good eigenvalue character. Their
quotient is quadratic. The neighbor relation therefore becomes Equation (4.1). Every
operation has level supported on S ∪ {ℓ}.

The weighted trace identity. The form and its recurrence are now constructed. We
next identify a squarefree coefficient with the reduction of the original genus sum, keeping
the orientation multiplicities and unit stabilizers explicit.
    For a squarefree D in the filter, put n = |h∗ |D = −k. An x counted in Equation
                                                                                √       (4.10)
is exactly an optimal √ embedding of the full order OKD into Rz , by sending k to x. At 2
the generator is (1 + k)/2, which is why the integrality condition above is necessary. At
a split level prime there are two choices of cyclic level orientation, and at ℓ there are two
choices of unramified quadratic orientation. There is one local orbit at the remaining primes.
The split-level assertion follows by decomposing a stable lattice into its two idempotent
summands: a cyclic quotient of order pe forces all of that quotient into one summand.
The division-prime assertion follows from the two reduced-norm parity cosets. At primes
outside 2N h∗ ℓ, stable lattices are the fractional ideals of the quadratic order, giving the
single local orbit.

constant weight on its orbit is a phase ζM (D) ∈ µ2∞ , since all its local weights are units.
Changing orientation at pe ∥N multiplies the Schwartz factor by wpe χh∗ (p)e , and multiplies
the norm-character-weighted parametrization value by the same sign. At ℓ both signs are
−1, since σχh∗ (ℓ) = −1. Hence every one of the 2ω(N )+1 orientation tuples contributes the

consequently
                   aM (D) = c0 2ω(N ) ζM (D) λM redℓ (LPD ) (mod 2M ).
                                                           
                                                                                       (4.11)
The initial basis at ℓ and initial orientation affect only ζM (D). Formula (4.11) specifies
the normalization using a character sum, with no class-number denominator. All rational
multipliers are fixed independently of D and ℓ.
    If the product derivative is zero, Gross–Zagier makes PD torsion. The fixed multiplier
kills it: genus sums and their twist comparisons are defined over biquadratic fields. The
torsion of a fixed elliptic curve in degree at most four is uniformly bounded, as one sees by
reducing its prime-to-residue-characteristic torsion at two fixed good primes of distinct
residue characteristic; the residue degrees are at most four. Thus the coefficient is zero
at the stated precision. If the derivative is nonzero, write the point against the free twist
line. Division by 2j(PD ) on that line implies the same divisibility after reduction and λM ,
up to the fixed-degree comparison and the fixed multiplier. Conversely, a visible image
bounds j(PD ) above by its visible valuation plus a fixed constant. Equation (4.11) proves
Equations (4.8) and (4.9), using Equation (4.6). Gross–Zagier–Kolyvagin then gives the
simple zero and finite Sha when the coefficient is visible. The detecting construction for
Dw supplies the final assertion. Fixed denominators or multipliers only reduce M by a
fixed amount; reindexing the precisions gives the formulation stated.

4.4   The least normalized depth
We now minimize the coefficient valuation after subtracting the prime weight w(D). This
normalization is fixed before any prime array is chosen. Attainment of the minimum will
detect analytic rank and bound the finite Selmer contribution; for the odd source this is
interpreted through the bounded-weight sequences and finite realizations below.
    For the even source, first rescale g so that a(D0 ) = 1 at one nonzero squarefree index.
Its nonzero squarefree valuations are then integers by Proposition 4.1. Set
                                                      (
                                                        1,   v(a(D)) = δ + w(D),
           δ = min{v(a(D)) − w(D)},          A(D) =                                      (4.12)
                 D                                      0,   otherwise,
with A(D) = 0 off the filter. The minimum exists: the lower bound is uniform, visible
valuations are integers after normalization, the fractional part of the weight is fixed, and a
nonzero witness exists.
    For the odd source fix a nonprincipal ultrafilter U on its sequence of precisions. Consider
all sequences DM of admissible indices for which w(DM ) is bounded and v(aM (DM )) is
visible on an ultrafilter-large set. For each such sequence, its normalized depth has a lower
bound in a discrete set. Let δ be the least depth attainable on an ultrafilter-large set. This
least depth exists and is attained: the uniform lower bound bounds it below, discreteness
gives a least value, and the fixed witness bounds the possible minimum above. For a
bounded-weight admissible sequence D = (DM ), define
                A(D) = 1      ⇐⇒     {M : v(aM (DM )) = δ + w(DM )} ∈ U,

                                              22
and set A(D) = 0 otherwise. We suppress the sequence notation when discussing finite
configurations; each application uses their simultaneous finite realizations, as proved below.
     For the odd source, a least weight supporting a unit symbol is minimized among the
same bounded-weight sequences that attain depth δ. After restricting to an ultrafilter-large
set, the weight of such a sequence may be held constant, since it lies in a bounded subset
of 12 Z≥0 . The set of these constant weights is nonempty and has a least element m. If unit
symbols of weight below m occurred on an ultrafilter-large set of stages, choosing one such
index at each stage would contradict the definition of m.

Lemma 4.3 (Uniformity at a fixed weight bound). For every fixed W , on an ultrafilter-large
set of sufficiently high precisions,

                                   v(aM (D)) ≥ δ + w(D)

for every admissible index with w(D) ≤ W , with valuations truncated at the working
precision. Any finite list of stabilized symbol assertions of bounded weight is realized
simultaneously at such precisions. The definition allows arbitrarily many weight-zero root
factors.

Proof. If the first assertion failed on an ultrafilter-large set, choose a violating DM at
each of those levels. Their weights are bounded by W and their normalized depths lie
in a fixed bounded discrete range. After selecting one depth on an ultrafilter-large set,
they contradict the definition of δ. For a finite collection of stabilized bits, intersect their
ultrafilter-large realization sets. A bound on weight, rather than on the number or size of
primes, is all that this argument uses. At weights beyond the chosen bound, the truncated
lower estimate of Proposition 4.2 still supplies any prescribed bounded-depth divisibility
test once the weight is sufficiently large. No claim of one finite level valid for all unbounded
weights is made.

Proposition 4.4 (Detection by a unit symbol). For either coefficient source, A(D) = 1
implies the prescribed analytic order: zero in the even case and one in the odd case. At
every such index,

        v(L(h)) ≤ 2w(D) + C                                    in the even case,         (4.13)
          j(PD ) ≤ w(D) + C                                    in the odd case,          (4.14)
            s(h) ≤ C,       dimF2 Sel2 (E (h) /Q) ≤ C.                                   (4.15)

The constants are fixed after choosing the original curve, filter, coefficient normalization
and genus partner. For the odd source they are independent of the detecting prime and
the working precision, and of the number and size of active primes. Statements about its
symbols are used through finite realizations as in Lemma 4.3.

Proof. In the even case substitute v(a(D)) = δ + w(D) into Equations (4.3) and (4.4).
In the odd case use a precision greater than the finitely many tested depths and apply
Equations (4.8) and (4.9). The constant δ is fixed and the comparison constants are
uniform, proving the first inequalities and the bound on s(h). At these indices the analytic
order is established, so Gross–Zagier–Kolyvagin gives the same algebraic rank and finite
Sha [31, 40]. The exact sequence for 2-Selmer then bounds its dimension by that rank, the
fixed bound on rational 2-torsion, and the 2-primary length of Sha. This proves the final
assertion without making any finiteness assumption at an index where the analytic order
has not yet been detected.


                                              23
5     Trace identities and prime substitutions
We now turn the least-depth coefficients into rules for replacing, contracting, and deleting
primes. The input is a coefficient system from Section 4, with fixed support S, characters
η, ψ, expansion g = n≥1 a(n)q n , minimum depth δ, and symbol A(D) ∈ F2 , extended by
                       P

zero off its filter. The coefficient lower bounds and the rational leading units proved there
are essential hypotheses. A unit symbol has the analytic interpretation of Proposition 4.4.
     Put F = Q(E[2]). A prime outside S is called split, simple, or a root according as its
action on E[2] is the identity, a transposition, or an element of order three; its weight is
respectively 1, 1/2, or 0. The same terminology will be used for Galois elements, according
to their image in Gal(F/Q). A nonroot is an element of either of the first two types. A
finite set of primes and its squarefree product will occasionally be denoted by the same
letter. Thus U/p means deletion of p from U , not deletion of a prime power from an
arbitrary integer.
     An oriented Frobenius label in an extension L of F means a rational arithmetic Frobenius
lift in Gal(L/Q), with a chosen place and its residual image in Gal(F/Q) retained. At
a simple prime this is the lift g of the transposition, not only the Frobenius g 2 of the
residue-degree-two place of F .
     For the odd source, fix the finite operator patterns, their weight bounds, and all
integer divisions required by the intended tests before choosing an actual stage. The
primes realizing those patterns may depend on the stage. The lemma below distinguishes
the sharp estimate on a prescribed bounded range of squarefree weights from integrality
at every Fourier index. Both are needed to form the integral weight-two trace lattice.
At a chosen stage the detecting prime belongs to S, and the level, coefficient field, and
characteristic-zero lift are fixed. Recurrences hold modulo the working precision before
division.

5.1   Normalized coefficient operators
Let U be a finite set of nonroot primes outside S. Write

The factors commute. We say that U has matching parity if δ + w(U ) is integral, and
opposite parity otherwise. Equivalently, w(U ) has, or does not have, the fractional part
prescribed by the filter. Consequently eU = 0 or 1/2, respectively.
    These operations increase the level at a newly introduced p only to exponent one. In
fact, Vp changes the character by (p/·) and raises the level by p, while on that raised level
Up = Up2 Vp . We denote the resulting character of BU by ψU . At a good prime q ∈    / S∪U

Lemma 5.1 (Coefficient bound and first shell). Write an index prime to S uniquely as

                   n = Jrm2 ,       J ⊆ U,       r squarefree,      (r, U ) = 1,

and put D = (U/J)r.
    For the even source, BU has integral coefficients and satisfies the following assertions
at every index. For the odd source, fix bounds on w(U ) and on the squarefree weights to be
tested, and fix an integer K ≥ 0. There is a larger cutoff W and an ultrafilter-large set
of arbitrarily high stages at which BU is integral at every index, the assertions below hold
whenever w(D) ≤ W , and

                                v(BU [n]) ≥ K        if w(D) > W.

These choices are uniform over prime realizations with the prescribed weight bounds.
    The coefficient BU [n] is zero unless D belongs to the filter. Within the range just
specified, it satisfies
                                             #{p ∈ J : p simple}
                       v(BU [n]) ≥ w(r) +                        − eU .                   (5.3)
                                                     2
At a split prime p ∈ J, the factor contributed by the difference operator, after removing its
character phase and one factor of 2, is
                                ap (E) − (ψ/η)(p)(−D/p) − 1
                     cp (D) =                                    (mod 2).                 (5.4)
                                              2
This is independent of the exponent of p in m. At a simple prime p ∈ J, Equation (5.4)
holds for the first shell, that is, when p ∤ m.
Proof. The good-prime recurrence of Section 4 expresses every relevant coefficient in terms
of the squarefree coefficient a(D). After removing the factor η(p)s , the multipliers for
powers p2s are determined by

         b0 = 1,      b1 = ap (E) − (ψ/η)(p)(−D/p),          bs+1 = ap (E)bs − pbs−1 .

If p | D, the Legendre symbol in b1 is zero. The recurrences at different primes commute
and their multipliers multiply.
    For p ∈ U/J the difference operator contributes bs − bs−1 , with b−1 = 0. Here p | D, so
b1 = ap (E) is even. Modulo 2 the sequence alternates between 1 and 0, and every such
difference is odd. For p ∈ J we have p ∤ D; now b0 and b1 are odd and every bs is odd. The
difference bs+1 − bs is therefore even.
    For the more precise assertion at a split prime put a = ap (E) and κ = (ψ/η)(p)(−D/p) ∈
{1, −1}. Since E[2] is rational over Fp , a ≡ p + 1 (mod 4). If ∆s = bs+1 − bs , the recurrence
gives
                               ∆s+1 = a∆s − p∆s−1 (s ≥ 1).
Every ∆s is even, whence ∆s+1 ≡ ∆s−1 (mod 4). Directly,

                   ∆1 − ∆0 = a2 − aκ − 2a + 2κ + 1 − p ≡ 0           (mod 4).

Thus ∆s /2 ≡ (a − κ − 1)/2 (mod 2) for every s. For a simple prime this last formula is
still the defining calculation at s = 0, which is all that will be required.

   Put dU = ⌈δ + w(U )⌉. Whenever the sharp squarefree bound v(a(D)) ≥ δ + w(D) is

                                                         #{p ∈ J : p simple}

For an allowed squarefree class this expression is integral, since δ +w(D) is integral. Its only
possible negative value before this parity condition is imposed is −1/2, so it is nonnegative.
For the even source the sharp bound holds at every squarefree index, which proves all-index
integrality and the displayed estimate.
    For the odd source, let u0 bound w(U ). Every prime in U is a nonroot, so #U ≤ 2u0
and the normalizing exponents dU are bounded. Proposition 4.2 gives a constant C such
that at every admissible squarefree index

                              v(aM (D)) ≥ min{M, w(D) − C}.

This includes the case of zero product derivative, when the coefficient vanishes modulo
2M . Choose W above the prescribed tested weights and so large that W − C − dU > K for
all permitted U . By Lemma 4.3, on an ultrafilter-large set of stages the sharp squarefree
bound holds simultaneously for every D with w(D) ≤ W . Take an arbitrarily high stage
in this set, with precision exceeding all the fixed normalizations, the first-shell tests, and
δ + W + 2u0 , and such that M − dU ≥ K for every permitted U .
    The recurrences and operator identities for this source hold modulo the working precision.
Their multipliers are integral, so iteration through any square exponent leaves errors in
the same precision ideal. After dividing by 2dU , the possible error has valuation at least
M − dU . Thus the sharp calculation above is valid throughout the bounded range. Outside
that range the same calculation with the coarse squarefree bound gives

                  v(BU [n]) ≥ min{M − dU , w(D) − C + #J − dU } ≥ K.

This proves integrality at every index of the actual form, while asserting the sharp estimate
only on the specified bounded range. There is no restriction on the number of weight-zero
root factors. Coefficients excluded by the filter are zero. Every removed phase is a value of
η, hence a unit reducing to 1 at the chosen prime above 2.

    At equality, the normalized leading coefficient is A(D) times the displayed shell factors.
In particular it is zero when A(D) = 0. For the odd source this assertion is used for
the finite stabilized symbol evaluations realized at the chosen stage. All omitted-prime
comparisons use the same source g and minimum δ.
    In later coefficientwise divisions, choose K to cover the largest additional division
and the desired final divisibility. If a least-weight argument also requires all lower-weight
symbols to vanish, that condition holds simultaneously on an ultrafilter-large set: a failure
would select a bounded-weight sequence contradicting the stabilized minimum. Intersect
that set with the one in the lemma. The bounded range then supplies the sharp calculation
and any required zero symbols; the coefficients beyond the cutoff already have all the
necessary factors of 2. Consequently all coefficientwise divisions and vanishings used below
hold on actual integral forms at one chosen stage for each finite collection of tests. No
stage is required to work for every unbounded configuration.
