5.3   Weight-two traces
We next express coefficient tests as traces of Galois representations. The trace identities
will give relations among the tests; the level-prime identity will compare a test with the
one obtained by omitting a prime.
    Multiply the integral half-integral-weight form under consideration by
                                                 X       2
                                     θ =1+2            qm .
                                                 m≥1

This produces a weight-two modular form with the same reduced expansion. Choose a
finite extension of Q2 containing all coefficient fields and character values needed at the
fixed level, and denote its ring of integers and maximal ideal by O and m.
    The characteristic-zero decomposition by the Hecke operators away from the level
supplies commuting operators

                                  t(u), d(u),        u ∈ GQ ,

by taking the traces and determinants of the corresponding two-dimensional Galois rep-
resentations. For cuspidal systems these are the weight-two representations of Deligne;
for Eisenstein systems they are the sums of the two defining characters. At a good
arithmetic Frobenius, t(Frq ) is the ordinary weight-two Tq , and the determinant is the
cyclotomic character times the diamond character. We use the existence and good-prime
compatibility of these representations in their characteristic-zero form [23, §3, (3.9)–(3.12),
and Theorem 4.9], and local-global compatibility [15, Theorem (A), §0.7]. We take the
contragredients of the cohomological representations in these references, so arithmetic
Frobenius has trace aq and determinant q times the diamond eigenvalue.
    Here is a precise integral framework for the reductions that follow. Take the finite-
dimensional sum of these characteristic-zero systems, retaining their multiplicity spaces,
that contains the vector under consideration and its diamond transforms. Its integral
Fourier lattice is finite over O: finitely many coefficients already give an injective map of
this space, and the lattice embeds in the corresponding finite free O-module. Good Hecke
operators preserve integral expansions. Diamonds do so as well. For the subtracted vector
this last claim follows from the formula
                                  1X
                                      BU [J] ψU (u) − η(u)χ−4 (u)i (−J/u) ΘJ :
                                                                         
           ⟨u⟩HU = ψU (u)HU +
                                  2 J

each parenthesis is zero or twice a unit. The same observation applies after multiplying by
θ.
    Traces at arbitrary Galois elements are limits of good Frobenius traces on this finite
lattice. Indeed, continuity gives a common finite quotient at any prescribed accuracy, and
Chebotarev gives a good prime in each required conjugacy class. Its size is unrestricted,
and it may avoid any specified finite list of indices and primes. This proves integrality of
all the traces and determinants used below, not only of the good Hecke operators with
which the construction began.
    Let T be the resulting commutative operator algebra on the reduced orbit of the vector.
Equality means equality of every Fourier coefficient after any further indicated operator
products. We retain this algebra, including its possible nilpotents.
Lemma 5.3 (Trace identities and fresh-prime tests). In this reduced operator algebra,

              d(u)t(v) = t(v),      t(u)t(v) = t(uv) + t(uv −1 ),     t(1) = 0.          (5.6)

                                                28
A coefficient of a product of traces can be tested by replacing its arguments with Frobenius
elements at distinct fresh primes avoiding the coefficient index. If the index is n and those
primes are q1 , . . . , qa , the test is the reduced coefficient at nq1 · · · qa . Determinants may be
applied first.
Proof. For a weight-two form f , the good-prime formula is

                                (Tq f )[n] = f [qn] + q(⟨q⟩f )[n/q].

When q ∤ n the second term is absent. Choosing the successive test primes distinct and
avoiding n proves the coefficient assertion. Chebotarev and continuity, on the finite lattice
just described, allow these choices for arbitrary trace arguments at any required accuracy.
    All the diamond characters are of 2-power order. On a form with one character its
determinant therefore reduces to the identity. For a subtracted vector the displayed
diamond formula leaves, in addition, only unary contributions ΘJ θ. A fresh trace kills each
such contribution modulo m: its coefficient test has index nq with q ∤ nJ, and cannot have
the squarefree part J. The same argument applies after any further tests. This proves
d(u)t(v) = t(v).
    For two invertible 2-by-2 matrices the characteristic-zero identity is

                           tr(A) tr(B) = tr(AB) + det(B) tr(AB −1 ).

Apply it to every characteristic-zero system and use the determinant identity just proved.
Finally, t(1) = 2 before reduction.

    The bounds of Lemma 5.1 are particularly well suited to these tests: an added prime
contributes its nonnegative weight, and additional root primes contribute no weight. A
zero established by one of these bounds consequently remains zero after all the further
tests used here. In the moving-precision case all the preceding constructions are made
at the actual fixed level of the chosen stage. The required divisibility is uniform at the
specified weight cutoff; continuity is used only in a finite-dimensional space at that stage.
    The only level-prime identity we need is the following. We distinguish the weight-
                (2)
two operator Up from the earlier half-integral-weight notation, although both extract
p-multiples in their respective Fourier expansions.
Lemma 5.4 (Inertia and a level operator). For p ∈ U , choose a place over p, an inertia
element τp on which the ramified quadratic character is −1, and a corresponding arithmetic
Frobenius lift ϕp . On the characteristic-zero space under consideration, before reduction,
one has
                      U(2)
                                                                     
                        p t(gτp ϕp ) − t(gϕp ) = d(ϕp ) t(gτp ) − t(g) .              (5.7)
The identity commutes with further good traces. After reduction it therefore evaluates a
difference at coefficient 1 by the corresponding difference at coefficient p.
Proof. At p the summands in question are either old from a level omitting p, or have
conductor exponent one and primitive ramified quadratic diamond character. For the
former the representation is unramified, so both trace differences are zero, irrespective of
                (2)
the action of Up on the old multiplicity space.
    For the latter, local-global compatibility identifies the local representation with a
principal series with one unramified and one ramified character; its monodromy is zero. In
a basis of these two lines write
                                             !                         !
                                      1 0                        λ 0
                           ρ(τp ) =        ,          ρ(ϕp ) =       .
                                      0 −1                       0 µ

                                                 29
The level eigenvalue is λ, the Frobenius eigenvalue on the unramified line, and d(ϕp ) = λµ. If
the lower-right entry of ρ(g) is z, the two trace differences are −2zµ and −2z, respectively.
Both sides of Equation (5.7) are −2zλµ. The Eisenstein calculation is the identical
calculation on its pair of characters. Copies old at other primes do not change it.
    These are all the possibilities: the new level has exponent one at p, with ramified
quadratic determinant, so the Steinberg case with unramified determinant does not enter.
A unary summand with p ∈       / J is old from a level omitting p; with p ∈ J it has the
same ramified quadratic local character. Thus the preceding classification also covers
Equation (5.5).
    Finally apply Equation (5.6) to remove the determinant from the right side after
                                                                              (2)
reduction. Taking coefficient 1 on the left is taking coefficient p before Up .

5.4   Rules when there is rational two-torsion
We now apply the common trace identities to the rational-torsion case. The coefficient
bounds first restrict the Galois data on which a symbol can depend. The level-prime
identity then determines how the symbol changes when one of those data is varied.
    An elementary extension of a number field means a compositum of quadratic extensions.
Whenever a ramification support is specified, only extensions unramified outside that
support are included. For finite support the resulting elementary extension is finite.
Labels below are Frobenius specifications in these extensions, with a chosen place when an
orientation over F is needed. They always include the fixed rational local conditions that
specify the filter.

Proposition 5.5 (Rational trace and contraction rules). Suppose E(Q)[2] ̸= 0.
  (i) For matching U , on the subtracted vector HU ,

                                       t(g)t(u) = 0   (u split).

       The trace on split elements factors through the elementary extension of F supported
       on S ∪ U . For opposite U , on BU , every product of two traces is zero, and traces
       factor through the rational elementary extension supported there.
  (ii) If U ∪ {q} has the parity of the filter, its symbol is read at coefficient 1 by t(Frq ) on
       HU for split q, and on BU for simple q.
 (iii) Fix p ∈ U and put g = Frq . Replacing g by gτp changes the symbol, additively in F2 ,
       by
                                 A (U/p) ∪ {q ′ } ,
                                                   
                                                         Frq′ ∼ gϕp ,                       (5.8)
      if p and q are not both simple. The label of q ′ is required only away from the omitted
      primes and carries their product labels. The replacement uses the original source with
      level support S ∪ (U/p): the whole rational prime p is omitted, including both places
      over it if p splits in F . If both endpoints are simple, the change is zero.
 (iv) A split vertex with trivial label relative to the level with that vertex omitted has symbol
      zero.

Proof. For matching U , two fresh tests, one of them split, add weight at least 3/2 to
Equation (5.3). The allowed valuation is integral, so the numerator has valuation at least
2. After the division in Equation (5.5) it still vanishes modulo m; fresh tests kill its unary
terms. For opposite U , two nonroot tests add weight at least 1, leaving at least 1/2 after
subtracting eU , and hence at least 1 at an allowed coefficient. This proves the two product
vanishings on the entire reduced orbit.

                                               30
    The trace identities show why these vanishings give the stated elementary quotients. If
u is split, apply the identity to t(gu)t(u) to obtain t(gu2 ) = t(g). The subgroup generated
by squares in GF has elementary quotient, since a group of exponent two is abelian. In the
opposite case the same calculation applies to every u ∈ GQ . Ramification outside the level
is absent from the characteristic-zero representations and remains absent in the quotient.
    For a split q, its added weight is 1: at coefficient q the division by 2 in HU places the
coefficient exactly at the normalized detection depth. There is no unary term at that index.
For simple q, eU = 1/2 already gives that depth on BU . The character phases reduce to 1,
proving (ii).
    For (iii), apply Lemma 5.4 at coefficient 1, so that its left side is a coefficient-p test.
Replace its trace arguments by fresh primes with Frobenius gτp ϕp and gϕp . Put V = U/p.
For X = U, V , set nX = ⌈δ + w(X)⌉ and let mX be one in matching parity and zero
otherwise. After a fresh trace kills the unary terms, the shell multiplier relative to the
omitted-source test is

                   21+nV −nU −mU +mV cp (D′ ),        D′ = V q ′ ,   Frq′ ∼ gϕp .

The exponent is zero for split p, and also for simple p with matching U . It is one for simple
p with opposite U , precisely the simple–simple case. This checks all integer divisions in
the contraction rule. For simple p only the first shell is used. The change ψU /ψV = (p/·)
is already included in that shell calculation; all remaining phases reduce to one.
    In the first three cases the shell is therefore cp (D′ )A(D′ ). Both fresh Frobenius elements
have the same image in the omitted source: that source is built from the original modular
form, the same η, ψ, δ, and the operators at V , so it is unramified at the entire rational
prime p. Inertia at either place over p acts trivially on its symbol. The ramified quadratic
coordinate of τp reverses the Legendre sign in cp , whose additive change is one. The
difference of the two shell values is consequently A(D′ ), proving Equation (5.8).
    If both primes are simple, their residual images are the same transposition, so gϕp
is split. The shell now has positive valuation after normalization and contributes zero.
Finally (iv) is (ii) with t(Frq ) = t(1) = 0 in the relevant elementary quotient.

   The contraction rules control changes in existing vertices. The next calculation gives a
way to add two simple vertices while preserving the symbol.

Proposition 5.6 (Insertion of a pair of simple primes). Suppose E(Q)[2] ̸= 0 and U has
matching parity. Let a rational elementary label, relative to S and all existing split vertices,
be realized by a simple prime q with

                                     #E(Fq ) ≡ 2      (mod 4).

Inserting two fresh simple primes with this same label leaves A(U ) unchanged. Their mutual
simple-edge label, and their labels against other simple vertices, do not affect this assertion.

Proof. First test t(Frq )2 on matching HU . The weight-two Hecke formula at coefficient 1
gives, after reduction,
                                   HU [q 2 ] + q(⟨q⟩HU )[1].
Only the J = 1 unary term can contribute at 1 or q 2 . Write b = BU [1], ϵ = χ−4 (q), and
κ = (ψU /η)(q). Substituting Equation (5.2) and the two characters in Equation (5.5), the
preceding expression is the reduction of
                               η(q)                                
                           b         aq (E) + κ(q − ϵ) − qϵi (1 + ϵ) .                     (5.9)
                                2

                                                 31
For q ≡ 3 (mod 4) the parenthesis divided by 2 is aq (E)/2 modulo 2. For q ≡ 1 (mod 4)
it is aq (E)/2 + 1. In either case it equals (q + 1 − aq (E))/2 = #E(Fq )/2 modulo 2. Since
b reduces to A(U ), this proves that the squared trace has coefficient (#E(Fq )/2)A(U ).
     By Lemma 5.3, replace the two copies of this trace by distinct fresh primes realizing
the same operator tests. Their product coefficient reads the symbol with the two primes
inserted. To pass to any other pair with the labels in the statement, vary one prime at a
time. After omitting that simple prime the opposite-parity test of Proposition 5.5 depends
only on rational elementary labels. Its changes against other simple primes are zero by
the simple–simple case of the same proposition. Thus only the fixed support and existing
split labels need be matched. Fresh intermediate primes can match both old and new
finite specifications, so that no collision of vertices is required. The assumed odd value of
#E(Fq )/2 completes the proof.

5.5   An elementary character on root elements
Assume now that E[2] is irreducible. Its image is either cyclic of order three or S3 . The
reduced trace algebra need not be a field: the order-two units below can have nontrivial
nilpotent parts. We therefore retain the algebra, rather than replacing it by its residue
field.

Lemma 5.7 (Root character). On matching BU one has t(u) = 0 for every nonroot u.
On opposite BU one has t(u) = 0 for split u and t(g)t(u) = 0 for two simple elements. In
either parity,
                                t(u)2 = 1 (u a root).
The traces factor through an extension of Gal(F/Q) by an elementary kernel over F .
Moreover, there is a multiplicative character α : GQ −→ T × into the reduced trace algebra,
unramified outside S ∪ U , such that

                     α(u)2 = 1    for all u,     t(u) = α(u)    (u a root).

In particular α factors through a rational elementary extension with that ramification
support.

Proof. The trace vanishings follow from Equation (5.3). In matching parity, one nonroot
test adds at least 1/2 and hence gives an integral valuation at least 1. In opposite parity,
either a split test or two simple tests add at least 1, again leaving positive integral valuation
after the loss eU = 1/2.
    For a root test, use the same fresh prime q twice, avoiding the coefficient index, rather
than two distinct primes. The weight-two Hecke formula and Equation (5.2) give the
identity operator modulo m: aq (E) is odd, and the middle quadratic-character term cancels
the diamond term modulo 2. This proves t(u)2 = 1 by continuity for every root u. For
completeness, it suffices to check indices prime to the chosen q; each particular index
permits such a choice of q with the prescribed finite trace data.
    Since split traces vanish in both parities, t(gu2 ) = t(g) for u ∈ GF . Thus the traces
factor through the quotient by the squares of GF . At any fixed accuracy the trace action
is on a finite lattice modulo m, so we may work in a finite quotient

                             1 −→ H −→ Γ −→ Gal(F/Q) −→ 1

with H elementary abelian. Choose an order-three lift R of a rotation. Such a lift exists
because the inverse image of the order-three subgroup is an extension by a finite 2-group.

                                               32
The trace identity with the identity element gives t(R) = t(R−1 ) = t(R2 ). Applying it to
R, R2 gives t(R)t(R2 ) = t(1) + t(R−1 ) = t(R); the left side is 1. Hence

                                      t(R) = t(R2 ) = 1.

   For a ∈ H set
                                  α(a) = t(aR) = t(aR2 ).
The equality follows from t(a) = 0 and the trace identity with R. Conjugacy invariance by
an element b ∈ H implies that α is unchanged on multiplying its argument by bR(b)−1 . It
therefore factors through the coinvariants H/[H, R]. Also it is invariant under conjugation
by R. The trace product now gives

          α(a)α(b) = t(aR)t(bR) = t aR(b)R2 + t(ab−1 ) = α aR(b) = α(ab).
                                                                        


The last equality uses the coinvariant invariance just proved; the split term is zero. Since
aR is a root, α(a)2 = 1.
    For cyclic residual image, this already extends to Γ by setting α(R) = 1. For S3 , choose
a transposition lift s normalizing ⟨R⟩. It can be obtained from any transposition lift by
conjugating its order-three complement by an element of H. Thus sRs−1 = R−1 and
s2 ∈ H. Conjugacy of traces shows that α on H is invariant under s. In both parities we
have t(s)t(sR) = 0, either because each factor is zero or by the two-simple product rule.
Its trace identity reads
                                0 = α(s2 ) + t(R) = α(s2 ) + 1.
Therefore the extension defined by α(s) = 1 respects the relation for s2 , as well as
conjugation and the relations in ⟨R⟩. It is the required multiplicative order-two character,
and agrees with the trace on both root cosets. At the fixed stage, choose one common finite
quotient through which the traces and residual representation factor, and pull back the
character constructed there. No canonical choice across quotients is needed. Unramifiedness
outside the level is preserved.

5.6   Irreducible deletion rules and minimal labels
The root character replaces products of root traces by a single multiplicative value. We
now combine that fact with the level-prime identity to compute changes at nonroot vertices.
The last lemma applies these rules at the least weight where a symbol is nonzero.
   The total root label is the product of the rational elementary labels of all root primes
being used. It includes the fixed-support conditions and the rational quadratic labels
against the active nonroot vertices. Its discriminant coordinate is trivial in the quadratic
quotient of S3 ; for cyclic residual image there is no such coordinate.

Proposition 5.8 (Irreducible substitution rules). Suppose E[2] is irreducible.
  (i) On matching BU , coefficient 1 of a product of root traces reads A on the union of U
      and those roots. It depends on the roots only through their total rational elementary
      label relative to S ∪ U . Any finite collection of roots, including the empty collection,
      may be replaced in this test by one virtual root with that label.
 (ii) Changing the Legendre bit of that root against a split vertex p has additive derivative
      equal to deletion of p, with its rational labels away from p transferred to the total root
      label. Changing the root bit against a simple vertex has derivative zero.



                                              33
 (iii) To vary a simple vertex q, omit it and use opposite BV , retaining the other root
       probes. At another simple vertex p, choose Frobenius lifts g and ϕp for q and p. If
       their transposition images are different, the derivative under g 7→ gτp deletes both p
       and q and transfers their rational labels to the root total. If their transposition images
       are equal, the derivative is zero.
 (iv) In the test in (iii), variation at a split p is given by the first-shell factors cp times
       symbols of merged configurations whose weight is one less. It consequently vanishes
       whenever all symbols of that lower weight vanish.
All these assertions preserve every specified label at the other vertices, and use the chosen
place for inertia and Frobenius.
Proof. Root primes have weight zero. Thus the fresh-prime tests at coefficient 1 of matching
BU are precisely the normalized coefficients defining A. Lemma 5.7 makes the product
of root traces equal to the value of α on their total elementary label. A rotation survives
every compatible elementary specification: an elementary quotient cannot eliminate the
order-three subgroup, and in the S3 case the only compatibility condition is its trivial
image in the quadratic quotient. Chebotarev therefore supplies the claimed single virtual
root, even for the empty total label.
    For (ii), apply Lemma 5.4 with g a root. If p is split then gϕp is a root. The coefficient
with J = {p} has zero cost in Equation (5.3); its first shell is cp times the symbol with p
deleted. The latter is unramified at p and is the same in the two tests, while cp changes by
1. Multiplication of Frobenius labels by ϕp is exactly the transfer of the deleted vertex’s
rational labels to the root. If p is simple, gϕp is simple, and its weight 1/2 plus the 1/2
contributed by J puts the shell above the testing depth. The derivative is then zero.
    For (iii), eV = 1/2. When the two transpositions differ, gϕp is a rotation, and the
1/2 contribution from the simple prime in J = {p} exactly cancels eV . The shell again
equals cp times the omitted-p symbol, now with both simple vertices removed and their
product represented by a root. The Legendre flip proves the asserted derivative. For equal
transpositions their product is split; its additional weight 1 forces the shell to vanish.
    Finally, if p is split and g simple, then gϕp is simple. The p-shell is at the first-shell
depth and is expressed by Equation (5.4) with D′ = (V /p)q ′ and the retained root probes.
Its nonroot weight is
                                           1              1
                                                           
                              w(V ) − 1 + = w(V ) +           − 1.
                                           2              2
This is one less than the original weight. In particular both shell terms vanish when symbols
of that weight vanish. The comparison omitting p is unramified there. Root probes can
be matched on the rational elementary support throughout, which proves the final label
assertion as well.
   At the least nonzero weight, every lower-weight term in the preceding rules vanishes.
This removes the dependence on internal edge labels; the remaining assertion keeps both
the fixed-support labels and the total root label.
Lemma 5.9 (Transfer at the least nonzero weight). Suppose E[2] is irreducible, and let m
be the least weight of an index on the filter with nonzero symbol. At weight m, the dependence
on each nonroot vertex is only through its oriented rational Frobenius lift in the Galois
group over Q of the maximal elementary extension of F unramified outside S, together
with the requirement on the total root label. In particular, replacing the nonroot vertices by
fresh vertices with those same fixed-support labels preserves the symbol, irrespective of their
internal edge labels, provided the root total is matched. The assertion allows simultaneous
changes using disjoint intermediate configurations.

                                               34
Proof. Every symbol of weight less than m is zero. For a simple vertex, Proposition 5.8
therefore makes its inertia derivatives at every other active nonroot vertex zero: different
transpositions give a lower-weight deletion, equal transpositions give zero directly, and
a split vertex gives a lower-weight merged configuration. Its labels against root vertices
are immaterial by the simple case of the root derivative rule. These statements hold for
conjugates of inertia too, because the Frobenius and inertia calculation is made at a chosen
place and may be conjugated. Starting with the elementary extension over F supported
on S ∪ V , killing the inertia at the active vertices leaves exactly fixed-support elementary
labels. This proves the assertion for a simple vertex.
    For a split vertex q, let V be the other nonroot vertices, so w(V ) = m − 1 and V still
has matching parity. We first claim that BV is divisible by 2 integrally. In Equation (5.3),
an entry of cost zero has r consisting only of roots and J consisting only of split primes.
Its underlying squarefree class has weight

                                      w(V ) − #J < m.

Its symbol is zero. The shell formula at every split prime, valid at all exponents, therefore
adds one more factor of 2 to the normalized coefficient. All other allowed entries already
have positive integral cost. This proves the claim, including at arbitrarily large coefficient
indices.
    Use now HV = BV /2. Two split traces give zero on HV : their added weight 2 in
Equation (5.3) still leaves one factor of 2 after this division. The trace restricted to split
elements consequently factors through the elementary kernel over F , by the same square-
invariance argument as before. Coefficient 1 with the new split trace and the retained root
probes reads the symbol at weight m.
    Apply Lemma 5.4 at p ∈ V to this divided vector. The element gϕp has the type of
p, because g is split. Its p-shell is cp times a symbol of weight m − 1, whether p is split
or simple; for a simple p only the first shell is used. For a simple p the normalization
can be checked directly: J = {p} and the new simple probe contribute 1/2 + 1/2 = 1 to
Equation (5.3). Writing nV = δ + w(V ), the divided shell is

                2cp (D′ )a(D′ )               ′
                                        ′ a(D )
                                = cp (D  )      ,   w(D′ ) = w(V ) = m − 1.
                    2nV +1                 2nV
The omitted set V /p has opposite parity, but its ceiling normalization is still nV . Thus
this is exactly its unit-symbol test, with no fractional loss. Both compared values are zero.
Every such inertia derivative therefore vanishes, leaving only the fixed-support elementary
label.
    No character α on this further divided vector is needed. Use actual matched root probes
in both tests. At weight m, their changes against a split vertex are already zero by the split
root-deletion rule, because its deletion has weight m − 1. They can therefore be matched
while the nonroot vertex is moved. Moving vertices successively, using fresh intermediate
copies when necessary, proves the joint assertion without imposing old internal edge labels.
    For a moving coefficient system, minimality is used on a fixed bounded weight range. A
failure of the required lower-weight vanishing on an ultrafilter-large set of stages would give
a bounded-weight sequence contradicting the stabilized minimum. Choose the actual stage
as in Lemma 5.1, with the additional division by 2 included among its fixed division bounds.
Its coarse estimate controls all larger weights, and its integral square-index recurrences
control every shell. Thus the preceding coefficientwise arguments apply to the actual forms
at that stage. The argument does not require m to be integral.


                                               35
5.7    Summary and finite realization
We collect the rules in the form used to construct finite prime arrays. The analytic meaning
of a nonzero symbol is unchanged; this section has established which substitutions preserve
it or compute its change.
Theorem 5.10 (Symbol rules). For either coefficient source of Propositions 4.1 and 4.2,
assume its stated coefficient lower bound and use its normalized symbol and filter. Then
the following rules hold for finite configurations of primes outside the level support.
   (i) With rational two-torsion, the elementary-label factorizations, contraction except in
       the simple–simple case, and isolation by a trivial split label are those of Proposition 5.5.
       A pair of identical simple labels realized with #E(Fq )/2 odd can be inserted without
       changing the symbol, as in Proposition 5.6.
  (ii) With irreducible E[2], all root probes combine into one total rational elementary label.
       A root-bit derivative at a split vertex deletes that vertex, while at a simple vertex it is
       zero. The derivative between two simple vertices deletes both when their transposition
       images are different, and is zero when they are equal. The remaining simple–split
       shell has weight one less. These are the rules of Proposition 5.8.
 (iii) At the least weight supporting a nonzero symbol in the irreducible case, each nonroot
       may be moved with only its fixed-support elementary Frobenius label prescribed;
       internal edges impose no further condition on the value, subject to matching the total
       root label. This is Lemma 5.9.
The analytic and finite-Selmer consequences of a nonzero symbol remain those of Proposi-
tion 4.4. In the odd source the rules mean finite tests at arbitrarily high stages chosen in
the ultrafilter-large sets of the prescribed stabilization.
Proof. The preceding propositions prove the assertions. For one application, put the
finitely many Galois modules and operator tests in a common finite quotient at the required
precision. Chebotarev then gives simultaneously fresh Frobenius representatives. The
omitted source is unramified at the whole omitted prime, whereas its ramified rational
quadratic coordinate is exactly the Legendre flip in the shell formula. These are therefore
substitutions with all other labels held fixed.
    The arithmetic compatibility of an entire array is addressed in the following sections.
The present assertions concern each finite array; in the odd case choose a sufficiently high
actual stage in the required ultrafilter-large intersection. Arbitrarily many weight-zero
roots cause no additional depth loss, because the coefficient bound is uniform at a fixed
weight and their traces combine through one total elementary label.



6     Prime graphs and simultaneous unit coefficients
The coefficient symbols will now be regarded as functions of finitely many quadratic residue
bits. We first identify the arithmetic data that those bits record and explain how the trace
calculation becomes a finite difference. This gives polynomials whose derivatives contract
prime labels. We then adjoin auxiliary vertices so that one or two nonzero coefficient
polynomials take the value 1 at every nonzero address of a binary cube.
    The two-polynomial case is needed for the odd converse: the odd symbol tests h, while
an even symbol tests a quadratic companion kh. Separate nonzero polynomials need not
have a common unit evaluation; for example, z and 1 + z do not. The auxiliary vertices
change the polynomials by controlled contraction operators. The arithmetic input is the

                                                36
rational contraction and isolation rules of Theorem 5.10, including omission of an entire
rational prime. The same argument applies to any coefficient test with these rules, such as
the restricted even test constructed in Section 7.5.
    Encoding quadratic residue symbols by graphs has a precedent in the congruent-number
problem: Feng–Xiong express descent conditions through prime graphs and the ranks of
their binary Laplacians [27, §2, Definitions 2.1 and 2.3 and Lemma 2.2]. Here the graphs
record coefficient-symbol rules, and their oriented contractions produce simultaneous unit
values.

6.1   Prime labels and their realization
Let F be Q or a quadratic field. The applications use F = Q(E[2]) when this field is
quadratic, and also an auxiliary quadratic field when E[2] is rational. Fix a finite set S of
rational primes containing 2 and the ramification support of the fixed data. Enlarge S so
that prime ideals above it generate the class group of F , and let SF include these places
and the infinite places. Set

                VS (F ) = {α ∈ F × /F ×2 : vp (α) ≡ 0   (mod 2) for p ∈
                                                                      / SF }.

The valuation sequence expresses this as an extension of the squareclasses of SF -units by a
subgroup of the 2-torsion of the SF -class group; in particular, it is finite-dimensional over
                                          (2)      p
F2 [47, Theorems 4.4 and 5.11]. Write FS = F ( VS (F )).
    A split vertex is a rational prime p ∈
                                         / S split in F , with an orientation p | p when F
is quadratic. Choose a generator πp whose divisor outside SF is p; this is possible by the
class-group condition. Use its conjugate for the other orientation. Then

                     NF/Q (πp ) = pup ,     up a signed rational S-unit.                 (6.1)

For F = Q, use the generator p. In the quadratic case an inert rational prime is a simple
                                                                                         (2)
vertex, with rational generator p. A split vertex has its fixed Frobenius label on FS ; a
simple vertex has rational fixed-support labels. A type may also specify local squareclasses,
support valuation parities, real signs, and finite ray conditions on its generator. Prescribed
types must be compatible and individually realizable. A filter fixes the total rational labels
and sign of the signed-prime product, where p∗ = (−1/p)p.
     Write quadratic residue symbols additively in F2 . Between two split vertices in the
quadratic case there are two independent forward bits: the residues of πp and πp at the
chosen place of the other vertex. Hilbert reciprocity and the types determine the reverse
bits. Their sum, with the fixed correction in (6.1), gives the rational norm bit. If an
endpoint is simple, or if F = Q, there is one rational forward bit. Vertices, types, and
these bits form an arithmetic graph; there is no self-residue datum.
     For the coefficient symbols, the vertices are the prime factors of the Fourier index.
Their types fix the support and elementary Frobenius conditions, while the edge bits record
the remaining quadratic residue tests. Varying an edge leaves those types and the total
filter unchanged. The next calculation explains the resulting contraction rule.
     For the standard rational-two-torsion symbol of Proposition 5.5, take split primes p, q
and let U contain p but not q, with matching parity. Write V = U/p and g = Frq . By
Proposition 5.5,                                      
                                    A(U q) = t(g)HU [1].
Changing one residue bit from p to q replaces g by gτp , where τp is the corresponding
inertia element. The level identity (5.7) moves the difference to the coefficient at p. Its

                                             37
two first-shell values are

             cp (D′ )A(D′ ),    (cp (D′ ) + 1)A(D′ ),    D′ = V q ′ ,   Frq′ ∼ gϕp .

The omitted source is unramified at the whole prime p, so its value A(D′ ) is the same in
the two terms. Their sum is A(D′ ). Thus, if z is the changed edge bit,

                                  A z=0 + A z=1 = A(V q ′ ).                              (6.2)

Here q ′ is a fresh prime carrying the product labels of p and q; it replaces two vertices by
one. Its integer value is not the product pq.
    In the quadratic field, the two choices of oriented bit correspond to using πp πq or
πp πq as a product template, up to the choice of output orientation. The template adds
the support labels and the external residue bits in the indicated orientations. There is
no internal residue attached to the new vertex. Omitting p removes both places above
that rational prime from the source, so a second difference in the other oriented bit gives
zero. Split–simple contraction has the same interpretation with rational product data; a
simple–simple difference is zero.
    To use these formulas on arbitrary arrays of bits, we must know that the arrays and
the product templates have prime representatives, and that different representatives give
the same symbol. The following lemma supplies both facts.

Lemma 6.1 (Finite realization and product labels). Fix finitely many old vertices and
individually realizable new types. Every choice of independent forward bits is realized by
distinct fresh primes, avoiding any given finite set. Further fixed local tests may be included
in S.
    A split cluster may be represented by the product of its oriented generators, with
conjugations prescribed along a contraction tree; its support types and external bits are the
corresponding sums. A cluster containing one simple vertex has the corresponding rational
product data. These products may be replaced by fresh prime representatives. For a symbol
satisfying the omitted-vertex dependence of Theorem 5.10, the graph data determine its
value, and these replacements preserve the contracted symbol.

Proof. The displayed generators account for every relevant radical. Let U be a finite set
of new rational primes and UF the places above them. From a squareclass with even
valuations outside SF ∪ UF subtract the generators at the primes of UF where its valuation
is odd. The remainder lies in VS (F ). At an inert prime its rational generator has valuation
one at the unique place of F . Thus the fixed labels and residue bits determine every
elementary Frobenius test with this ramification support.
    For a new split vertex, start with a template π0 of its type, at a prime ideal p0 away
from the old vertices. Weak approximation gives a ∈ F × sufficiently close to 1 at SF ,
positive at the specified real places, and with residues at the old prime ideals chosen so
that aπ0 has the prescribed quadratic residues. Use a ray modulus containing deep enough
powers of SF , all old prime ideals, and the real places. A prime ideal q in the ray class of
(a)p0 satisfies
                        q = (ab)p0 ,    b ≡ 1 (mod ray modulus).
Then abπ0 has the desired divisor and type. The prime ideals in this ray class have positive
Dirichlet density [48, Chapter V, Theorem 2.5]. For s > 1, the sum of (N q)−s over prime
ideals of residue degree greater than one is at most 2 p p−2s , which stays bounded as s ↓ 1.
                                                      P

The corresponding sum over the ray class diverges. Hence that class contains infinitely


                                               38
many degree-one primes. Outside the fixed ramification support these give split rational
primes, and finite exclusions remove finitely many choices.
     At an old inert prime, prescribe instead the norm of the residue in F×     p2 . Its norm
         ×
onto Fp is surjective. For a new simple vertex all conditions are rational congruences,
including the inert class in F ; the Chinese remainder theorem and Dirichlet’s theorem
apply. Successive selection realizes the whole graph. Reciprocity determines reverse bits,
since the support Hilbert symbols are fixed and only the two endpoint residues remain.
Taking norms gives (6.1), with no extra independent condition.
     The same construction applies to a product template. It preserves its support data and
its residues at every remaining vertex; no internal residue is imposed on the replacement.
For a simple cluster use rational congruences for the norm labels. To check the rational
filter in a split cluster, write π = i πi and π ′ = abπ for its replacement. The multiplier ab
                                    Q

is a square unit at SF and positive at real places. Consequently

                                                     N π ′ = qu
                                        Y
                               Nπ = (       pi )u,
                                        i
                                                                                Q
have the same signed rational S-unit u: its valuations and sign agree. Thus q/ i pi = N (ab)
is locally square at the rational support. The condition at 2 also identifies q ∗ with i p∗i in
                                                                                      Q

the fixed tests. This verifies preservation of the norm correction.
     Finally, compare two disjoint realizations of the same graph. Insert fresh bridge vertices
with matching bits to both realizations and replace vertices one at a time. The omitted-
vertex Frobenius tests agree by the radical description above, so the symbol values agree. A
preliminary bridge removes any overlap. This proves both graph dependence and invariance
under the product replacements.

    Consequently, fixing the vertex types gives a well-defined function AG of the independent
forward edge bits: choose any prime realization of the graph G and evaluate its coefficient
symbol. Lemma 6.1 makes the result independent of that choice. We will write its unique
multilinear polynomial as P . Equation (6.2) says that a derivative of P evaluates the
contracted graph.
    A split vertex is primary if its generator is a square unit at every finite place of SF and
positive at every real place. The principal ray class realizes such types. Reciprocity makes
their fixed radical labels trivial, and their signed rational contributions are squares in the
fixed tests. Including stationary vertex primes in those tests makes primary incidences
to them neutral. Including√the support of a quadratic discriminant k ensures that every
primary prime splits in Q( k).
    Two symbols may use different fixed-support tests or read only rational norm labels
from this same graph. We call these compatible projections when they forget fixed-label
coordinates or take norm data, preserve primary vertices, and commute with the omitted-
vertex product substitution. In the norm projection either oriented-bit toggle changes the
rational bit once. Thus one physical edge difference induces the specified difference for
each symbol; it need not give the same polynomial for both symbols.
Definition 6.2 (Witnesses and permitted partitions). A witness is a finite graph with
symbol 1. A graph is partitionable if it admits a partition into nonempty clusters, each
containing at most one simple vertex, and oriented contraction trees whose contracted
graph is a witness. The contraction trees are part of the data; this definition makes no
assertion about a Selmer group.
     For a two-isogenous curve with quadratic field F ′ = Q(E ′ [2]), a simple vertex is good
if it is inert in both F and F ′ . A donor is split in F and inert in F ′ . Attaching a donor

                                                39
turns a simple vertex split in F ′ into a good one. These are conditions on finite labels;
their supply will be established in the arithmetic application.

6.2    Forests as finite-difference operators
For an edge bit z, put ∆z f = f |z=0 + f |z=1 . Every function on a finite binary cube has a
unique multilinear polynomial over F2 ; ∆z is its formal derivative. Differences therefore
commute and satisfy ∆2z = 0.

Lemma 6.3 (Oriented forests). Assume the contraction, isolation, and omitted-prime
assertions of Theorem 5.10. Differentiation along a tree contracts it, adding product
labels and external incidences; the result is zero if the tree contains two simple vertices.
A collection of edge variables containing a cycle has zero derivative, including when its
orientations are incompatible. The two oriented variables on one pair form a cycle of length
two.

Proof. The one-edge rule is the symbol contraction identity, with product replacement
supplied by Lemma 6.1. Induction contracts a tree: split–split mergers remain split,
split–simple mergers remain simple, and merging two simple components gives zero. A
root orientation determines all relative orientations along the tree. Its product template
uses support types and external bits, not the differentiated internal bits.
    For the two variables on a split pair p, q, differentiate first in one inertia bit over p.
The resulting symbol omits the entire rational prime p, so its elementary extension is
unramified at both places over p. The derivative in the other oriented bit is zero. Repeated
use of the same variable gives zero by ∆2z = 0.
    For a longer cycle, contract a spanning path. The closing edge becomes internal to
one cluster and changes neither its support label nor an incidence to a remaining vertex.
The omitted-endpoint argument just given therefore kills its derivative. Conjugating the
product only changes its orientation; it does not restore the omitted endpoint or create a
self-residue.
    For example, after contracting aligned edge 12, the two bits from its product to vertex
3 are t0 = z130 + z 0 and t = z 1 + z 1 , up to fixed type corrections. An aligned edge 23
                     23      1    13    23
differentiates t0 . The closing edge 31 differentiates either t0 again, giving zero, or t1 , giving
the omitted-endpoint zero. Every longer cycle reduces to these cases. Further differences
commute with this zero derivative.

    Call the original vertices terminals; any stationary external vertices retain their types
and incidences. Fix their vertex types and a realizable baseline graph. Write z = (ze ) for
the independent toggles of the terminal–terminal bits, and let P (z) be the terminal symbol.
A network adjoins primary vertices and specified incidences involving them. It adds no
terminal–terminal edge: the original bits z remain parameters. All unspecified primary
incidences are neutral. Within each network fix a consistent orientation at each terminal.
Different networks may use different alignments.
    Write y = (ye ) for the bits on the added incidences, and let f (z, y) be the symbol of
the enlarged graph. We shall express f (z, 1) as a finite-difference operator applied to P (z).
The differences in y first contract the added graph; the forest rule then expresses each
surviving term as differences in the original variables z.
    For example, join one primary vertex r to two permitted terminals a, b, in a fixed
alignment. Expanding in its two added incidences gives four terms. The term with no
difference is zero because r is isolated. Each one-edge difference contracts r onto one
terminal and leaves the original symbol P , so these two terms cancel in F2 . The two-edge

                                                40
tree contracts the terminal pair and gives ∆zab P . Thus this path acts exactly as the
terminal difference ∆zab . The path has two added edges, but its operator has contraction
degree one. In general, a component meeting t terminals has terminal contraction degree
t − 1.
    The lowest two operator degrees can be read from a matrix. Index the internal primary
vertices by I, let eij be their internal edge bits, and let ca be the incidence column from
them to terminal a. The grounded Laplacian is the symmetric matrix over F2 with
                                                                 X              X
                      Qij = eij   (i ̸= j),       Qii =                 eij +       (ca )i .
                                                                 j̸=i           a

Equivalently,                                     X
                                         Q1 =             ca .                                 (6.3)
                                                      a
Thus prescribed off-diagonal entries and terminal columns realize a given symmetric Q
precisely when this row-sum identity holds. For the one-primary-vertex path above, Q = (0)
and ca = cb = (1).
    The coefficients below are instances over F2 of the all-minors matrix-tree theorem [19,
§1, Equation (2)]; we include the incidence-matrix proof needed here.
Lemma 6.4 (Network expansion). A network satisfies f (z, 1) = N P (z), where N is a
sum of terminal finite-difference operators indexed by forests. In each surviving forest term,
every component containing an internal primary vertex meets a terminal. A component
meeting t terminals has contraction degree t − 1; a component containing two simple
terminals acts as zero. Networks with disjoint internal vertices and no edges between them
multiply as operators on the same P . In particular, degree greater than deg P annihilates
it.
    For the grounded Laplacian Q and terminal columns ca defined above, the constant and
the pair-contraction coefficients are

                              det Q,      cta adj(Q)cb             (a ̸= b).                   (6.4)

The latter multiplies the terminal difference in the selected alignment; a prohibited pair
multiplies the zero operator.
Proof. For the set E0 of added incidences, keep the terminal variables z fixed and expand
in the variables y:
                                              (∆yT f )(z, 0).
                                          X
                               f (z, 1) =
                                              T ⊆E0

Lemma 6.3 removes cycles. A component meeting no terminal contracts to an isolated
primary vertex, so isolation removes it, including an untouched primary vertex. In a
surviving component the primary labels and external primary bits are trivial. Its contracted
label and external incidences are thus exactly those obtained by contracting its terminals in
the original graph. A terminal tree with the same relative orientations gives that operator,
of degree t − 1.
    Expanding two disjoint networks gives composition: product labels agree in either
order, and any resulting cycle vanishes. Polynomial differentiation then proves the degree
bound. This reasoning also verifies compatibility with projections at every degree. Norms
commute with oriented products, primary norm corrections are neutral, and either oriented
toggle induces its rational toggle on the original terminal polynomial. Repeated projected
differences or prohibited simple mergers give zero. Forgetting fixed-label coordinates
commutes with the same products.

                                                41
    For the coefficients, write the Laplacian of the added network as L = DDt , where
each incidence column has ones at its edge endpoints. Let I and A be the internal and
terminal sets. Cauchy–Binet for Q = LI,I counts edge sets whose incidence matrix becomes
invertible after deleting the terminal rows. They are exactly forests with one terminal in
each component: cycles give dependent columns, a component with no deleted row gives
dependent rows, and a tree with one row deleted is invertible by leaf removal. These are
the degree-zero terms.
   Apply Cauchy–Binet instead to row set I ∪ {a} and column set I ∪ {b}. Each component
must contain one root from each of A \ {a} and A \ {b}. Thus a, b lie together and every
other terminal lies in its own component. The corresponding minor is
                                                  !
                                       Q cb
                                   det t              = cta adj(Q)cb .
                                       ca 0

The corner is zero because there is no added terminal–terminal edge. Signs disappear in
characteristic two, and the adjugate identity remains valid when Q is singular.

6.3    Selecting one address
An address is x ∈ Fb2 , where b ≥ 1. A vertex with activation λ ∈ (Fb2 )∗ is present when
λ(x) = 1. Fix a nonzero target x∗ and an edge bit z joining terminals a0 , a1 active there.
Either all terminals are always present, or all have nonzero linear activations with at
least one terminal for each nonzero linear form. For one symbol we need a derivative as
the leading term at x∗ and a constant term 1 elsewhere. For two symbols we must also
account for a derivative allocated to just one factor; the second network includes a binary
parameter for this purpose. We construct these two networks by prescribing their constant
and degree-one terms; the final degree count will remove every higher term.
      Choose a dual basis λ1 , . . . , λb with λi (x∗ ) = 1 for every i. Indeed, start with λ1 (x∗ ) = 1
and add it to a basis of the annihilator of x∗ . Make b groups of three primary vertices, with
all three vertices in group i activated by λi . The physical coordinate vectors index these
actual vertices. In each three-dimensional coordinate space choose a computational basis
(ei , ui , vi ) with ei = (1, 1, 1). Prescribe a symmetric form Q by making the ei orthonormal
and orthogonal to all uj , vj , and using on the remaining coordinates

Q. Write QJ for the restriction to the physical coordinates in groups J.

do not mix groups, hi is supported on group i; it is independent of T, B. Attach this
incidence column to a terminal of activation λi , or to any fixed terminal in the stationary
case. When the active groups are J, the sum of these anchor columns is

The third terminal is absent at x∗ . Equal activations, or two stationary terminals, require

these attachments preserve the grounding at every restriction.

filtration, not a numerical estimate. Figure 1 depicts the group-level cycle and path used

proper J, including the empty restriction, whose determinant is 1.
