6.4   Simultaneous units on a punctured cube
The preceding programs control only two degrees of their operators. To make this sufficient,
at each address we spend exactly the degree of a chosen top monomial. Any additional
contraction then vanishes. For two symbols, the same accounting uses the ordinary product
of their top homogeneous parts. The multiplication is performed before imposing the
Boolean relation z 2 = z, so one occurrence of z in each factor remains two contraction
requirements.
Theorem 6.6 (Address lemma). Assume the contraction, isolation, and whole-prime
omission rules of Proposition 5.5, or the same rules for another symbol, together with the
realization freedoms of Lemma 6.1. Let b ≥ 1 and consider either of the following terminal
arrangements:
   1. One symbol on a nonempty set of always active terminals, whose polynomial in the
      independent pair bits is nonzero.
   2. One or two symbols on terminals with nonzero linear activations, including a terminal
      for every nonzero linear form. At each x ̸= 0, each symbol separately is a nonzero
      polynomial in the active bits. The symbols may have stationary external data and
      may read compatible projections of a common graph.
At every x ̸= 0, assume that the active terminal data and the stationary external data obey
each required filter. Then finitely many primary auxiliaries with nonzero linear activations,
and one realizable assignment of graph bits, make all the symbols 1 at every x ̸= 0. No
auxiliary is present at 0. All may satisfy fixed splitting and neutral-incidence conditions
included in their primary type.
    A nonzero evaluation or a nonzero iterated edge difference suffices for the polynomial
hypothesis. In particular, a partition as in Definition 6.2 suffices in the stationary case.
Proof. Fix a common realizable baseline for the terminal bits and regard their toggles as
variables; omit variables invisible to a symbol. A nonzero iterated difference implies a
nonzero polynomial, while every nonzero polynomial has a top monomial with coefficient 1.
By Lemma 6.3, this also proves the assertion about partitions.
    For one symbol, let Px have degree dx at x ̸= 0 and choose a monomial of degree dx
with coefficient 1. For each of its variables adjoin a separate singular program targeted at
x, all with disjoint internal vertices. At address x, its own dx programs have zero constant,
so they consume at least dx contraction degrees. Any extra degree from any program kills
Px . Only the selected degree-one terms of these programs and the constants of the others
survive, giving the chosen coefficient 1. A nonzero constant polynomial needs no program.
Stationary anchors give the same argument for always active terminals.

                                                44
    For two symbols, write their polynomials at x as Px , Rx , of degrees dx , ex . Multiply
their top homogeneous parts in the ordinary ring F2 [z1 , . . . , zm ], before imposing Boolean
relations. This ring is a domain, so choose a coefficient 1 at a monomial
                          Y                                       X
                   Mx =        z νxz ,    νxz ∈ {0, 1, 2},             νxz = dx + ex .      (6.7)
                           z                                       z

For each exponent two add one singular program targeted at x; for each exponent one add
one nonsingular program, with its own parameter εxz ∈ F2 . Keep all internal sets disjoint.
Let                        Y
                    Φ(ε) =     (final value of Px )(final value of Rx ).
                                  x̸=0
               P
We show that ε Φ(ε) = 1, which implies that one choice makes every factor 1.
    Expand the operators by contraction degree. At its target a singular program has
zero constant in both factors, so it consumes at least one degree in each. A nonsingular
program has constant 1 independent of its parameter. Summing that parameter cancels
a term using only its constants everywhere; a surviving term must consume at least one
degree somewhere for that program. The total of these minimum costs is
                                                X
                                          D=        (dx + ex ).                             (6.8)
                                                x̸=0

A nonzero term cannot consume more, since some factor would then be differentiated
beyond its degree. Every surviving term therefore uses each minimum cost exactly.
    Thus a singular program contributes precisely ∆z in each factor at its target and
constants elsewhere. A nonsingular program contributes degree one in exactly one factor.
Summing its parameter kills that contribution away from its target and gives ∆z at the
target; every other occurrence contributes the common constant 1. In particular, no higher
forest coefficient remains.
    At one address put Zi = {z : νxz = i} for i = 1, 2, and write zJ = z∈J z. The
                                                                            Q

surviving allocation sum is
                    X
                          [zZ2 zJ ]Pxtop [zZ2 zZ1 \J ]Rxtop = [Mx ](Pxtop Rxtop ) = 1.
                   J⊆Z1

Wrong-degree coefficients are zero. Since the total degree is D and no factor exceeds its
degree, each factor receives its full degree; its derivative is exactly the displayed coefficient,
independent of the baseline. Allocations at distinct addresses are independent, so their
product is 1. This covers constant factors too. For instance, if both top forms are z, their
ordinary product is z 2 : a singular program differentiates each once. Boolean reduction
would lose that allocation.
   Choose parameters with Φ = 1 and realize the resulting finite graph by Lemma 6.1.
Primary types preserve the filters and the stipulated neutral incidences and splitting
conditions. Nonzero linear activations ensure that no new vertex appears at 0.

    For a stabilized symbol this is a finite construction at each dimension. First choose b,
the terminal data, and all networks; their weights and coefficient tests then have a finite
bound. Select an actual stage where the numerical bounds and the finitely many stabilized
symbol evaluations hold for that range. The required precision may grow with b. The
unit-valuation constant, however, is the constant of the coefficient construction and must
be independent of b; the forest argument supplies no additional uniformity assertion.



                                                   45
7     The converse with rational two-torsion
We prove the pointwise converse for curves with E(Q)[2] ̸= 0. The finite graph construction
supplies binary families with a unit coefficient at every nonzero address. Cyclotomic
interpolation settles the even case. For the odd case we first establish the missing lower
bound for the moving coefficient system, then keep one nonvanishing quadratic companion
fixed throughout the interpolation.
    Fix such a curve E/Q. We use the prime weights w(h), normalized values L(h),
and finite 2-primary Shafarevich–Tate lengths s(h) from the preceding sections. The
corank c2 (E (h) ) is a different quantity: a corank-zero base has finite s(h) before analytic
nonvanishing is known. All new primes are odd primes of good reduction. A filter fixes
the real sign and the local unit squareclasses at a finite support containing 2N , and may
exclude any further fixed finite set.
    For a signed parameter h, write A(h) for the symbol at the positive index |h| on its
chosen filter. We will use two bounds attached to a unit coefficient:

    A(h) = 1 in the even system     =⇒     L(E (h) , 1) ̸= 0,     v(L(h)) ≤ 2w(h) + C,    (7.1)
    A(h) = 1 in the odd system      =⇒     a(E (h) ) = 1,       j(Ph ) ≤ w(h) + C.        (7.2)

Here Ph is the genus Heegner sum with a fixed analytic-rank-zero partner h∗ , and j(Ph )
is its free-line index valuation. The first bound is available from the even coefficient
construction and Theorem 3.5. We use the second only after Proposition 7.7 has supplied
the arithmetic lower bound required to normalize the odd system. The contraction and
isolation identities are those of Theorem 5.10; their finite graph consequences were proved
in Section 6.                                                       √
    Write jh (k) = j(P|h| (h)) for the ring-class index over K = Q( k). Theorem 3.7 states
that, if k < 0 is an odd fundamental discriminant with (k, 2N h) = 1, has at most A prime
factors, is square at 2N h, and defines a nonexceptional imaginary field, and the twists
h, hk have analytic orders one and zero, then

                           2jh (k) ≥ 4w(h) + s(h) + s(hk) − CE,A .                        (7.3)

All coefficient and interpolation constants depend on the fixed curve, its parametrization,
and fixed local data. A bound on ω(k) may enter CE,A ; the sizes of its primes do not. The
finite networks, required coefficient precision, and prime sizes may depend on the binary
dimension. The unit bounds in Equations (7.1) and (7.2) may not.
    By Lemma 2.2, an isogeny preserves analytic rank and Selmer corank. If the isogeny
class contains a curve with full rational two-torsion, we use that curve. Otherwise choose
a rational two-isogeny E → E ′ and write F = Q(E[2]), F ′ = Q(E ′ [2]); both fields are
quadratic. A prime split in F is called split, and one inert in F is called simple. Their
weights are respectively 1 and 1/2. A simple prime is good if it is also inert in F ′ . At such
a prime
                                   #E(Fp ) ≡ 2 (mod 4).                                    (7.4)
Indeed there is exactly one nonzero rational two-torsion point in the reduction. If the
group order were divisible by four, its two-primary subgroup would contain a rational point
of order four. Its image under the two-isogeny would supply on E ′ a rational two-torsion
point different from the dual kernel, contradicting inertness in F ′ . Thus the good-twin rule
applies. When F = F ′ , every simple prime is good.




                                              46
7.1   Seeds and finite supplies
The word “label” below means an individually realizable fixed-support elementary Frobenius
label, with orientations when required. It does not include a self-residue of a prime generator.
All contractions and comparisons are those of Theorem 5.10 and Lemma 6.3.

Lemma 7.1 (Minimum seeds).             1. In an all-split alphabet, suppose a unit symbol has a
       nonempty witness. At the least positive vertex count r of such a witness, all mutual
       edge bits are immaterial. The symbol is determined by the individual fixed-support
       labels. This remains true if the empty configuration is a unit, provided no contraction
       to the empty configuration is used.
    2. In the quadratic case, suppose the symbol has a nonzero witness, the alphabet is closed
       under the allowed contractions, and it contains the good twin operations. There are
       r ≥ 0 and s1 ≥ 1 such that for every s ≥ s1 of the permitted simple parity, the
       minimum split count of a unit with s simple vertices is r. At these counts all edge
       bits are immaterial. A chosen minimum seed may be padded by arbitrarily many good
       twin pairs.
     The assertions also hold for a stabilized moving odd symbol, with every particular finite
list of uses made at a sufficiently advanced stage.

Proof. For the first assertion, a derivative in an edge of an r-vertex graph is a symbol on
r − 1 vertices. If r ≥ 2, this graph remains nonempty and has value zero by minimality. If
r = 1, there is no edge. The omitted-vertex description then leaves exactly the fixed-support
labels.
    For the second assertion let r(s) be the minimum split count at simple count s, with
value +∞ when no witness exists. Once a finite value occurs, good-twin insertion gives
r(s + 2) ≤ r(s). A nonincreasing sequence of nonnegative integers stabilizes. Choose s1 ≥ 1
in that range. A derivative of an edge with a split endpoint reduces the split count and
preserves the simple count; it is therefore zero at the minimum. A simple–simple derivative
is zero by the symbol rule. Twin insertion gives the last assertion.
    For the moving system, take the minimum and the nonincreasing sequence in the
stabilized profile. Each comparison just used involves bounded vertex counts. A failure
on an ultrafilter-large set of stages at any one such bound would produce a bounded-
weight sequence contradicting that minimum. Thus any fixed finite collection holds on an
ultrafilter-large intersection of stages, from which an arbitrarily high stage may be chosen.
No assertion about every sufficiently high stage, or about all unbounded counts at one
stage, is needed.

    Recall that a list is partitionable when a permitted contraction forest has a unit symbol
as its output. Its terminal polynomial is then nonzero, since the indicated iterated finite
difference is nonzero.

Lemma 7.2 (Finite label reserves). Fix a minimum seed and a finite label alphabet.
   1. In the all-split rational alphabet, a list with the same total label as the seed and
      sufficiently many copies of its labels is partitionable.
   2. In the quadratic alphabet, retain r split anchors with the seed labels. Suppose there
      are sufficiently many simple vertices of the required parity and sufficiently many split
      reserves of each realizable rational shift. If F ̸= F ′ , also suppose

                        ∆ = #{p : p split in F and inert in F ′ }
                                                                                          (7.5)
                              − #{p : p inert in F and split in F ′ } > C0 .

                                              47
      Here C0 depends only on the anchors and the reserves. Then all other split vertices
      can be attached to simple vertices so that the result is the minimum seed and good
      twin pairs. The twins may match a prescribed finite enrichment of the rational labels.
      The base simple labels may be specified on any projection on which the required total
      agrees.
All reserve requirements depend only on the finite alphabets, seed, and projection. They do
not depend on the original list length.

Proof. In the first case retain the first r − 1 seed labels and merge all other vertices to the
last cluster. Sufficient copies ensure that this cluster is nonempty. Its label is forced by the
total, hence is the last seed label. Lemma 7.1 makes its unknown edges immaterial.
    For the second case, the rational labels of split attachments form the subgroup with
trivial F -coordinate. They act transitively on the simple coset, so every prescribed change
of a simple label has a realizable split shift. Reserve sufficiently many representatives of
every such shift. Remove these reserves and the anchors from the donor pool. If F ̸= F ′ ,
(7.5), with C0 exceeding the number removed, leaves a distinct donor for each bad simple
vertex. Attach these donors; every simple vertex is now good. If F = F ′ , this operation
was unnecessary.
    Pair equal enriched good labels. By withholding a bounded number of pairs, leave
between s1 and s1 + C1 unpaired vertices, with the correct parity; C1 depends only on the
number of enriched labels. Use reserved shifts to turn the excess unpaired vertices into
good pairs and to set the first s1 − 1 base positions to the desired seed labels. The seed
labels themselves need not all be good. Attach every remaining nonanchor split vertex to
the last base position. Its projected label is forced by the total, the anchors, the other base
positions, and the twin pairs. It is the required last seed label. Each cluster contains one
simple vertex, so every contraction is permitted. Only a bounded number of reserve shifts
was used.
    When incidences to a retained split anchor are needed only to remove twins, they
can be prescribed after the contracted labels are fixed, by the independent terminal-edge
realization. Fixed stationary vertices instead enter the finite label enrichment from the
outset. These two uses do not increase the number of label types with the original list
length.

Lemma 7.3 (Neutral packets). For any of the finite reserve problems above with prescribed
rational total zero, one can choose a finite packet of terminal labels with rational total zero
such that every nonempty union of copies of the packet has the required supplies. In the
quadratic case with F ̸= F ′ , its donor surplus may be made arbitrarily large. Compatible
fixed splitting conditions may be included whenever the needed individual labels and shifts
remain realizable.

Proof. Choose ample copies of every required label, including anchors and shifts. When
the quadratic fields F, F ′ are distinct, add enough donors to exceed all the losses from
the other labels and the prescribed reserve margin. When F = F ′ , every simple vertex is
already good. Duplicate every full label in the resulting list. Duplication gives total zero
in every rational elementary coordinate and preserves any strict donor surplus. Choose the
margins so that after reserving the finitely many anchors in any one packet, the union of
any positive number of packets still meets the reserve requirements. All labels are realized
by distinct fresh primes; duplication never means repeating an actual prime factor.




                                              48
7.2   The even converse and its uniform estimate
Proposition 7.4 (Even original base). If c2 (E) = 0, then a(E) = 0.
Proof. Parity gives even functional sign. Fix an even symbol on the filter of 1, including
all auxiliary class-group tests. If its value at 1 is a unit, analytic nonvanishing is already
detected. Otherwise use a nonempty minimum seed in the full-torsion case, or a stabilized
quadratic seed in the other case.
      For each b, assign a neutral packet from Lemma 7.3 to every nonzero linear form on
Fb2 , and activate its primes by that form. At every nonzero address at least one packet is
active; their union satisfies the reserve problem and has a nonzero terminal polynomial. At
zero no terminal is active. The single-symbol case of Theorem 6.6 adds primary auxiliaries
and makes the symbol a unit at every nonzero address. All local classes remain those of 1.
Equation (7.1) gives the uniform bound at all nonzero vertices. Theorem 3.1, applied at
the original corank-zero base, now gives a(E) = 0.

Proposition 7.5 (Partitionable even estimate). Fix an even unit symbol on a prescribed
filter. For every partitionable parameter h on its filter with c2 (E (h) ) = 0,

                        L(h) ̸= 0,     v(L(h)) − 2w(h) − s(h) ≤ C.                      (7.6)

The constant depends only on the symbol and fixed local data.
Proof. If the parameter has no prime factors, the only contraction is the identity, so
partitionability means that the empty symbol itself is a unit. Equation (7.1) then proves
the assertion directly. Otherwise the stationary terminal set is nonempty. Keep the prime
factors of h stationary. The iterated edge difference along the defining contraction forest is
1, so their terminal polynomial is nonzero. The stationary one-symbol case of Theorem 6.6
gives arbitrarily large binary cubes based at h, with only primary new primes, whose other
addresses have unit symbol. Equation (7.1) and s(hx ) ≥ 0 give the uniform upper bound
required by the cyclotomic transfer. The base corank is zero, so Theorem 3.1 gives (7.6).
A base-dependent dimension threshold does not affect the asserted constant.

7.3   The lower bound needed for odd coefficients
The even estimate also supplies the arithmetic hypothesis of Proposition 4.2. For an already
analytic-rank-one parameter h, we choose a companion k ′ = k ′ (h) with boundedly many
prime factors and estimate the central value at hk ′ . Comparing the ring and genus Heegner
sums then bounds j(Ph ) from below. The companion in this argument may vary with h;
the odd coefficient system has not yet been normalized.
    To compare the two fixed isogenous curves E, E ′ , add subscripts to s, w, L. At a
parameter h already known to have analytic rank zero, both twists have finite Tate–
Shafarevich groups by the forward theorem. Cassels’s isogeny formula for the arithmetic
BSD quotient [16], as explained in Tate [72, Theorem 2.1 and the following discussion],
gives
                     sE (h) + 2wE (h) = sE ′ (h) + 2wE ′ (h) + OE,E ′ (1).              (7.7)
Indeed, at an active odd good prime the component-group valuation is tp for the corre-
sponding curve, so the active Tamagawa valuations sum to twice its weight. At the fixed
support only finitely many local twisting classes occur. Differential, period, and torsion
ratios are bounded by the fixed isogeny and its reverse, and the rank-zero regulator is 1.
These account for the bounded error in (7.7). The isogeny identity applies to the known
finite groups and does not assume the BSD value formula.

                                             49
    The two twists have the same L-function. Their fixed normalizing periods therefore
give v(LE (h)) − v(LE ′ (h)) = OE,E ′ (1), uniformly in h. Combining this with (7.7) shows
that v(L(h)) − 2w(h) − s(h) changes by a uniformly bounded amount. Consequently (7.6)
transfers in either direction with one constant, even when the choice between the two fixed
isogeny orientations depends on h.
Lemma 7.6 (A bounded companion for a varying odd parameter). Fix a positive odd-sign
filter and a negative genus partner h∗ with L(E (h∗ ) , 1) ̸= 0, chosen using Proposition 3.8
and then excluded from the variable parameters. There are constants A, C such that every
h on this filter of analytic order one admits a negative odd fundamental discriminant k ′
for which
                                                                       √
    1. (k ′ , 2N h) = 1, k ′ is a square at every place of 2N h, and Q( k ′ ) is nonexceptional;
    2. ω(k ′ ) ≤ A;
                 ′
    3. L(E (hk ) , 1) ̸= 0 and

                                v(L(hk ′ )) ≤ 2w(hk ′ ) + s(hk ′ ) + C.                    (7.8)

Proof. Choose the class-group support and all other fixed label tests before varying h.
Prepare an even negative-index symbol for each possible total type at this support, and
for both isogeny orientations if needed. There are finitely many such preparations. The
positive index |hk ′ | has nonzero total rational type: its negative-discriminant parity is
already nontrivial among the tests at 2. Thus in the full-torsion case a unit seed has
positive count. In the quadratic case use the stabilized seed of Lemma 7.1.
    When F ̸= F ′ , interchanging the isogeny orientation interchanges the two counts in
(7.5). Select the orientation with initial balance at least zero. The existing donors can then
pay for arbitrarily many existing bad simples. They do not have to be supplied by new
primes.
    Let R be the absolute prime-count bound in Proposition 3.8. Insert in k ′ a finite
dummy supply that meets the anchors, minimum simple count, and reserve requirements
for every one of the finitely many prepared symbols. When the quadratic fields F, F ′ are
distinct, add donor slack paying for every reserved loss, the new dummy bad simples, and a
further possible loss of R from the later rough factor. Add a fresh ramified prime if needed
to exclude the exceptional imaginary fields. The number of dummy primes is bounded
independently of h. Their labels and initial incidences may be chosen by Lemma 6.1.
    Absorb h and the dummy product into the curve. Apply Proposition 3.8 to complete k ′
by a squarefree factor coprime to all primes already used. Prescribe its sign and its local
unit classes so that the final k ′ is an odd negative fundamental discriminant, is a square
at 2N h, and has the required original local classes at 2N . These conditions are reduced
congruences at the level of the absorbed curve. Additional fixed primes may be excluded.
The lemma makes the central value at hk ′ nonzero with at most R new factors. Its size
threshold may depend on h and all these congruences; R does not.
    No particular resulting unit class at the auxiliary class-group support was assumed
in advance: dummy supplies were prepared for every possible total there. For the actual
total, Lemma 7.2 makes hk ′ partitionable. Analytic nonvanishing gives Selmer corank zero
by the forward theorem. Proposition 7.5 therefore gives (7.8), with the isogeny comparison
above if the other orientation was used. The finite preparation and the bound on dummy
and rough factors give A, C independent of h.
                                                                                          √
    Proposition 3.2 compares the genus sum Ph with the ring-class sum over K = Q( k):

                             2jh (k) − 2j(Ph ) = v(L(hk)) + O(1)                           (7.9)

                                              50
whenever the two twists have analytic orders one and zero. The error is uniform in h for
fixed k, and uniform in h, k when ω(k) is bounded and the prescribed local classes are
retained. Thus the same comparison applies to the bounded companions just constructed.

Proposition 7.7 (Uniform lower bound for the odd detector). Every analytically rank-one
h in the fixed positive filter satisfies

                                 2j(Ph ) ≥ 2w(h) + s(h) − C.                             (7.10)

In particular j(Ph ) ≥ w(h) − C uniformly in h and its prime count.

Proof. Choose k ′ by Lemma 7.6. Combine (7.3), (7.9), and (7.8). Since the factors are
coprime, w(hk ′ ) = w(h) + w(k ′ ), and w(k ′ ) ≤ ω(k ′ ) ≤ A. Hence

                     2j(Ph ) ≥ 4w(h) + s(h) + s(hk ′ ) − v(L(hk ′ )) − C
                             ≥ 2w(h) + s(h) − 2w(k ′ ) − C
                             ≥ 2w(h) + s(h) − C ′ .

Only even symbols and independently nonvanishing companions were used. Thus this
proof precedes, and does not assume, the odd minimum-depth normalization.

7.4   A fixed companion for the odd converse
Suppose the functional sign is odd. By Proposition 7.7, the odd coefficient system can now
be normalized on the positive filter of 1. Fix its old support and genus partner h∗ . Its
moving detecting prime ℓ has not become an extra fixed support requirement.
    Choose a separate even symbol for negative discriminants square at 2N , excluding the
fixed odd support, the relevant torsion ramification, and the exceptional imaginary    √ fields.
                                                 (k)
Choose a unit k of that symbol. Then L(E , 1) ̸= 0. Fix this k and K = Q( k) for
the rest of the construction, before choosing any cube dimension. Its fresh ramification
makes it independent of the old fixed label extensions. At sufficiently advanced stages ℓ
avoids k. The intrinsic odd label tests continue to omit the primes of k. When the odd
symbol already detects the base, the converse follows directly. We henceforth consider the
remaining case, in which the odd symbol has the nonempty seed described above.
    For the moving odd source, fix its stabilized minimum depth and seed profiles before
constructing packets. A bounded-weight seed has boundedly many vertices here, since
every prime has weight at least 1/2. For each fixed field used in the labels, permitting
ramification above the one moving prime ℓ adds only boundedly many dimensions to its
elementary squareclass space. Identify these spaces compatibly with their fixed projections
and stabilize the finitely many seed and label profiles. The reserve bounds of Lemma 7.2
are uniform over these profiles, so the packet constructions below have a fixed size. They
are finite tables of labels; their prime representatives will be chosen at an actual coefficient
stage after the networks and their weight bounds have been specified.
    Our remaining task is to construct, for every binary dimension, a family in which the
odd test at h and an even test at hk are simultaneously units away from the zero address.
Every new prime will split in this single field K. For quadratic F , the good-twin rule
makes the unit at k a nonzero even test on additional prime configurations. With full
rational two-torsion, the restriction to K-split primes can instead leave only the empty
configuration at the original minimum depth. The next construction supplies a nonempty
witness in exactly that case; its extra division changes the valuation estimate by a fixed
constant.

                                              51
7.5    A nonempty witness with full rational two-torsion
Lemma 7.8 (Recurrence congruences). Let p be odd, a ≡ p + 1 (mod 4), and let

                         b0 = 1,   b1 = a − κ,      bs+1 = abs − pbs−1 .

If κ = ±1, then bs+1 − bs is even and

                         bs+1 − bs   a−κ−1
                                   ≡                 (mod 2)    (s ≥ 0).
                             2         2
If κ = 0, put b−1 = 0; then bs − bs−1 is odd for every s ≥ 0. Finally, for κ = 1, if a ≡ p + 1
(mod 2m ) then bs ≡ ps (mod 2m ) for every s ≥ 0.

Proof. Modulo 2 the recurrence is bs+1 = bs−1 . Its initial values prove the parity assertions.
For ds = bs+1 − bs , the recurrence for ds is the same. Directly, d1 − d0 ≡ (p − 1)(p − κ) ≡ 0
(mod 4) when κ = ±1. Thus ds /2 is constant modulo 2. The last assertion follows by
substituting the sequence ps in the recurrence and checking its first two terms.

Replace the trace by a fresh split prime p′ with the same finite data, including every

spaces together.

We verify integrality at every coefficient. Write a test index uniquely as Jrm2 , where

This cancellation can be checked without dividing by a leading multiplier, which may be
zero. Factor from both shell coefficients the common phase and the scalar 2−|U |+|J| a(U/J),
whose valuation is at least 1 by (7.13). The remaining factors are the odd differences at
U/J, the integral divided differences at J, and the ordinary square-index factors outside
U . By Lemma 7.8, their product is congruent modulo 2 to the product of their leading
values, even when one of those values is zero modulo 2. Since m is odd, multiplication by
m preserves this congruence. The common scalar supplies the second factor 2. Retaining
the phase η(m) therefore gives

                            BU [Jm2 ] ≡ BU [J]η(m)m       (mod 4).

This is exactly the cancellation with BU [J]ΘJ .

this lattice: when J ̸= U , BU [J] is even and the quadratic character difference supplies
another factor 2; when J = U , the characters agree exactly.
The new symbol and its witness. Multiply (7.15) by ordinary theta and use the integral trace
operators. Two fresh traces in GK give zero after reduction at every coefficient. To see this,
start with an arbitrary test index Jrm2 and choose the two fresh primes outside that index
and the level. The filter imposes χK (D) = 1; since U is all split, the number of inert factors
in r is either zero or at least two. The fresh traces replace r by rq1 q2 , with both qi split.
In the first case the valuation after division by 4 is at least ω(r) + 2 + 1 − 2 = ω(r) + 1 ≥ 1.

In the second case it is at least ω(r) + 2 − 2 = ω(r) ≥ 2. The unary terms disappear under
these fresh tests. Additional fresh GK traces only improve these estimates, so vanishing
holds on the entire reduced trace orbit. The trace identity therefore gives
                                 t(gu2 ) = t(g)        (g, u ∈ GK )
on this reduced trace orbit. Thus the tests factor through the elementary extension of K
unramified off the level.

Changing the oriented inertia bit changes cp once. The omitted-prime identity gives the

Consequently its repeated contraction has precisely the forest interpretation of Lemma 6.3.
An omitted-vertex label equal to the identity has trace zero, which is primary isolation.
No contraction to the empty configuration has occurred.
   It remains to prove that this deeper test has a witness. Fresh ramification gives

                     p ≡ 1 (mod 8),       ap (E (k) ) ≡ −2 (mod 8).
On H e ∅ , whose coefficient at 1 is zero, test t(u)2 at coefficient 1, where u = Frp . The square

The determinant term at coefficient 1 cancels. In characteristic two, t(u2 ) = t(u)2 , so
a single trace in GK is nonzero. A fresh prime realizing this finite trace test is split in
K. Moreover u2 is trivial on every rational quadratic character, so the prime lies on
the required total rational filter. It is a nonempty witness for (7.17). Finally the exact
coefficient ratio gives (7.11), with the additional depth at most one.
Lemma 7.10 (Orientation correction). Let K be imaginary quadratic, let S0 contain 2
and its ramification support, and let V0 be the elementary label space over K unramified
off S0 . Complex conjugation acts on V0 by c. If the rational total of an oriented prime
configuration is zero on every rational quadratic character unramified off S0 , then its
oriented total belongs to (1 + c)V0 . A reserved supply containing every label allows its
orientations to be flipped to realize any prescribed change in (1 + c)V0 .
Proof. Complex conjugation has order two outside GK , so GQ = GK ⋊ ⟨c⟩. A c-invariant
quadratic character of GK extends by giving c value zero. At a rational prime outside S0 ,
inertia lies in GK and the character is trivial there; thus its extension remains unramified
outside S0 . The assumed total therefore annihilates (V0∗ )c .
    For a finite vector space with involution, the annihilator of ker(1 + c∗ ) = (V0∗ )c is
im(1 + c). This proves the first assertion. Flipping a vertex of label v changes its oriented
label from v to cv, and hence changes the total by (1 + c)v. Reserve one representative for
every preimage needed to express the desired element of the image. Their independent
flips give the second assertion. These flips preserve every rational norm label.

                                                  55
7.6    Common packets and rank-one interpolation
Proposition 7.11 (Families with a fixed companion). With the fixed odd coefficient system
and companion k chosen in Section 7.4, suppose the odd symbol at 1 is zero. There is a
constant C with the following property. For every b ≥ 1 there are distinct primes outside
2N k and the fixed supports of both coefficient tests, with nonzero linear activations, such
that their signed products hx , x ∈ Fb2 , satisfy h0 = 1. Every√hx is positive and has the fixed
local classes of 1, and every new prime splits in K = Q( k). The primes also avoid the
detecting prime of the actual odd coefficient stage. In particular (hx , k) = 1 for every x.
For every x ̸= 0,
                            a(E (hx ) ) = 1,       L(E (hx k) , 1) ̸= 0,
and
                    j(Phx ) ≤ w(hx ) + C,       v(L(hx k)) ≤ 2w(hx ) + C.
The constant C and the discriminant k are independent of b.

Proof. We construct common terminal packets, individually split in K, such that the odd
terminal polynomial and an even terminal polynomial testing hk are separately nonzero at
each nonzero address.
    First suppose F, F ′ are quadratic. Keep the original even symbol evaluated on hk,
treating the vertices of k as stationary external data. Enrich the rational labels of the
h-vertices by the primes of k. Their total there and at the even support is required to
be zero. Let V be the resulting rational elementary label space, including the F, F ′ and
K coordinates and all relations among these characters. The allowed simple labels form
the affine set {v ∈ V : χF (v) = 1, χK (v) = 0}, in additive notation. The difference of
any two of its elements belongs to ker χF ∩ ker χK , precisely the realizable rational labels
of the permitted split attachments. Translation by this subgroup is therefore transitive.
The fields F, F ′ are unramified outside the old support, whereas K has a fresh ramified
prime; thus K is independent of their compositum. When F ̸= F ′ , this realizes both
the good-simple pattern (1, 1, 0) and the donor pattern (0, 1, 0) in their three coordinates.
When F = F ′ , all simple labels are good.
    For the old odd projection, take a normal closure of its finite label extension, including
the moving prime ℓ at any stage realizing the stabilized profile. It is unramified outside
the old support and ℓ. Once ℓ avoids k, a prime ramified in K remains outside that set.
Hence K has trivial intersection with this normal closure, and every old odd label can be
realized together with splitting in K. This applies also to oriented split anchors over F .
The added stationary k coordinates may have relations, but they are already included in
V ; they do not impose extra requirements on the omitted odd projection.
    Use packets with the required simple supply and reserves in this kernel, and with
sufficiently large donor slack when F ̸= F ′ . For the even test, take no split anchors. Correct
all bad simples to good ones, pair equal enriched labels, and retain a nonempty good
base pair. Use reserves to match the pair and the remaining good pairs, and attach every
leftover split vertex to the last base position. Its label is forced by total zero. The output
is a collection of good twin pairs, each matching also at every split vertex of k. Twin
insertion into the unit at k proves that this output has even symbol one. Thus the even
terminal polynomial is nonzero.
    For the odd test, project away the newly added tests at k. The same packets contain
enough split anchors, simple vertices and reserve shifts to contract to the stabilized odd
seed and good pairs, by Lemma 7.2. Its total on that projection is the prescribed zero total.
Incidences between the retained anchors and the good pairs can be set after contraction,


                                              56
by independent edge choices. The odd polynomial is therefore nonzero as well. This odd
test does not include the stationary vertices of k.
    Now suppose the curve has full rational two-torsion. Use the even test of Lemma 7.9,
with K-oriented labels on its support S0 , and let v1 , . . . , vr be a minimum nonempty
witness. Its oriented total v1 + · · · + vr lies in (1 + c)V0 , by Lemma 7.10. Choose packets
containing ample copies of every needed even label, all odd seed labels on their rational
projection, and orientation-shifting reserves. In the common compositum, restriction to
the even label extension is surjective, so each even label has a lift. Each intrinsic odd label
also has a lift fixing K: fresh ramification gives K ∩ Lodd = Q, and the moving odd prime
avoids k. Extending these elements to the common compositum gives the required lifts.
Take sufficiently many lifts for each projection separately, without prescribing their other
projection. Duplicate each full label, so the packet has rational total zero.
    For the even contraction, retain anchors with labels v1 , . . . , vr−1 . After reserving these
                                                            P
anchors, use orientation flips to make the total equal to vi ; this is possible by Lemma 7.10.
Merge all remaining vertices into the last cluster. Its label is vr . The cluster is nonempty,
and the minimum nonempty count makes the output a unit independently of its mutual
edges. For the odd contraction, use its rational projection. Every odd seed label lifts to a
K-split prime because K was freshly ramified outside its intrinsic support. Retain all but
the last odd seed label and merge the remainder. Total zero gives the last label, so this is
also a unit. Orientation correction is conjugation over Q, which preserves every rational
quadratic label, including those at the moving odd prime. Adding k to the common support
has not added it to the intrinsic odd projection.
    In both cases put a sufficient common packet at every nonzero linear form on Fb2 . Each
nonzero address has two separately nonzero terminal polynomials. Theorem 6.6 supplies
primary auxiliaries split in K (and F in the quadratic case), neutral at the stationary primes
of k, and makes both symbols one at every nonzero address. The auxiliary generators are
primary at every support coordinate used by either projection. Thus the odd and even
graph identities remain identities on the same common terminal polynomial data. All
active products h are positive and square at the fixed tests, and all their primes split in K.
    For each fixed b, the terminal tables and networks now give a finite list of coefficient
tests and a maximum weight. Choose the working precision after these bounds are fixed.
Finer ray neighborhoods affect prime realization but do not enlarge the elementary label
alphabet. Lemma 4.3 and Theorem 5.10 realize the required divisibilities and symbol
identities on an ultrafilter-large intersection of stages. At an arbitrarily high actual stage
in that intersection, realize the finite graphs by distinct primes. The unit-depth constants
and the companion k were fixed before b was chosen, and therefore remain uniform in these
realizations.
    At each nonzero address the odd unit detects analytic rank one and gives j(Ph ) ≤
w(h) + C. The even unit detects L(E (hk) , 1) ̸= 0 and gives v(L(hk)) ≤ 2w(hk) + C, or the
bound (7.11) in the full-torsion case. The fixed quantity 2w(k) is absorbed into C. These
are the asserted estimates.

Proposition 7.12 (Odd original base). If c2 (E) = 1, then a(E) = 1.

Proof. Parity gives odd functional sign. Proposition 7.7 therefore permits the odd nor-
malization used above. If its symbol at 1 is a unit, it already gives a(E) = 1. Otherwise
apply Proposition 7.11 for arbitrarily large b. At every nonzero address its estimates and

                                     2jh (k) ≤ 4w(h) + C.


                                               57
The finite Sha lengths there are nonnegative. Hence 2jh (k) − 4w(h) − s(h) − s(hk) ≤ C,
as required for Heegner interpolation.
    At address zero, L(E (k) , 1) ̸= 0 gives c2 (E (k) ) = 0 by Lemma 2.1. Lemma 2.2 gives

                       corankZ2 Sel2∞ (E/K) = c2 (E) + c2 (E (k) ) = 1.

Since K is fixed, the finite quotient of this base Selmer group has a fixed length. Theorem 3.3
makes L(E, s)L(E (k) , s) have a simple zero. Its second factor is nonzero at 1, so a(E) =
1.

There is no restriction on the rational isogeny configuration, complex multiplication, or
reduction at 2 or at the bad primes.
Proof. The initial isogeny choice preserves both quantities and covers both rational two-

8     Selmer matrices and three-state relations
Assume that V = E[2] is irreducible, and write F = Q(V ) and G = Gal(F/Q) ∈ {C3 , S3 }.
The symbol rules will be useful because a unit symbol bounds the 2-Selmer dimension. We
now express local Selmer conditions by a binary matrix and make that bound constrain
the number of singular blocks. The second half of the section develops the elementary
matrix operations needed to apply this constraint.
   Write the Weil pairing additively, and choose a basis of V in which its matrix is
                                                       !
                                                 0 1
                                         J=          .
                                                 1 0

Fix a finite set S containing 2, 3, the bad primes, all auxiliary exclusions, and rational primes
whose prime ideals generate the class groups of every subfield of F . Local specifications at
S will also include the archimedean places. The detecting prime ℓ for an odd coefficient
system is kept separate: all generators below have valuation zero there. This separation
keeps the set of support types finite as ℓ varies. We shall apply the construction to a fixed
curve, after absorbing any initial quadratic twist into E.

8.1    Global classes and local equations
At a split prime p, choose a place of F and put Hp = 1. At a simple prime, choose a place
with Frobenius a specified transposition c and put Hp = ⟨c⟩. Set

                                  Fp′ = F Hp ,        Vp = V Hp .

Thus Vp has dimension two or one, respectively. The chosen place lies above a degree-one
prime Pp of Fp′ . By the choice of S, there is πp ∈ (Fp′ )× whose divisor away from S is Pp .
For x ∈ Vp define
                           Cp (x) = corFp′ /Q (χπp x) ∈ H 1 (Q, V ).                   (8.1)

                                                 58
Here χπp is the quadratic Kummer character; its product with x is a cocycle because Hp
fixes x.
     The inertia coordinate of Cp (x) at p is x, and all its other inertia coordinates outside S
vanish. Indeed, local restriction–corestriction has exactly one ramified summand, at the
degree-one place where πp has valuation one. These coordinates therefore make the classes
for distinct p independent. A root prime has V Frp = 0 and contributes no coordinates.
     Let h be a twist containing the active nonroot primes in its discriminant. At a good
odd prime p ramified in h, inertia identifies the local Kummer condition with Vp . To
see surjectivity, halve a Frobenius-fixed two-torsion point over the maximal unramified
extension. The ramified twisting involution negates its half, so the resulting Kummer
inertia is that two-torsion point. The Kummer condition has dimension dim Vp by local
duality, giving the asserted isomorphism.
     Fix arithmetic Frobenius lifts ϕp . A second local coordinate is unramified evaluation at
ϕp , modulo Frobenius coboundaries. Let Kp (x)(ϕp ) be this coordinate for the Kummer lift
of inertia x. It is determined by E[4] and the local twisting squareclass. Define the block
matrix B by

          Bqp (y, x) = ⟨Cp (x)(ϕq ), y⟩ − 1p=q ⟨Kp (x)(ϕp ), y⟩,   x ∈ Vp ,   y ∈ Vq .     (8.2)
                                                                                     P
Thus Bx = 0 is exactly the collection of active-prime Selmer equations for p Cp (xp ).
Changing the unramified unit part of the twist adds J to a split diagonal block. On a
simple line it changes nothing, since the Weil pairing restricts to zero on a one-dimensional
space.
    Record in the type hp the subgroup Hp and the squareclasses of πp at the places over
S, including signatures. Only finitely many types occur. Finer generator neighborhoods
will be used to reproduce self entries, but they are not part of this type alphabet and do
not enter its codimension cost. Global reciprocity gives
                                           t
                                    Bqp + Bpq = b(hq , hp ),                               (8.3)

where b is the sum of the local cup pairings at S. Indeed, at an active prime decompose
each class into its Kummer lift and its unramified difference. Both planes are isotropic,
and the cross pairing is unramified evaluation paired with inertia. The terms at p and
q give the left side; the sum over all places is zero. Elsewhere outside S the classes are
unramified, so the local pairing vanishes. This includes ℓ, where the generators have unit
valuation. The same calculation shows that b(h, h) is alternating.

8.2   Prescribing the matrix entries
We next prove that the off-diagonal entries and the rational Legendre bits can be varied
independently. The latter bits will control split diagonals without disturbing the other
blocks. The class-field inputs are ray-class reciprocity, the Hilbert product formula, and
Chebotarev [53, Chapter VI, Sections 7–8, and Chapter VII, Section 13]; see also Milne
[48, Chapter V, Theorems 3.23, 5.3 and 5.5, and Example 5.4].

Lemma 8.1 (Realization of finite matrix prescriptions). Fix finitely many individually
admissible oriented prime templates, including generator neighborhoods at S, any additional
finite places, and oriented Frobenius on E[4] and fixed quadratic-radical extensions unrami-
fied outside that support. At arbitrarily fresh primes these templates admit the following
simultaneous prescriptions.



                                               59
   (i) Between distinct vertices, one direction of the double-coset residue bits is free and
       reciprocity determines the reverse direction. The available matrix blocks are all binary
       matrices for two split vertices in the S3 case, and JF4 in the C3 case, with the rotation
       algebra identified with F4 . For split–simple vertices the full vector space is available,
       and for simple–simple vertices the scalar entry is available.
  (ii) The rational Legendre bit between the absolute primes is independent of the matrix
       block, subject to rational reciprocity in the reverse direction.
 (iii) Repeating a template fixes its simple self entry and its split self block modulo a scalar
       multiple of J. A sufficiently primary split template with trivial E[4]-Frobenius has
       self block in F2 J.
Here primary means sufficiently close to 1 at the finite places of S and totally positive at
the real places. New cross-bit prescriptions are made outside the support. All assertions
concern finite configurations; an additional moving prime ℓ may be included among the
prescribed places.

Proof. Off-diagonal entries. Restriction–corestriction in Equation (8.1) gives, for distinct
vertices,                                  X
                           Bqp (y, x) =           epq (g)⟨gx, y⟩.                      (8.4)
                                          g∈Hq \G/Hp
                                                                                     −1
The bit epq (g) is the quadratic residue of gπp at the oriented q-place in F Hq ∩gHp g . Indeed,
pair local transfer with the Hq -invariant vector y; the orbit length [Hq : Hq ∩ gHp g −1 ] is
precisely the residue degree in the norm calculation. In this intermediate field, each of πq
and gπp has its off-support divisor at a single prime: its oriented Frobenius generates the
subgroup fixing the generator. Hilbert reciprocity therefore relates epq (g) to eqp (g −1 ), with
correction only at S.
    For a new vertex the independent forward bits are residue conditions at distinct primes
of its own field Fp′ . Move an initial template away from the old primes and choose a degree-
one prime in the ray class whose quotient generator has the prescribed neighborhoods
at S, real signs, and nonzero residues at those old primes. Multiplying the template
generator by this quotient gives the required new generator. These conditions also preserve
the admissible fixed Frobenius data: changes by local units at old primes are trivial on
the intersection with extensions unramified outside the fixed support. Chebotarev thus
imposes the ray and fixed-label conditions simultaneously. Prime ideals of degree greater
than one over Q have density zero among prime ideals of Fp′ , so requiring degree one loses
no admissible class. The same argument permits additional finite support, including ℓ.
Reverse bits are then determined by reciprocity. Rational norm bits at already chosen root
primes may be imposed as additional residue conditions in the ray modulus: finite-field
norm maps are surjective, and these primes contribute no matrix coordinates.
    Matrix span and the norm bit. The norm to Q writes the rational absolute-prime
Legendre bit as the sum of the double-coset bits, plus a fixed support-unit correction. For
two split vertices the matrices in Equation (8.4) are Jg. They span Mat2 (F2 ) for S3 and
JF4 for C3 . A nontrivial rotation satisfies 1 + R + R2 = 0: changing these three bits fixes
the matrix and changes their sum. For split–simple vertices, the three nonzero vectors or
dual vectors likewise span the full space and have the same odd relation. For two simple
vertices, the matching double coset has zero pairing and the other has nonzero pairing.
The first bit changes the norm bit alone. This proves (i) and (ii).
    Self entries. The nonidentity double cosets in self-transfer are unramified at the oriented
prime. The identity term is a scalar times the pairing and is therefore zero on a simple
line. At a split vertex the two rotation terms have fixed sum by reciprocity; changing

                                               60
both alters the block only by a scalar multiple of J. Every other term has an involution
representative τ exchanging the oriented place with another one. For a simple vertex this
includes a transposition other than c representing the nonmatching self double coset.
    Work in F τ and put

8.3    Symmetrization and the Selmer bound
The realization lemma supplies the allowable matrix blocks. We now remove their fixed-
support reciprocity defect and compare the resulting kernels with actual Selmer classes. The
fixed support types, stationary environment and additional local conditions will account
for the loss in dimension.
    Choose a matrix rule L(h, h′ ) in the available block spans such that

                                 L(h, h′ ) + L(h′ , h)t = b(h, h′ ).                     (8.6)

For distinct types, choose either direction using Equation (8.3). For equal types, the right
side is alternating. It is a matrix plus its transpose in the full span; in the rotation span
use R + R−1 = 1 after multiplication by J. On a simple line it is zero. Choose L = 0 when
one type is primary and trivial at S, since then its support pairings vanish. Define, on
every pair including the diagonal,

                                     Mqp = Bqp − L(hq , hp ).

The matrix M is symmetric. Lemma 8.1 allows zero cross-M entries between prescribed
blocks independently of their internal entries.
    A stationary environment consists of the fixed coordinates outside the variable blocks;
its dimension is the number of those coordinates.



                                                 61
Proposition 8.2 (Nullity forces Selmer dimension). Fix the curve, support types, a bound

entries, and denote their matrices by Mi . There is a constant depending only on these fixed
data such that                                  X

                                                             i
At a unit coefficient symbol the sum of nullities, and hence the number of singular blocks, is
therefore uniformly bounded. The bound works for both coefficient sources and is independent
of the moving prime ℓ.

Thus Bx = 0 at all block positions. Set the environment coordinates to zero and impose its

    At a unit symbol, Proposition 4.4 bounds the Selmer dimension, giving the remaining
