bounded independently of the growing support. This proves (12.11).
    If the central value is zero, reciprocity makes the dual exponential zero. The rational
ordinary specialization then satisfies the finite condition at 2, and it satisfies the finite
conditions elsewhere automatically. It lies in the rational finite Selmer group, which is
zero. Its wedge with the real-place class is therefore zero. The constant-rank determinant
specialization proves ux (0) = 0.



                                                 92
12.5    Residual freeness and the lower bound
The central formula expresses D(h) through the determinant valuation. When the residual
representation is an extension of trivial modules, we can bound that valuation directly: the
positive complex becomes a free rank-two module in degree one after extension to O[G].
The fixed-lattice class then supplies the lower bound without any loss from a growing
ramification support.

Lemma 12.4 (Residual concentration). If E[2] is an extension of two trivial F2 -modules,
then there is an isomorphism in the derived category

                               CS+ (T) ⊗R O[G] ≃ O[G]2 [−1].

Proof. The ring O[G] is local, with residue field Ω obtained by setting 2 = 0 and every g = 1.
Thus the quadratic characters disappear. Since E[2] has a filtration with trivial factors, it
is enough first to compute the positive residual complex for the trivial representation.
    Let Kn be the real cyclotomic layer of degree dn = 2n . The number of finite primes of
Kn above a fixed S is bounded with n: at 2 there is total ramification, and the Frobenius
of each fixed odd prime has nonzero image in Γ. The 2-ranks of the class groups of Kn
are also bounded. Let X be the inverse limit, under norm maps, of their 2-primary class
groups. The unique prime above 2 is totally ramified in this tower, so class-field-theoretic
control gives
                                     XΓ2n ≃ Cl(Kn )[2∞ ]
[63, Proposition 3.2.11 and Remark 3.2.12]. Ferrero–Washington gives µ(X) = 0 for this
abelian tower [28]. The Iwasawa structure theorem then makes X finitely generated over
Z2 , so all its coinvariants have uniformly bounded 2-rank. Passing to the quotient S-class
groups preserves this upper bound.
    The signature map of units in Kn is surjective. For n ≥ 1, let ζ be a primitive 2n+2 th
root and put ϵ = 1 + ζ + ζ −1 . If f1 (X) = X 2 − 2 and fn+1 (X) = fn (X 2 − 2), then fn is
the minimal polynomial of ζ + ζ −1 and fn (−1) = −1. Hence NKn /Q (ϵ) = −1. Its signature
vector has odd augmentation. The embeddings form a regular set for the cyclic 2-group
Gal(Kn /Q), whose group algebra over F2 is local. A vector with odd augmentation is a
unit in this group algebra, so its translates span every signature. For n = 0 the assertion
follows from the unit −1.
    The signature calculation supplies the real-place surjectivity needed for the positive
complex. The class-group and support terms are bounded along the tower. We can
therefore identify the one cohomological degree whose dimension grows with the layer
degree, and then recover the rank after inverting t.
     Kummer theory now determines the cohomological growth. Writing sn for the number
of finite support places and cn for the 2-rank of the S-class group, one has

                            dim H 1 (GKn ,S , µ2 ) = dn + sn + cn .

The real degree-one restriction is surjective by the signature result. In degree two, the
Kummer sequence has class-group term of dimension cn and Brauer term. The Brauer
invariants at the real and finite support places have their single sum relation. Since a
finite support place above 2 is present, restriction of the Brauer term to the real places is
surjective and has kernel of dimension sn − 1. Thus the degree-two kernel of restriction
to infinity is bounded. Restriction is an isomorphism in higher degrees by global duality.
The degree-zero restriction is injective. The cohomology of the positive fiber is therefore
bounded in every degree except one; its degree-one dimension is dn + OS (1).

                                              93
    Apply Lemma 12.1 modulo 2. Derived specialization to the nth layer is specialization
           n
modulo t2 . For a perfect complex over F2 [[t]], the universal-coefficient exact sequence
shows that a rank-r cohomology module contributes r2n + O(1) to these dimensions; the
torsion terms contribute only O(1). It follows that after inverting t the trivial-constituent
fiber has dimension one in degree one and vanishes in every other degree. Extensions
preserve this concentration, so the fiber for E[2] has dimension two in degree one. A
minimal finite free complex over the local ring O[G] has zero residual differentials. The
concentration just proved forces all its terms except the rank-two term in degree one to
vanish. No denominator is introduced by this exact freeness statement.

Proof of Theorem 3.5. Apply Lemma 12.4 to the single discriminant h. The fixed-lattice
construction and the free complex give 2Cint uh ∈ O with Cint depending only on E.
Equation (12.10) also gives uh ∈ Λ[1/2]. Since O ∩ Λ[1/2] = Λ, it follows that v(uh (0)) ≥
−Cint . Analytic rank zero supplies Selmer corank zero by the forward theorem of Gross–
Zagier–Kolyvagin. Proposition 12.3 now gives D(h) ≥ −Cint − Cloc . All constants come
from the fixed form, the fixed real period line, or the finitely many local twisting classes at
2N .

12.6    Transfer to the base
It remains to prove the missing-vertex theorem. Residual freeness gives a single integral
family in the reducible case. In the irreducible case we instead need a bounded clearing
factor after eliminating the new local blocks. The bound on v2 (q 2 − 1) enters precisely
here: the next lemma controls the negative Laurent powers in those inverse blocks at each
fixed precision.

Lemma 12.5. Suppose that q is an added prime and v2 (q 2 − 1) ≤ L. At augmentation
G → 1, the inverse singular Frobenius block contributed by q has, modulo each fixed 2M ,
negative Laurent support bounded in terms of L and M . This bound is independent of q.

Proof. Identify Γ with 1 + 4Z2 using a fixed generator. If γq = (1 + t)αq is the image of
arithmetic Frobenius at q, then

                                     v2 (αq ) = v2 (q 2 − 1) − 3

when the generator is 5; changing the generator multiplies αq by a unit. In particular
αq ̸= 0. The singular quotient is represented by a block of bounded size whose determinant
is the Euler polynomial in Equation (12.7). Modulo 2, with z = (1 + t)αq , it is

                                           1 + aq z + z 2 .

If aq = 1, its constant coefficient is 1. If aq = 0, it is (1 + z)2 , whose t-order is 2v2 (αq )+1 ≤
2L−2 . Thus the inverse determinant has uniformly bounded pole order modulo 2. Lift its
inverse modulo 2M by a geometric expansion of length at most M . Each term contains
only a bounded number of inverse residual factors, and all numerators have nonnegative
powers of t. The adjugate formula gives the same bound for every entry of the inverse
block.

Proof of Theorem 3.1. At every nonzero vertex, analytic rank zero gives c2 (E (hx ) ) = 0 by
the forward rank-zero theorem. Thus Proposition 12.3 applies at every vertex.
   First assume E[2] is an extension of trivial modules. Lemma 12.4 and the fixed-lattice
construction give 2Cint u ∈ O[G]. Moreover, the character coordinates of u are in Λ[1/2].


                                                 94
Fourier inversion, performed rationally, shows u ∈ Λ[1/2][G]; intersecting coefficientwise
with O[G] gives
                                     2Cint u ∈ Λ[G].                                (12.13)
The coefficients at t = 0 therefore obey the bounded-degree Boolean congruences of
Lemma 11.4. For any fixed precision and sufficiently large b, there is a nonzero x for which
ux (0) agrees with u0 (0) to that precision. The fixed multiplier in Equation (12.13) affects
the required precision but has no dependence on b.
    Now suppose E[2] is irreducible. At augmentation, represent the positive complex as
the old complex, with ramification set excluding Q, extended by the direct sum of singular
blocks at the new primes. The differential is upper triangular with the old complex as
subcomplex. The finite-model argument of Lemma 11.6 lifts this model over R: stabilize
the finite free models and lift their graded changes of basis. These lifts are invertible
because the augmentation ideal lies in the Jacobson radical. Cancel all additional lifted
unit disks before retaining the old-plus-singular grading; their augmented cross terms
are zero, so the remaining augmentation is exactly the prescribed block model. Thus no
stabilization disk enlarges the old complex. Lemma 11.2 then applies to this block model.
All the new blocks are invertible over O[G]: modulo its maximal ideal their Frobenius
determinants are nonzero in Ω. Schur elimination leaves a complex of bounded size. Its
augmentation is the fixed old complex, and its entries have bounded negative Laurent
support modulo each fixed 2M . Under the determinant-line identification, write

                                u = ηubd ,        η ∈ O[G]× ,

where ubd is the coordinate on this bounded complex and η is the determinant factor of
the eliminated blocks. No uniform Laurent bound on η is needed.
    The pole bound remains uniform as primes are added. At augmentation the inverse new
block is a direct sum of the inverses in Lemma 12.5. At any character its perturbation has
entries divisible by 2 and no negative powers of t. Modulo 2M , the inverse uses a geometric
series of length at most M , independently of the number of blocks. Each product therefore
uses a bounded number of inverse entries. Sums of an arbitrary number of such terms
cannot decrease the nonarchimedean valuation or the common lower Laurent bound. This
is the required bound for Schur elimination.
    At t = 0 the old positive complex has rational cohomology only in degree one, of
dimension two, by Proposition 12.3 at h0 . Its finite torsion and all old local data are
fixed. Choose boundary pivots there, leaving those two free coordinates. Their sizes and
valuations may depend on h0 but not on b. Apply the determinant clearing argument
of Lemma 11.3 to the wedge of the two bounded-integral cycles a, 2zS on the remaining
complex. Its allowance for an arbitrary factor η puts the eliminated-block factor into the
numerator alone. It yields

                 d = f u ∈ O[G],      f ∈ O[G],        f0 ∈ Λ,   f0 (0) ̸= 0.        (12.14)

For each fixed M , the negative Laurent support of fx mod 2M is bounded independently of b
and x. The clearing factor is a polynomial of bounded degree in the remaining bounded-size
matrix, with the pivot denominators cleared by adjugates. Neither the eliminated-block
unit, the zeta class nor d is required to have a uniform bound on its negative Laurent
support.
    Equation (12.10) ensures that every ux has no negative t-powers. Thus all hypotheses
of Lemma 11.5 hold for Equation (12.14). In particular, for each fixed precision and
sufficiently large b, some nonzero x satisfies the corresponding central congruence between

                                             95
ux (0) and u0 (0). The dependence on h0 enters the required precision and dimension,
through v(f0 (0)), and not the eventual valuation comparison.
    In either residual case let C bound the absolute error in Equation (12.11). The nonzero
vertices have v(ux (0)) ≤ B + C. If L(E (h0 ) , 1) = 0, the central coordinate at zero is zero,
and a congruence of precision larger than B + C is impossible. Hence the central value is
nonzero. If D(h0 ) > B + 2C, then v(u0 (0)) > B + C, giving the same contradiction. This
proves Equation (3.5) with CE = 2C. Only the specialization error determines this final
loss; all pivot and pole bounds determine how large a cube is needed.


13     Heegner points and transfer
We prove the Heegner comparison, the genus and ring bounds, and the interpolation criterion
stated in Section 3. The point and its paired functional define one determinant coordinate.
Its central value measures the point index after the Selmer and local contributions have
been subtracted. Local switches then control its divisors. An outer limit supplies the
uniform ring bound when residual irreducibility is unavailable; a universal binary family
supplies interpolation.

13.1    Height and base Selmer comparisons
Retain the genus and ring data, character sums Pc (e), and lattice indices of Section 3. We
first justify the two comparisons used there.

Proof of Proposition 3.2. The explicit Gross–Zagier formula for ring classes [14, Theo-
rem 1.1, pp. 2524–2525] applies. Automorphic induction of the quadratic character gives
the product in Equation (3.6). Discriminant and conductor are prime to N , and the
classical split Heegner hypothesis holds; consequently the shared-prime corrections in the
general formula are absent. The unit-index convention is constant in the fields      p retained
here. With a character sum, solving the formula for the height gives exactly c |k|, as in
Equation (3.6). The forward Gross–Zagier–Kolyvagin theorem gives the rank and finiteness
assertion [31, 40].
    For Equation (3.7), both free points are compared with the free line on E (h) (Q). Its
regulator cancels from the ratio of their heights, since absolute canonical height is unchanged
by twist identification. This height invariance follows from the canonical-height limit and
the degree formula for heights under morphisms [49, Chapter        IV, Lemmas 4.1 and 4.5–4.6,
and Remark 4.12]. The remaining variable factor is |hk|L(E (hk) , 1); its infinity sign is
                                                           p

that of h∗ . Period and lattice changes have bounded 2-valuation. If the rank-one line is
instead that of the fixed curve E (h∗ ) , its regulator is fixed and the same calculation gives
Equation (3.8).

Proof of Lemma 3.4. Restriction, twisting, and addition or subtraction of conjugates give
maps in both directions between the Selmer group over K and

                            Sel2∞ (E (h0 ) /Q) ⊕ Sel2∞ (E (h0 k) /Q)

whose composites are multiplication by 2. They induce isogenies on divisible subgroups. On
the finite quotients the kernel of either induced map is killed by 2: subtract an element of
the divisible subgroup to lift a divisible image, and then use the composite. The generator
dimensions are uniformly bounded. Indeed the mod-2 Selmer group over K is bounded by
Kummer cohomology over K(E[2]); this is a quadratic extension of a fixed field with a
bounded number of ramified primes. Kummer theory bounds the generator number of the

                                              96
corresponding pro-2 group over the fixed field, and the index-two subgroup bound preserves
a uniform bound. The fixed local support includes that of h0 . The finite quotient on the
displayed product is bounded by hypothesis; therefore its counterpart over K has bounded
length as well. For a finite collection of fields, finiteness of each quotient is enough.

13.2     Integral models and ring-class deformation
The determinant argument uses characters of increasing conductor. We construct their
cochain limits together with maps to the original cochains. These maps will let us detect
limiting Selmer classes by Galois evaluation and choose local switches. Later we retain the
same maps through the outer limit needed for the ring bound.
     Write T = T2 E and Λ = Z2 [[t]]. For a quadratic squareclass h, put T h = T ⊗ χh . The
imaginary quadratic field K satisfies the split Heegner hypothesis at 2N . We omit the two
fields with extra units and, when E has complex multiplication, its CM field. A quadratic
character χ comes from a quadratic twist over Q. We use

                                   Ai = (Z/2i Z)[t]/(ti ).

The same constructions work over Ai [G] and Λ[G], where G is a fixed finite elementary
abelian 2-group and the additional character has values in G. Statements uniform in the
number of generators of G will be identified separately; finite-diagram compactness by
itself is a statement for fixed G.

13.2.1    Finite models with their cochain maps
The general finite-model construction is Lemma 11.6. We apply it to the following arithmetic
cochains and retain its actual contractions throughout.
    For a profinite group and a finite free coefficient module over an Artin local ring A,
continuous inhomogeneous cochains are flat: locally constant functions are the filtered union
of the modules of functions on finite clopen partitions, and these modules are finite free.
Their reduction is the cochain complex with reduced coefficients. The same observations
apply to restrictions, mapping cones, and local conditions given by finite free complexes.
In the applications below the groups are Galois groups of totally imaginary number fields
or nonarchimedean local fields. Their 2-cohomological dimension and the usual finiteness
theorems give the bounded residual cohomology required in Lemma 11.6.
    For an inner limit, the quadratic field K, the active conductor support, the finite group
G, and the number of derivative primes are fixed. The residual representation is E[2]: all
the scalar characters and group-like coefficients become trivial in the residue field. Put
F = K(E[2]). The number of places of F in the permitted set Si is bounded even when
its auxiliary primes move. Kummer theory bounds H 1 (GF,Si , F2 ) by the Si -units modulo
squares and Cl(F )[2]; their dimensions are bounded by the unit rank, #Si , and the fixed
class-group dimension. Hochschild–Serre for the fixed group Gal(F/K) then bounds the
residual global H 1 for E[2]. The global Euler characteristic bounds H 2 , and H 0 is already
bounded. Local cohomology and the specified local conditions have bounded residual
dimensions, since the local degrees of K are at most two and the number of places is
bounded. Thus the target Selmer cones over K have uniformly bounded finite-model ranks
in this inner limit. This bound may depend on the fixed active support; the stronger outer
bound is proved in Lemma 13.15. No rank bound is asserted for cochains over the growing
ring-class fields, or for the induced modules in their Shapiro descriptions.




                                             97
Lemma 13.1 (Limits of finite diagrams). Suppose diagrams of bounded finite free complexes
over Ai , or over Ai [G], have a common bounded cohomological degree interval and bounded
ranks in every degree. Include the chain homotopies and inverses for every asserted identity
or equivalence. Along a cofinal subsequence, or a nonprincipal ultrafilter, their matrices
converge to the same diagram of perfect complexes over Λ or Λ[G].
    Moreover, the contractions into the original cochains may be retained for the evaluation
construction in Lemma 13.7. Adding finitely many new objects or maps does not require
changing already chosen models for the old objects. Finite-stage arithmetic operations with
unbounded source complexes are permitted when only finitely many of their outputs, and
the witnesses to their identities, are retained in the bounded target complexes.

Proof. There are finitely many possible graded ranks. Fix them on an ultrafilter-large set.
At a fixed precision j, all matrix entries belong to the finite ring Aj (or Aj [G]), so have
unique ultrafilter limits. These limits are compatible as j varies. Matrix identities, including
the homotopy-inverse identities, are finite polynomial identities and pass to the limit. New
models and maps may be mapped to old models by their already fixed contractions. Passing
to any slower cofinal precision has the same old limits. For the all-sequence evaluation
statements below, fix this ultrafilter once and for all, and keep the original contraction at
every stage. Later arithmetic diagrams must extend these same stages. Restriction to an
ultrafilter-large set and passage to slower cofinal coefficient precision preserve every old
evaluation on every old group sequence. Thus later operations use the same stage diagrams
and ultrafilter, preserving the arithmetic maps already constructed.
    Here is the last assertion in cochain terms. Let Di be an original target cochain complex,
with contraction (ιi , πi , hi ) onto its bounded finite model Ci . Perform transfer and scalar
weighting at stage i before applying this contraction. A resulting cycle zi′ ∈ Di1 gives
zi = πi zi′ ∈ Ci1 , and
                                          zi′ − ιi zi = d(hi zi′ ).
Thus its relation to the original arithmetic cocycle is retained by the original contraction. If
an identity in the target is u′i −vi′ = dwi′ , retain the vectors πi u′i , πi vi′ , πi wi′ ; the corresponding
identity is still exact. For identities involving maps between target complexes, use the
transported maps πi′ fi ιi and their contraction homotopies, so that the same assertion
includes the required boundary corrections. Local lifts, closed degree-two functionals,
and witnesses to specialization, duality and switch identities are treated in the same way.
They are finitely many vectors or matrices of bounded size. Their entries, as well as the
differentials, therefore have simultaneous limits. Neither a free model of the arithmetic
source nor the entire transfer map from that source is included in the limiting diagram.
Any precision lost in a finite-stage calculation is accounted for before retaining these
vectors; passing to the same slower cofinal precision for the whole diagram preserves all its
identities.

    The finite models now retain both the original cochains and their comparison maps.
We next specify the local conditions on those cochains. The point is to retain the actual
Kummer images at every place dividing 2N ; the determinant comparisons will use their
integral orthogonality, not just the rational Selmer ranks.

13.2.2     Integral local conditions and duality
Lemma 13.2 (Integral Kummer local condition). Let F be a finite extension of a nonar-
chimedean local field of characteristic zero, and let B/F be any elliptic curve. Put



                                                     98
MF = B(F )∧  limn B(F )/2n B(F ). There is a perfect local condition
          2 =← −
                              UF = MF [−1] −→ RΓ(F, T2 B).

For every n ≥ 1, derived reduction has

          H 0 (UF ⊗L Z/2n ) = B(F )[2n ],      H 1 (UF ⊗L Z/2n ) = B(F )/2n B(F ).

Its map in degree zero is the isomorphism onto H 0 (F, B[2n ]), and its map in degree one is
the finite Kummer injection. The condition is its own exact orthogonal complement under
local Tate duality, for every reduction type and with no hypothesis on B[2].

Proof. The local analytic description of B(F ) shows that MF is a finitely generated Z2 -
module. Its torsion is the finite group B(F )[2∞ ]. Consequently MF has a free resolution
of length at most one. Also H 0 (F, T2 B) = 0, since B(F )[2∞ ] has bounded exponent. The
integral Kummer injection into H 1 (F, T2 B) therefore defines the displayed morphism using
the truncation in degrees at most one of RΓ(F, T2 B).
    Tensor the free resolution of MF with Z/2n . Its two cohomology groups are MF [2n ]
and MF /2n MF . The inverse limits of the ordinary Kummer sequences identify their maps
as asserted.
    For orthogonality, compose cup product and the Weil pairing with the local invariant
map. A morphism
                              MF [−1] ⊗L MF [−1] −→ Z2 [−2]
is determined by its homomorphism MF ⊗ MF → Z2 : the possible Tor1 is in degree one
and gives no extra degree-zero morphism to this target. The indicated homomorphism
vanishes, because the finite Kummer image annihilates itself at every level by local Tate
duality. Choose a nullhomotopy in free models. The induced morphism

                        RΓ(F, T2 B)/UF −→ R HomZ2 (UF , Z2 )[−2]

is a quasi-isomorphism. This can be checked after reduction modulo 2: degree one is the
perfect pairing of the finite Kummer quotient with the Kummer image, and degree two
is dual to B(F )[2]. Its perfect cone is therefore zero by derived Nakayama. This proves
exact orthogonality and also its compatibility with derived reduction. This is precisely the
finite-module duality input of [52, Proposition 5.2.4].

   Where the scalar deformation is trivial, extend this local condition by derived scalar
extension to Ai or Ai [G]. At an unramified good prime use the inflated residue-field
complex
                                   Frv −1
                         Uv = [ M −−−  −→ M ] in degrees 0, 1.
Its quotient in full local cochains is the singular local complex. The full local condition is
the identity on local cochains, and its orthogonal strict condition is zero. At a derivative
prime the conditions used later are the following specified truncations instead. In its split
local model write
                             Lv = D0 ⊕ (Fv ⊕ Sv )[−1] ⊕ D2 [−2],
where the differential is zero, D0 is dual to D2 , and the finite and transverse planes Fv , Sv
are each isotropic. Put

Ustr = D0 ,     Urel = D0 ⊕ (Fv ⊕ Sv )[−1],        UF = D0 ⊕ Fv [−1],      US = D0 ⊕ Sv [−1].

    For a finite permitted set S the Selmer complex is, by definition,

by the nontrivial automorphism of K/Q, conjugate transport followed by the Weil pairing
identifies the two representations over the same scalar ring. All maps and nullhomotopies
in this paragraph are finite diagrams to which Lemma 13.1 applies.

13.3    The moving conductor character
The conductor character must preserve the local conditions at 2N while making the new
local terms invertible after reduction modulo 2 and inversion of t. The following proposition
supplies those local values. Its separate bounds on Tate Frobenius control the contribution
of the moving prime when we later specialize to t = 0.

Proposition 13.3 (Ring-class characters with prescribed local values). Fix finitely many
split primes of K above odd rational primes, outside 2N , and orient one prime in each
conjugate pair. There are distinct split rational primes ri avoiding all the fixed data and
characters
                      ξi : Gal(Hri /K) −→ Z/2ni Z,       ni −→ ∞,
such that ξi is surjective on the inertia quotient at ri , is zero at the primes above 2N , and
has odd value at each prescribed oriented prime. We can require ri → 1 in Z2 and

               v2 (2 + ari (E)),   v2 (2 − ari (E)),   v2 det(1 ± Frri | T ) ≤ cE .      (13.3)

The constant cE is independent of the list and of K.

Proof. We give the compatibility argument before imposing the Tate condition. Choose
prime ideals p1 , . . . , ps with pairwise disjoint conjugate pairs, including the oriented pre-
scribed primes and the primes above 2N , and enough additional split prime ideals to
generate Cl(K). Such additional ideals may avoid any fixed finite set. Put weight wj = 1
at a prescribed odd prime and wj = 0 at every additional prime and every prime above
2N . The conjugate weight is −wj . Let

    At a rational prime r split in a sufficiently large finite cyclotomic subfield, the power-

                      r is surjective onto µ2n . The split ring-class exact sequence is

             1 −→ (OK /rOK )× /(Z/rZ)× −→ Pic(Z + rOK ) −→ Cl(K) −→ 1.
The excluded extra-unit fields are the only source of a unit correction. The first group is F× r ,
with a principal relation measured by β/β at the oriented prime over r. Consequently (13.4)
says exactly that its primitive power-residue character extends to the class group with the
specified values wj . More explicitly, the inertia group and the chosen ideal classes present
the ring-class group, and the basis of R lists its additional relations. We have imposed those
relations on the inertia character itself; we have not divided by a class-group order to extend
a previously fixed character. Thus this construction introduces no denominator, even when
the class number has a large 2-part. The class group and the number of prescribed primes
change the finite Chebotarev extension, but not the integrality of the character. Since the
ring-class extension is unramified at 2N , value zero on Frobenius means that the resulting
local character is trivial there. Conjugation acts by inversion.
    We now show that imposing these finitely many radical conditions cannot force either
forbidden Tate trace. In the non-CM case the image of GK(µ2∞ ) is an open subgroup of
SL2 (Z2 ), by the fixed-prime open-image theorem and the cyclotomic determinant identity
[62, Chapter IV, §2.2, theorem, p. IV-11, and Chapter I, §1.2, Example 2, p. I-4]: the image
is the intersection of the open image of GK with the determinant-one subgroup. Its index in
the corresponding fixed-E image changes by at most two when K changes. Its commutator
subgroup contains a fixed open subgroup UE of SL2 (Z2 ). One may see uniformity by
starting with a principal congruence subgroup in the fixed image: every index-two subgroup
contains its squares, and the commutators of the resulting fixed congruence subgroup are
open. Because the radical extension over F∞ is abelian, these commutators act trivially on
it. Hence every permitted Tate coset contains a translate of UE .
    For completeness, in the CM case let M be the CM field. The image over KM (µ2∞ )
contains a fixed open subgroup of the norm-one Cartan; the index changes by a bounded
factor, as [KM : Q] ≤ 4. Choose γ ∈ GKM with χcyc (γ) = 54 = 625. This is possible
uniformly: the cyclotomic image of GKM has index at most four in Z×        2 , so contains fourth
powers. On the radical Galois group, conjugation by γ is raising to 625, because all the
radicands lie in K. On the Tate image over KM , conjugation is trivial. Therefore
                               γuγ −1 u−625 ,             u ∈ GKM (µ2∞ ) ,

                                                 101
fixes every prescribed radical and acts on T as ρT (u)−624 . Raising to the fixed nonzero
power 624 carries an open subgroup of the one-dimensional norm-one Cartan onto an
open subgroup. This proves the required uniform kernel statement also in the CM case.
The open Cartan image used here is the CM part of the usual ℓ-adic image theorem [62,
Chapter IV, §2.2, Remark, p. IV-13].
    On a coset of an open subgroup of SL2 , trace is not identically 2 or −2. The same
holds on a coset of an open norm-one Cartan subgroup; on the other Cartan-normalizer
component trace is zero. The relevant determinant-one sets are compact and have finitely
many cosets modulo the fixed open subgroup. Choose in each coset an element whose
trace is neither forbidden value. The largest of the finitely many resulting valuations is a
constant depending only on E.
    Finally restrict the prescribed radical automorphism and such a Tate lift to a finite
Galois extension, including as many roots of unity as required. Chebotarev gives infinitely
many rational primes with this Frobenius class; choose the place over the prime to realize
the chosen representative. They split in K and have arbitrarily high prescribed congruence
to 1. Since det(1 ± Frr ) = 1 ± ar + r, a congruence precision larger than the fixed trace
bound gives all of (13.3). All these conditions concern a finite extension at each stage.

    Choose ni large enough that 1 + t has order dividing 2ni in A×  i , and put Ψi = (1 + t) .
                                                                                              ξi

For example ni ≥ 2i is sufficient. Orient the inertia generator to act by 1 + t; the prescribed
split Frobenius exponents remain odd in the limit. Use global cochains unramified outside
2N , ri , the active conductor primes and the derivative primes already retained. At 2N use
the Kummer conditions above and elsewhere outside the conductors use the unramified
condition. Reduction modulo t, with the unramified condition restored at ri , gives the
ordinary fixed-field complex; retain its comparison map at every finite stage.
Lemma 13.4 (Acyclic relaxed local complexes). For a primitive character constituent,
let C denote the limiting Selmer complex with full conditions at r and at its active good
conductor primes, and the prescribed Kummer, unramified or derivative conditions elsewhere.
The limiting full local complexes are acyclic over every height-one residue field of Λ of
characteristic zero. At (2), with residue field Ω = F2 ((t)), the r-complex is acyclic, as
are the complexes at active split primes with odd prescribed Frobenius exponent of the
deformation character and at active primes q with tq = 0. At each of these acyclic relaxed
places, the full and orthogonal strict conditions agree on the indicated test. If every relaxed
place is acyclic at that test and the remaining local conditions are self-dual, global duality
makes the Selmer complex self-dual. The Selmer complex has no global invariants on these
tests.
Proof. Use tame inertia and unramified Frobenius, or the Hochschild–Serre complex for
these two procyclic directions. At r, inertia minus one is multiplication by t times a unit.
It is invertible on all the indicated fields except the characteristic-zero fiber t = 0. There,
r → 1, and the two remaining Frobenius differentials have nonzero determinant by (13.3),
including either sign of the quadratic twist. The bound also ensures that the integral local
torsion at this central fiber has bounded length.
    At an active odd conductor prime, inertia of the quadratic character acts by −1.
Its differential is thus −2 and is invertible in characteristic zero. In characteristic two
the quadratic character disappears. At a split prime the Frobenius action is zB, where
B ∈ GL2 (F2 ) and z = (1 + t)a has odd a ∈ Z2 . Its coefficient of t is one, so z is nonconstant.
No nonzero polynomial over the finite constant field vanishes at z. Thus both Frobenius
complexes, with operators zB − 1 and q −1 zB − 1, are invertible over Ω; here q = 1 in the
residue field. No irreducibility assumption is used.

                                              102
    If tq = 0, the rational residual Frobenius B has order three. At a split place the
operator is again zB − 1, invertible whether z is nonconstant or z = 1. At an inert place
the ring-class Frobenius is trivial, since the ideal (q) is principal, and the Tate Frobenius
is B 2 , which also has no eigenvalue one. These observations give the additional tq = 0
assertion.
    The orthogonal full and strict conditions agree at each place where full local cochains
are acyclic. When this holds at every relaxed place and the remaining local conditions are
self-dual, Equation (13.2) gives global self-duality. Global invariants in characteristic two
are excluded by the nontrivial r-inertia scalar. In characteristic zero the same argument
works unless the scalar is trivial; in that case T ⊗ Q2 over GK is absolutely irreducible.
For non-CM curves this follows from the open image, and for CM curves from the open
Cartan and its nontrivial normalizer component, since K is not the CM field. Lemma 13.7
below realizes these actions on the limiting complex as well. On the self-dual tests, duality
pairs degrees one and two.

13.4    The Heegner determinant and its central value
The point index is not itself the quantity transferred across a growing family. Each new
conductor prime changes the local Selmer terms as well as the point. We combine these
changes in a determinant coordinate. Its central valuation will be twice the point index,
less the finite Selmer length and the conductor contribution. This formula reduces the
index estimates to lower bounds for the determinant coordinate.
     Retain a primitive genus or ring instance from Section 3, with point Pc (e) over K. Use
the conductor characters just constructed, taking ni ≥ 2i, and the representations T χe Ψi .
Retain the same auxiliary primes and the same fixed inner ultrafilter, now denoted Uin .
     The Selmer complex C is a coefficientwise limit of the corresponding finite free cochain
models along Uin . It has the integral Kummer condition at 2N , the full condition at ri
and the active good conductor primes, and the unramified condition at all other permitted
finite places. Here the full condition means that no restriction is imposed on local cochains.
Section 13.2 and Lemma 13.4 constructed the local models, their duality maps, and the
comparison at t = 0. We use these concrete complexes and maps; cohomologically equivalent
complexes without the comparison maps would not suffice for the later prime choices. Over
Frac(Λ), the modified full local complexes are acyclic, so the full conditions agree with
their orthogonal strict conditions. Global duality, followed by conjugate transport and the
Weil pairing, therefore gives a perfect pairing

                  H 1 (C ⊗Λ Frac(Λ)) × H 2 (C ⊗Λ Frac(Λ)) −→ Frac(Λ).

Here conjugation in K/Q inverts the ring-class character. These are the pairing and
identification supplied by Equation (13.2) and Lemma 13.4.
    Fix the single integer multiplier L of Lemma 13.12. It depends on the curve, parametriza-
tion and fixed local types, and clears descent and local Kummer ambiguities for the complete
conductor. In particular its valuation does not grow with the number of primes. The
following construction and formulas include that multiplier throughout.
    At each finite stage transfer the Kummer class of ycri from Hcri to K with the specified
scalar coefficients, using Lycri . Complete the finite coset sum before contracting its target
cocycle and local Kummer lifts into the models over K. The resulting cycles Pi , with their
contraction witnesses, give P ∈ H 1 (C) by Lemma 13.1. Local Kummer conditions at 2N
are preserved because Ψi is trivial there. At the center of the ith finite model, the norm



                                             103
relation has the factor
                           bi = ari − χe (Frri ) − χe (Frr̄i ),       ri = ri r̄i ,
                                                                                         (13.5)
                      Pi t=0 = εi bi κ(LPc (e)).

where κ denotes the Kummer class in the central comparison and εi is a unit; the equality
is taken modulo the finite coefficient precision. Since χe is defined over Q, its values at the
conjugate Frobenius elements agree; explicitly, bi = ari − 2χe (ri ). To retain the central
identity at the cochain level, let Bi be the reduction of a fixed finite model of the ordinary
Kummer complex over K, and let βi : Bi → Ci /(t) be its comparison map to the relaxed
central complex. Choose a cycle qi representing κ(LPc (e)). The finite norm relation gives
a boundary witness vi in the target model such that

                                  Pi mod t − εi bi βi (qi ) = dvi .

Retain βi , qi , vi , and the target contraction homotopies on the same stages and at the
same coefficient precision as Pi . Retain these units in the same ultrafilter Uin as all the
comparison maps. Then b = limUin bi has bounded valuation by Equation (13.3), and
ε = limUin εi is a unit. Thus P |t=0 = εbκ(LPc (e)), with b ̸= 0. The special complex
differs from the ordinary Kummer complex only by the active-prime relaxed quotients
and the limit of the singular quotient at the single moving prime ri , the latter of bounded
length. Inflation from the complexes omitting ri gives this comparison. All modified local
complexes are rationally acyclic at the center, so pairings agree there as well.
    If the product has a simple zero, its rational special fiber has one-dimensional cohomology
in degrees one and two, and P specializes nontrivially by Gross–Zagier. The generic
dimensions are at most these special dimensions. Moreover, the long exact sequence for
multiplication by t shows that H 1 (C[1/2])[t] = 0, since the special H 0 vanishes. A torsion
class that disappeared over the generic field would be killed by a power of t after localization
at (t); hence the nonzero special class cannot disappear. Thus the generic ranks are one in
degrees one and two, P spans H 1 , and the rational rank is constant at t = 0. If only base
Selmer corank one is known, the same dimension bound gives generic ranks at most one; a
zero generic point is allowed.

Definition 13.5. Choose an integral basis of det−1 Λ C. In generic rank one, the element
                  1                              2
formed by P in H and the functional ⟨P, ·⟩ on H determines a scalar u(C, P ) ∈ Frac(Λ) in
that basis. Put u = 0 if P = 0 or the generic rank is zero. Integral changes of determinant
basis multiply u by a unit.

    In the simple-zero case, constancy of the rational cohomology rank at t = 0 makes
u regular in the local ring Λ(t) . Its value u(0) ∈ Q2 is therefore already defined. This
specialization takes place at (t); the stronger assertion u ∈ Λ[1/2] will follow from the
divisor estimates below.
    The determinant has a simple integral interpretation. On a rank-one free Selmer line,
the point and its paired functional each contribute the point’s index. Torsion in degree two
subtracts its length from the coordinate in the inverse determinant lattice. For example,
                  2a
the complex [Z2 −→ Z2 ] in degrees one and two has inverse determinant lattice mapping to
2a Z2 in the rational determinant. The coordinate of a fixed rational tensor therefore loses
a in valuation. The next lemma carries out this calculation with the actual local Kummer
conditions. It also compares the Selmer length over K with those of the two rational twists.

            v(u(0)) = 2j(Pc (e)) − length Sha(E (e) /K)[2∞ ] −

   length Sha(E (e) /K)[2∞ ] +

                                            tv = s(e) + s(ek) +         tq + 2          tq + OE (1).   (13.7)
                                 q|c v|q                          q|k             q|c

The constants include the one fixed class multiplier and the bounded local contribution at
the moving prime ri .

Proof. The ordinary integral Kummer complex over K is self-dual. Its H 1 is the compact
Selmer module, and the torsion in H 2 has the length of the finite quotient of the discrete
Selmer group by its divisible subgroup. This follows by derived base change to Q2 /Z2 : the
local complexes give exactly the discrete Kummer conditions. In the present simple-zero
case that finite quotient is Sha(E (e) /K)[2∞ ]. Duality pairs the free parts in degrees one
and two perfectly. Torsion in degrees one and three costs a bounded amount, by global
torsion and duality. The uniform torsion bound here comes from a fixed nonidentity Tate
scalar acting trivially on every ring-class field in the construction: its scalar minus one
annihilates that torsion. Lemma 13.8 constructs this scalar, and the descent argument of
Lemma 13.12 uses the same bound.
    At an active odd conductor place inertia acts by minus identity. All integral H 1 is
Kummer, and the relaxed quotient contributes only H 2 (Kv , T e ) in degree two. Its length
is tv , by local duality and the fixed 2-torsion. At the moving prime ri the contribution
is bounded by Equation (13.3). The generic pairings specialize to the ordinary Kummer
pairing because the modified local complexes are rationally acyclic at the center. Thus the
free determinant tensor has valuation twice the point index, whereas torsion in degree two
contributes negative length to the inverse determinant. Together with Equation (13.5),
this proves Equation (13.6). The specialization is at constant rational cohomology rank, so
no specialization torsion is being substituted for the point.
    For Equation (13.7), apply the isogeny invariance of the BSD quotient to quadratic
Weil restriction and E (e) × E (ek) [72, Theorem 2.1 and the preceding discussion]. All
the groups in this comparison are finite apart from the known rank-one lattices; no BSD
equality is assumed. The forward and reverse isogenies have composites multiplication by
2. Consequently the rank-one lattice indices, height ratios, torsion ratios and period ratios
contribute bounded 2-powers. For periods, the pullback indices of Néron differentials at 2
are bounded by those composites; real kernels and component ratios are bounded as well.
    The remaining terms are the Tamagawa numbers. A ramified quadratic twist of good
reduction at an odd prime q has type I0∗ and component number one plus the number of
roots of its reduced auxiliary cubic [1, Theorem 4.1, Table 4 and proof, Case 3]. To identify
this number, write a good integral model as y 2 = f (x), retaining the quadratic term, and
the twisting parameter as d = πu. The auxiliary cubic is ū3 f¯(T /ū); it is separable and its
roots correspond to the nonzero rational two-torsion points. Thus the component number
is #E[2]Frq and its 2-valuation is tq . This argument also applies in residue characteristic
3. Its connected reduction is unipotent. Some inertia element acts by −1 on the twisted
2-adic representation, so its local 2-primary torsion is precisely its Frobenius-fixed 2-torsion.
Over an unramified extension the same formula holds with the Frobenius of the enlarged
residue field. At unramified or split places, Weil-restriction component numbers are the

                                                  105
products over v | q. At a prime q | k outside 2N , the curve over K has good reduction and
its Weil restriction has connected special fiber. Indeed, Weil restriction of its Néron model
is the Néron model of the restriction of the curve [6, Section 7.6, Proposition 6]. Its special
fiber has the original connected elliptic fiber as quotient, with connected vector-group
layers from ramification. Exactly one rational twist has the ramified component factor.
At a conductor prime q | c, the two rational twists give a combined valuation 2tq , and the
                                         P
corresponding contribution over K is v|q tv . All places above 2N have bounded local
contributions. These are precisely the terms of Equation (13.7).

   In a simple-zero genus instance, the fixed partner absorbs the terms involving h∗ , while
c = 1 and k = hh∗ . The displayed formulas therefore identify
                       v(u(0)) = 2j(P1 (h∗ )) − 2w(h) − s(h) + OE,h∗ (1).
In a simple-zero ring instance with ω(k) ≤ a, the discriminant term is bounded and the
conductor term is 4w(h), giving
                    v(u(0)) = 2j(P|h| (h)) − 4w(h) − s(h) − s(hk) + OE,a (1).
The ring identity also applies to the interpolation families in which an added nonsplit
prime has tq = 0; such a prime contributes no weight. The all-split hypothesis is needed for
the uniform ring lower bound. These formulas explain both the lower-bound statements
and the normalization in Heegner interpolation. To prove the lower bounds, we must
show that u has no negative divisor away from (2) and has a uniformly bounded negative
divisor at (2) in the stated cases. We will construct integral local switches that preserve its
determinant valuation, and use inner detection to choose switches lowering excess Selmer
rank. The uniform ring estimate will require an additional characteristic-zero limit; the
universal binary family will instead require a bounded clearing factor after the new local
blocks have been removed.
    The main intermediate estimate is

    v(2) (u) ≥ −OE,h∗ (1) in genus data with irreducible E[2],                          (13.8)

To construct the switches used in these estimates, we first retain enough Galois evaluation
data to select their derivative primes.

13.5     Evaluation and the exact kernel
The determinant estimates require switches that detect the excess Selmer dimension at a
height-one test. To choose switching primes, we must still detect a nonzero limiting Selmer
class by Galois evaluation. The next two lemmas first retain that detection under coefficient
specialization, then restrict it to sequences acting trivially on the full Tate module, all
ring-class fields, and the quadratic characters in use.
Lemma 13.7 (Evaluation on arbitrary Galois sequences). Fix K and a sequence of the
finite cochain constructions above. Keep the contraction maps into the original global
cochains. Let ΓK = i GK and Γ = ΓK ⋊ ⟨τ ⟩ ⊂ i GQ , with simultaneous complex
                       Q                              Q

conjugation τ . Thus ΓK has index two in Γ; the larger unrestricted product of rational
Galois groups is not used for induction. The matrix limit defines a coefficient action of ΓK
on Λ2 . For every field L receiving a map from Λ, there is a natural injection
                                H 1 (C ⊗Λ L) −→ Habs
                                                 1
                                                     (ΓK , L2 ),

                                               106
where the right side denotes abstract crossed-homomorphism classes. Here C may be the
global complex or a Selmer complex whose local conditions contain all local degree-zero
invariants and inject into local H 1 . The same statement holds for the induced representation
of Γ.
    The evaluation maps and their degree-two cochain identities exist for every group
sequence and commute with coefficient extension.

Proof. Let Ci be a finite model and let ιi be its chosen map to the original global cochains,
using the projection from the Selmer cone when appropriate. For every g = (gi ) and
h = (hi ) define matrices

                     e0i : Ci0 → A2i ,   e1g,i : Ci1 → A2i ,    e2g,h,i : Ci2 → A2i

by evaluating ιi in degrees zero, one, and two. Their source ranks are fixed and their
entries belong to Ai . Thus each has a coefficientwise ultrafilter limit, for every choice of
g, h. The same fixed ultrafilter defines these limits for all g, h. Likewise ρ(g) = limi ρi (gi )
is a representation. The original cochain identities imply

                                     e1g d = (ρ(g) − 1)e0 ,                               (13.9)
                                   e2g,h d = ρ(g)e1h − e1gh + e1g .                      (13.10)

All identities pass to the limit because their matrix expressions have finitely many terms. A
cycle x ∈ C 1 ⊗ L consequently gives the crossed homomorphism g 7→ e1g (x), and a boundary
gives a coboundary.
    We prove injectivity in a way that also controls base change. At each finite stage let
k = F2 be the residual field. Choose a space of representatives for residual H 1 . Evaluate
pairs (c, m), where c is in this space and m ∈ k 2 , by bc (g) − (ρ(g) − 1)m. The intersection
of the kernels of all these evaluations consists precisely of pairs (0, m) with m an invariant.
Since the vector space is finite dimensional, a list of at most dim H 1 + 2 elements already
has this intersection. For these elements evaluation is a chain map
                                                   ((ρi (gj )−1))j
                             Ci −→ Di = [ A2i −−−−−−−−→ (A2i )d ]

in degrees zero and one, inducing an isomorphism on residual H 0 and an injection on
residual H 1 . For Selmer complexes this uses the exact sequence of (13.1): the indicated
local degree-zero isomorphisms give H 0 (Ci ) = Hglob
                                                    0   and H 1 (Ci ) ,→ Hglob
                                                                          1    residually.
     Retain these matrices and their chosen group-element sequences. The residual global
and local complexes, and the local conditions, have no negative cohomology. The cone
definition therefore gives H n (C ⊗ k) = 0 for n < 0. Put Q = Cone(C → D). The long exact
sequence gives H n (Q⊗k) = 0 for n < −1; its groups in degrees −1 and 0 vanish because the
detecting map is an isomorphism on H 0 and injective on H 1 . Any H 3 (C ⊗ k) contributes
in degree two of this cone, so causes no negative-degree obstruction. Successively cancel
the unit differential blocks of Q. This gives a homotopy-equivalent bounded free complex
starting in degree one. For any Λ-algebra L, its ordinary tensor product computes Q ⊗L     ΛL
and still starts in degree one; no flatness is needed. Thus the long exact sequence makes
H 1 (C ⊗L) → H 1 (D ⊗L) injective. A crossed cocycle that is a coboundary on all of ΓK is in
particular a coboundary on the detecting list, proving the desired injection. For induction
the same proof has coefficient rank four and the detecting bound is dim H 1 + 4.

Lemma 13.8 (The exact common kernel). In the notation of Lemma 13.7, let H be the
group of sequences each of whose entries acts trivially on the full Tate module, on all

                                                 107
ring-class fields of K, and on the quadratic characters in use. Restriction from the crossed
classes in that Lemma to H is injective at every characteristic-zero test. It is also injective
at the residual (2) test. The characteristic-zero annihilator needed for this assertion can be
chosen with a fixed finite 2-valuation depending only on E, also as K varies.
Proof. The Tate image contains an open subgroup of scalar homotheties, both in the CM
and non-CM cases. Choose once and for all g ∈ GQ with Tate action u 1, where u ∈ 1 + 4Z2
and u ̸= 1. Its square belongs to every quadratic subgroup. Put

                                         z = g 2 τ g 2 τ −1 .

The generalized dihedral property of every ring-class extension implies that z is ring-class
trivial. It is also trivial on every quadratic character, and its Tate action is the fixed scalar
u4 . For every sequence in ΓK , its commutator with the constant sequence z is in the exact
group H, since its Tate commutator and all its abelian character images are trivial. Thus
z is central in ΓK /H and acts on the coefficients by u4 .
     Here the elementary central-scalar argument suffices. If a crossed cocycle b is defined
on a group with such a central element, comparison of b(zg) and b(gz) gives

                                  (u4 − 1)b(g) = (g − 1)b(z).

In characteristic zero this makes b a coboundary. Inflation–restriction then gives injectivity
on H. The valuation of u4 − 1 is fixed.
    In characteristic two choose, at each stage, inertia at ri mapping onto the chosen
generator of ξi . Its Tate action is trivial and its ring-class images commute, so the resulting
sequence is central in ΓK /H and acts on M by 1 + t. Its scalar minus one is invertible in
F2 ((t)).
    For the induced representation W , first restrict to ΓK . There W = M ⊕ M τ , and this
inertia sequence acts on the summands by 1 + t and (1 + t)−1 . Hence W ΓK = 0. Inflation–
restriction for the index-two inclusion gives an injection H 1 (Γ, W ) → H 1 (ΓK , W ), even in
characteristic two. Apply the central-scalar calculation separately to the two summands to
obtain injection on restriction to H. The same argument works in characteristic zero with
the fixed scalar u4 . Throughout, H is defined by exact actions at every stage.

Lemma 13.9 (Irreducibility on the characteristic-zero tests). On an inner characteristic-
zero test where Ψ2 ̸= 1, the four-dimensional representation induced from T χΨ is absolutely
irreducible. If Ψ2 = 1 on an inner test, it splits as two inequivalent absolutely irreducible
quadratic twists.
Proof. The two induced summands are distinguished by r-inertia, whose scalars are Ψ and
Ψ−1 . On either summand, multiplying the Tate matrices by scalars does not alter invariant
subspaces. The Tate representation restricted to GK is absolutely irreducible under the
exclusions already specified. These facts give absolute irreducibility of the induction when
the two inertia characters differ.
    If Ψ2 = 1, extension across conjugation gives the two quadratic twists. They are
inequivalent, since a quadratic self-twist of the Tate representation here would make its
restriction to GK reducible over an algebraic closure. In the CM case this would force K
to be the CM field, which was excluded.

Lemma 13.10 (Simultaneous finite Chebotarev realization). For any element u of an
exact kernel in Lemma 13.8, derivative primes can be chosen, at increasing finite precision,
whose rational Frobenius is τ u on all retained data. Their finite localization is evaluation on
(τ u)2 . The assertion is compatible with any fixed finite list of previous derivative operations.

                                                108
Proof. At one Artin stage, a coordinate of a cochain in the image of a finite model is a
locally constant function. The finitely many coordinates being used factor through one
finite quotient of the absolute Galois group. Enlarge it to contain the Tate action at the
required precision, the finitely many ring-class fields and quadratic characters currently
in use, and all old finite data. Chebotarev realizes the conjugacy class of τ u on this
finite quotient, with a chosen place realizing the desired representative [48, Chapter V,
Theorem 3.23]. It gives a rational prime inert in K, outside the old finite set. Because
u is exactly Tate-trivial, its Frobenius tends to ρT (τ ); in particular aℓ → 0 and ℓ → −1.
Because u is exactly ring-class trivial, the square Frobenius on the K-prime is the identity
on every retained ring-class field. The unramified local coordinate is therefore the stated
Frobenius-square evaluation.
    Repeat at each finite stage with increasing precision. All conditions remain finite, and
no density bound or prime-size bound is required. Previously imposed finite diagrams are
unchanged.

   In induced-module notation, restriction to the exact kernel is additive. For a crossed
cocycle b and an exact-kernel element u,

                                   b((τ u)2 ) = (1 + τ )b(u).

Indeed τ 2 = 1, u acts trivially on the coefficients, and the cocycle identity cancels the two
occurrences of b(τ ). Projection to either induced summand identifies im(1 + τ ) with a
two-dimensional coefficient module, also in characteristic two.

13.6    The local switch and its integral determinant
Fix a complex conjugation τ and write Cτ for its action on T . The next two Lemmas
construct the integral local switches and the classes that pass through them. Their
finite-level formulas are needed before applying the limit constructions above.

Lemma 13.11 (The split local model). Let a sequence of inert good primes ℓ have rational
Frobenius lifts tending on T to a fixed complex conjugation Cτ . At the unique prime of K
above ℓ, the limiting local complex has

                   H 0 = M,       H 1 = F ⊕ S = M ⊕ M,                 H 2 = M,       (13.11)

with zero differentials in a free split model. Here M = T ⊗ Λ with the relevant scalar
coefficient structure. The two degree-one coordinates of a cocycle are

                               f (c) = c(γ 2 ),         s(c) = c(σ),                  (13.12)

where σ is an oriented pro-2 tame generator. The plane conditions F = {s = 0} and
S = {f = 0} include H 0 and exclude H 2 . Each is its own orthogonal complement for
conjugate-Weil duality. The strict condition H 0 and the relaxed condition H 0 ⊕ H 1 [−1] are
mutually dual. All four inclusions can be equipped with compatible integral duality triangles.

Proof. The prime-to-ℓ local cochains are computed by the tame inertia and residue-field
directions. In these two directions the differentials are formed from inertia minus one and
Frobenius minus one, with the usual Frobenius action on tame inertia. On M , inertia
is trivial at the derivative prime. The square of a rational Frobenius is trivial on every
ring-class character. In the limit it is also the identity on T , while ℓ2 tends to one. Thus
both differentials become zero, giving (13.11).


                                                  109
     Here is a finite-precision check of the pairing, needed at 2. At a retained quotient
killed by 2a , impose also q = ℓ2 ≡ 1 (mod 2a+1 ), as well as trivial Frobenius-square action
on the coefficient module at this quotient. The tame two-generator complex then has
zero differentials, with the coordinates in (13.12). Its degree-one scalar generators are the
unramified and tame characters. More explicitly, use the presentation ϕσϕ−1 σ −q = 1. After
augmentation with trivial coefficient action its Fox resolution has terms of ranks 1, 2, 1,
with d0 = 0 and d1 = (0, 1 − q), in the ordered finite and tame coordinates. The diagonal
from the same resolution has zero pure-finite term, unit mixed terms, and pure-tame
coefficient ± 2q . The latter coefficient is the augmented  second derivative of σ −q together
                                                       q
with its adjacent σ in the relation. Both 1 − q and 2 vanish modulo 2a under the imposed
congruence. Thus this is an actual split complex and an actual pure-plane zero pairing at
the retained precision. The field contains the 2a+1 -st roots of unity, so −1 is a 2a -th power.
The norm-residue identity (x, x) = (x, −1) for the 2a -Hilbert symbol therefore makes the
square of each scalar generator zero. The two generators form a basis; local Tate duality
makes their cross pairing a unit. The same calculation applies coefficientwise to the free
Artin coefficient module, including its nilpotent t and quadratic variables. Thus the pure
plane restrictions are already zero at every retained quotient. All these congruences can
be imposed simultaneously with the derivative Frobenius conditions by increasing their
precision. Taking the compatible limit proves integral vanishing of the pure squares. It is
not deduced from skew symmetry in characteristic two.
     Equivalently, in the limiting tame presentation ϕσϕ−1 = σ q with q → 1, the rank-
(1, 2, 1) resolution specializes to the exterior resolution for two procyclic directions. The
finite Hilbert-symbol calculation specifies the cup product on its split cohomology and rules
out a hidden square term. Conjugation transports the finite Tate coordinate by Cτ and
the singular coordinate by −Cτ , with possible scalar units coming from the orientations.
Consequently the cross pairing is
                      β(x, y) = ϵ ⟨x, Cτ y⟩,    ϵ ∈ Λ× ,     x ∈ F, y ∈ S,              (13.13)
where ⟨ , ⟩ is the perfect alternating Weil form. This proves the assertions about orthogonal
complements. Since the restrictions of this explicit pairing to either pure plane are zero as
maps of the split complexes, their isotropy homotopies may be chosen to be zero. Only a
product of two degree-one terms could map to the degree-two local invariant target; the
degree-zero terms introduce no additional restriction. These zero homotopies agree on the
strict subcomplex. These are the actual orthogonality data required in the Selmer duality
construction [52, Theorem 6.3.4]; the compatibility assertion is therefore an assertion about
these displayed complexes and maps. Lemma 13.1 is used to retain this finite diagram
together with the unchanged local data.
    For a finite set I of derivative primes, let CI be obtained from C by replacing the finite
plane condition F by S at each prime of I, retaining all other local conditions. The classes
below use the same multiplier L already included in P ; in particular, P∅ = P .
Lemma 13.12 (Descent and the switch at 2). Suppose that the finite ring-class transfers
are performed before retaining their target cycles and witnesses as in Lemma 13.1. There
is one integer L ̸= 0, depending on the fixed curve, parametrization and fixed local types,
such that the classes obtained from the Heegner derivatives of L times the point satisfy, for
every finite switched set I,
                PI ∈ H 1 (CI ),     f (PI∪{ℓ} ) = 0,       s(PI∪{ℓ} ) = Cτ f (PI ).     (13.14)
The same L works for every number of active and derivative primes. The previously imposed
singular conditions are retained.

                                               110
Proof. We first descend the derivative classes using one multiplier for the complete conduc-
tor, and verify their Kummer conditions at 2N . We then compute both local coordinates
in Equation (13.14). The last step retains these classes and identities in the same bounded
target cochain diagrams used for evaluation.
    Choose the orientations coherently. The relative ring-class group at an inert prime ℓ is
cyclic of order ℓ + 1. For distinct new primes these groups form a product: the ring-class
exact sequence has kernel the product of the local residue-unit quotients, and the only
global units here are 1, −1, whose images in these quotients are trivial. Set

                     Tr(ycrn ) = aℓ ycrn/ℓ ,  yecrn = Frℓ yecrn/ℓ .             (13.16)
These are the point relations, not an application of a residual irreducibility theorem. A
fixed multiple of the parametrization removes cuspidal terms.
    There is a uniform bound on the 2-primary torsion over the union of the ring-class
fields in use. To see this, choose a fixed nonidentity scalar homothety of infinite order in
the 2-adic Tate image. Square a lift so that it belongs to every quadratic subgroup under
consideration. Multiply it by its conjugate under complex conjugation. Its action on each
ring-class field is trivial, because conjugation inverts the abelian ring-class action, while its
action on T is still a fixed nonidentity scalar. If 2e annihilates its scalar minus one up to a
unit, then 2e kills the torsion in question. The existence of this fixed scalar, including the
CM case, is the Tate-image input used in Lemma 13.8.
    Work first with coefficients modulo 2a . Require aℓ and ℓ + 1 to vanish to a precision
substantially larger than a. Equations (13.15) and (13.16) then make the derivative
Kummer class invariant under the relative ring-class group. In the inflation–restriction
sequence, both the obstruction to descent and the ambiguity of descent are killed by 2e ,
since their coefficient module is killed by 2e . Multiplication by 22e therefore gives a descent
independent of this ambiguity: descend 2e times the class and multiply its lift by 2e . There
is no repetition of this factor for individual primes; descent is performed once from the
field with the complete conductor.
    The global descent has cost only the fixed factor 22e , regardless of the number of
derivative primes. It remains to ensure that the descended class satisfies the Kummer
condition at every fixed bad place; this is a separate local descent issue.
    At a place over 2N these descent extensions are unramified. Here is the local descent
argument, valid also for even extension degree and for toric or additive reduction. Let F ′ /F
be a finite unramified extension with group G, and write k ′ /k for its residue extension.
The successive formal-group quotients Ej (F ′ )/Ej+1 (F ′ ) are additive copies of k ′ . The
normal basis theorem makes each a cohomologically trivial G-module, without a restriction
on |G|. Every finite quotient E1 (F ′ )/Ej (F ′ ) is therefore cohomologically trivial. The
inverse systems of these finite groups and their invariant groups satisfy the Mittag–Leffler
condition. Taking inverse limits in finite G-cochains consequently gives H 1 (G, E1 (F ′ )) = 0.
Let E/OF be the Néron model and put E           e 0 = E 0 , a smooth geometrically connected

[45, Proposition 3, p. 559] gives H 1 (G, E e 0 (k ′ )) = 0. The smooth reduction sequence then

                                H 1 (G, E(F ′ )) ,−→ H 1 (G, Φ(k ′ )).

Existence of the smooth finite-type model and smooth lifting follow from Bosch et al.
[6, Section 1.3, Corollary 2, and Section 2.3, Proposition 5]. The right side is killed
by the exponent of Φ(k). By inflation–restriction, the left side is exactly the kernel of
H 1 (F, E) → H 1 (F ′ , E). Thus the same fixed exponent makes a class in H 1 (F, E[2a ])
Kummer whenever its restriction is Kummer. Néron models commute with unramified
base extension [6, Section 7.2, Theorem 1 and Corollary 2], so their geometric component
groups are unchanged. Only the finitely many fixed local twisting types at 2N occur, so
the product of these component exponents is bounded independently of the conductor
and of the extension degree. Include this product, the single descent factor 22e , and the
parametrization factor in L. Thus the descended class has the integral Kummer condition
at 2N . No good-ordinary or semistability condition was used. Transfer with the scalar
character preserves this condition because Ψ = 1 there.
    The single multiplier L now gives descent with the required Kummer conditions. We
next prove the finite-to-singular identity itself. Both coordinates matter: the singular
coordinate must become Cτ f , while the new finite coordinate must vanish at the retained
precision.
     Here is the local calculation, including the division by two. Apply the derivatives at all
primes other than ℓ, and write X, Y for the resulting lower and upper points. Put Fℓ = Frℓ .
The lower field splits at the K-prime (ℓ), since that ideal is principal. Reduction therefore
gives Fℓ2 X = X. The following Frobenius-polynomial identities are taken on the reduction;
good odd-prime reduction is injective on the 2-primary torsion coordinates being compared.
